POULTRY IN AUTUMN.

A Start Should be Made with Pullets

Not Old Birds.

A FAVOURABLE SEASON.

There are some people who seem firmly con- vinced that in poultry-keeping, says an expert "Farm and Home," a commencement Should be made during the spring month, but it has often been proved that if a start is made early in autumn excellent results can be achieved. However, rather more capital out- lay will be necessary, and, perhaps, the risk of disease is somewhat greater.. Those who begin in the spring and summer Months should cer- tainly make sure of getting a fair number of eggs almost immediately; but though they will have the moulting process to go through, this, at its best, is a monotonous business, and if some of the birds are sold off, most likely they will realise low prices. .

have often noticed that where testart"was made in autumn with genuine pullcts.hatched at the Proper•time, then a wonderful degree of success has been achieved. Those who pin ther faith to yearling hens simply because they can be bought at a low rate more often, than not suffer the penalty. In many caSes it is very difficult to distinguish yearling hens from those two years old. Two-year-old birds are of little use for utility purposes, but if a start is math With pullets—it does not matter whether they are pure or cross, providing they are old enough to lay—a fair number of eggs is sure to be the result during autumn and .rinter.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
BEST HATCHING MONTH.

March is undoubtedly the best month for hatching, and if the birds are of the heavy kind, they will give far better results than lighter varieties. During the last few years the price Of genuine pullets, both pure and first-cross, has risen considerably, and now pure-bred Pullets, Which appear as though they will com- mence laying in November, can seldom be pur- chased under six shillings per head. Farmers, and those who breed more than they intend to keep, sometimes have a sale in the autumn months, and really good bargains can be picked up, but the averhge poultry-breeder will not be able to buy a large quantity, and thus he must pay correspondingly higher prices.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
GET GOOD ACCOMMODATION.

It must not be supposed that cross-bred birds, that is if they are principally first-cross, are not as good layers as pure varieties, because during autumn and winter, unless the fowls are of a hardy nature, few eggs will be forthcoming, no matter what breed they are. I have frequently seen large, early-hatched, first-cross ptulets sold at farm sales as low as 7s. per couple. Sonw- times pure breeds will go at Bs. to 10s., but the demand for these is often very great, and priecs are certainly rising every year. The intending poultry farmer must obtain really good houses, that is, buildings Which are strongly invi3e, not thç fliinSy.kind often seed. These mist be well lighted, properly Ventilated, With the -roof 40%; erect With good felt; if Scratching- sheds a: attached, the number of winter eggs Will be considerably increased. The comfort of the birds must be studied, or inferior results are sure to occur.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
SUMMER HOUSING.

I do not think the plan which some poultry- keepers adopt of allowing their fowls to roost In the open air during warm weather is a 'Very good one. I have often tried the plan, but I have never found that it answered. quite satis- factorily. Colds and other complaints seem to be encouraged, and there is always great diffi- culty in persuading the birds to enter a house again when autumn arrives. A far better plan is to use an open-fronted house, which, while It keeps the birds dry, admits a plentiful supply of fresh air. Instead of having the front boarded in the ordinary manner, it is wire- netted, a canvas blind being fixed so that when It is wet it can be lowered, thus keeping the Interior quite dry. 'For several years I have used houses of this description, not only dur- ing the summer, but throughout file entire twelve months, and I have found that the hens living therein are more prolific and healthier than those living in the closed-in houses, while a larger percentage of their eggs proves .fertile, and the chickens hatched therefrom are hardier and easier to rear. An ordinary house can be Improved during warm weather, by leaving the door wide open all day and night, substituting one made of wire-netting.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
FEEDING TURKEY CHICKENS.

The chicks' first food should consist of hard- boiled egg, chopped fine and dried off with ground oat or crammings. This food may be given for the first four or five days, varying it with an occasional feed of biscuit-meal and a a last feed at night of a reliable dry chick food. After the first few days the eggs may be re- placed by fine pollard, scalded and dried off with ground oats, or boiled rice dried off with crammings, these foods to be continued for the first three weeks. The chicks must be fed regularly and sparingly every two hours, and although it may not be:- apparent just now. overfeeding is fatal, and special attention must also be paid to the regular supply of water, flint, grit and oyster shell. After three weeks the interval between the feeds may be increased to three hours; green fobd in the form of chopped onions or dandelions may be added to the soft food, which may be mixed with skimmed or new milk. Cracked wheat or groats may replace the dry chick food. Vary ] the foods as much as possible. but do not make ' any sudden changes. Move the coop and run on to fresh ground daily, and when space and weather will permit, allow tne hen to roam about with her chicks after the first week. The hen and chicks should-he periodically examined to see that they are all quite free from vermin, which are the cause of many fatalities.

FARM AND GARDEN.

LINSEED AS A PROFITABLE CROP.

Flax, the cultivation of which once formed an important feature, has practically disappeared from our fields. It was chiefly grown for the sake of the straW as the raw material for linen manufacture, and the seeds were quite a sec- ondary consideration. The shiftings of trade put an end to the demand for the straw, and the cultivation of flax became a thing of the past in England, though it still survives in Ire- land. The turning of Time's wheel, however, is not unlikely to bi•ing about a revival, and flax may once more be regarded with favour— not so much, this time, for the value of the straw as for the seed, for which there is a great and growing demand.—" Country Life."

===== 0002601_19130913_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
ESTIMATING YIELD CROPS.

A fairly accurate computation of the yield of any crop may be obtained by marking off a representative square of tfie crop, and weighing carefully the produce therefrom. . Eleven yards square—viz., 11 yards by 11 yards—is exactly one-fortieth part of an acre. The weight of crop obtained from this square, multiplied by forty, will give the approximate yield per acre.. Still more-accurate results will be obtained if three squares, each measuring 11 yards by 11 yards, are selected in different places, all typical of the crop as it stands. Having obtained the weight of these, add them all together and then divide the sum by the number of squares harvested, in this case three, this then gives the average weight of one square of 11 yards of this crop, and multiplied by forty will not come Very far from the exact yield of the acre, were you to weigh every portion of the crop obtained. In green crops, where green weights are to be obtained, weigh at once before the crop loses by evaporation, as if 'you' allow _some- portions to remain fdr hours • after cutting and Then weigh up othOrs at once, your weights will not be comparable.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
CLF. tN MILK IND CONSUMER'S RESPONSIBILITY.

Much, says a writer .in " hoard's Dairyman," has been said about the production of clean milk. The producer has ,been accused of plac- ing unclean milk on the market, of which offence. he may or may not be guilty. The dairyman is often responsible for the number of bacteria found in milk, as well as

for the bad odours that sometimes occur_ The neglect of little things while handling milk may increase the number of bacteria as well as the undesirable tastes which may develop before the product reaches the consumer. One of the causes of impure milk is the use of unclean utensils. I have seen milk produced on a cer- tain farm for the past three months, and have taken particular notice of the bacteria found in the milk by a bacteriologist who tested the milk daily. By slight precautions and the, use of the closed-top pails, which prevent th,e dust from getting Into the milk, the bacterial count has been reduced from 1,000,000 to 4,000 cubic centi- metres of milk. The record has been reached from a little extra care in cleaning the cows and utensils and in handling the milk. Now we may look at the other side of the question. . Does the consumer take proper care of the milk after it is received at the home? For instance, when the milkman arrives at the door the milk is often drawn from the tank into an open pitcher which has not been sterilised. The dust from the street readily comes in contact with the milk as it is being drawn.

When • the milk' is received in bottles and a portion q it is used, the remainder does not always receive proper care. The bottle is often left unproperly corked and is Set on a shelf in the open room, or in a refrigerator with other food. Milk absorbs odours very easily, .and when left in this Way may become unfit for use. For this condition of milk the p;•6rlticer is often blamed. Since the producer has eminined such gotsd resUlts' ftoin- the use of. closed-lop pails and careful- handling of: milk,- could- we not have still better results if the Consumer Would sharc. the responsibility of keeping milk clean?

===== 0002601_19130913_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

Large clumps of Pyrethrums may now be divided and replanted in a deep, rich soil in a sunny position. Larger blooms are thus obtained.

Chrysanthemum maximum is a popular gar- den flower, flourishing under the most adverse conditions. As a cut flower it is invaluable, and if the flowers are constantly cut a long supply is maintained. Propagate by means of division in the autumn or spring. Eryngiums, or, as they. are -more popularly known, Sea Hollies, are remarkable for their lasting qualities. The rigid growth and the difference in form of the flowers from any other subject render them additionally charming to a floral display. Seedling Wallflowers, Sweet Williams, Can- terbury Bells, and Foxgloves should be trans- planted to get good, sturdy plants for putting into their permanent quarters. Many spring bedding plants, such as Dais!es, Auriculas, Primroses, Polyanthuses, Arabis, Aubrietia, Saxifrages, and others, may now be divided. Break them up into pieces, with roots attached if possible, and plant in a partially shaded place in &ill that will remain moist. Large clumps are all the better for occasional divisions.

An easy way of propagating Virginian Creeper is to layer the shoots in sandy loam soil in pots. They will soon take root and thus form a new plant.

The young shoots growing from the roots.of the Rambling Rose should be tied in and en- couraged. It is from these shoots that the best of next.year's blooms will be produced. Cut out all thin kind weakly growths. •

===== 0002601_19130913_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
INCREASING VIOLAS.

The popularity ,of the viola has steadily in- creased year by year, until to-day if .easily raiiks 'among the half-dozen favourite flowers. The variety Gracilis Is one of the best of the recent introductions, resembling a- huge violet and producing a mass of flowers over a long period. Viola cuttings may be taken in August and September. The young shoots ,which spring from the base should he selected for preference, often having roots, and being established in their new quarters with no trouble whatever. Unroofed shoots, provided they are not sappy or hollow, may also be taken, however, the cut being made below the joint two inches from the base, and the two lower leaves being removed. They will require no other preparation. The most suitable place for viola cuttings is a shady border, well dug, levelled, and made firm, and with plenty of road grit mixed well in with the soil. The cuttings must be planted firmly three inches apart each way, and given a good water- ing through a rose. They will need no further attention save damping over on all fine days. ,

HARDY ANNUALS FOR SPRING FLOWERING.

. Often during the spring there is a gap or scarcity of flowers between he bulbous flower- ing -plants and- -the summer flowers. It is at this peripd of the year that annuals and biennials, sown at the present time, light up the garden with welcome masses of fresh and vivid colourings. At one time it was not recog- nised to any great extent to sow annuals at this time of the year, and" even now they 'are. not used to:the extent they deserve. There are niany ;quite suitable—Cornflowers, Collinstas, Venus' Looking-glass, Shirley Poppies, Lim- nanthes, Virginian Stocks, Eschscholtzias, Larkspurs, Nemophila; SWeet Peas and many others; in fact, all hardy annuals are worth trying, as they can be purchased far quite al small outlay.' Successful Garden Fete at Cz House.

FULL OF HOPE AND EIti,EFIGY.

By kind permission of Captain George ,Cros- field a garden fete, with sale of work and sports, was held at Tower House, Lodge-lane, last Saturday afternoon, Glorious weather favoured the occasion, and as the attendance was far in excess of all anticipations, the pro- moters, the Warrington Junior Liberal Associa- tion, were more than satisfied with the results. In the absence of Mrs. John Crosfield, the pro- ceedings were opened by Mrs. E. Osborne, and the grounds were visited during the afternoon by many prominent Liberals, including Mr. C. E. Parker, president of the Liberal Association, and Alderman James Evans. The success of the undertaking surpassed all expectations, and there is no doubt that another year the juniors will tackle a much more ambitious programme. At the opening several speeches were deliv- ered from a platform in the sports field to a large find interested audienC& of both sexes. Mr. John Griffiths occupied the chair.

Crosileld for the exceedingly kind way in which he had allowed his beautiful house and grounds to be placed at their disposal. (Laughter and applause.) If there are any Conservative friends with us, however,. Mrs. Osborne proceeded, I extend to them a very sincere welcome, and hope that though Ave may differ in political.views, we may still be the best of friends. (Applause.) Per- sonally, I respect my opponents' opinions, and should be very sorry to think that I had no friend g amongst the Members of the Conserva- tive party. As a matter of fact, I know that I have, and I think it is almost criminal for any- one, especially those, in high places, to en- deavour to embitter the minds of others against those who may not share the same views. (Ap- plause.)

LTURDAY, SEPTEMBER 13, 1913.

Liberal Achievements and Liberal Aims for the Future.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
I.S.

field's

ORANGE DEMONSTRATIONS

What They Cost in Blood Money

and Human Suffering

BY A WARRINGTON IRISHMAN.

A Coleraine "Orangeman," writing to one of the Ulster weeklies, hints that as it has been suggested that Orangemen should cease their processions, Roman Catholics should do like- wise- Now, as a matter of fact, Nationalists, both in Great Britain and Ireland, never hold any annual demonstrations to perpetuate any national event, such as the " 12th " is with the Carson party. As to whether the Orange gatherings should be held or not, we will allow a Royal Commis- sion and the friends of the Orange party to speak. The Belfast Royal Commission of 1857 says: "The 12th of July has always brought with it its Orange gatherings, its party dis- plays, and its consequent riots. The Belfast riots of 1886 lasted five months, during which period firearms were used frequently, thirty-two lives were lost, 432 policemen were injured, women insulted and shot down in the streets, twenty-nine private houses were wrecked and looted—twenty-two of these belonging to Roman Catholics.' The value of property. lost was esti- mated at £90,000, which had to be made good by the ratepayers; four hundred and ninety-two arrests were made, and the number of police alone in that city (not including military) amounted to 2,000." This is one specimen of the work of men for whose wives- and children, " Our Member" in- tends to open boarding establishments in the town which he mis-represents. The Derry procession of 1869 cost the rate- payers at least £12,000, and the conduct of the mobs organised to loot the houses of Roman Catholics, was a disgrace to the "law-abiding, loyal, and peaceful citizens." The "London Times" of August 18th, 1870, denounces these "yellow" processions. The. Bel- fast Royal Commission of 1857 characterises as a public scandal the disorder and confusion in Belfast, arising.' out of these demonstrations. Similar evidence was given by Mr. Berwick, the Commissioner appointed to inquire into the Dolly's Brae massacre of '49. In his report he strongly urged upon the Government the neces- sity of putting down Orange processions "with a strong hand." Mr. Thomas Drummond, once Under-Secretary, stated: "The effect of Orange procession.s was not confined to the days on which they took place. The spirit excited on those days remained for a long period after- wards, and on every succeeding fair, or when-

ever the people were brought together, disturb- ances followed." The Royal Commission of Inquiry into the riots of 1886 urged the Govern- ment to abolish these "Orange processions," and referred in strong terms to the " exaspera- tion " caused by them, and declared that "the Catholics were not to blame for the disturb- ances, and were quite willing to accept a general prohibition." The late Colonel Sanderson, M.P., leader of the Orange party, declared in the House of Commons, "that in the present (1870) disturbed state of Ireland, they (Orange processions) should cease.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
A DISGRACE TO PROTESTANTISM.

But these law-abiding subjects of King Carson still continue their proceedings, which are inimical to the peace of the country, of whose population they only form one-fifth. The Grand Orange Lodge expels Orangemen for such high crimes as "marrying a Papist," or sup- porting a candidate "in favour of Catholic Uni- versities"; but it considers the welfare of men who take part in illegal processions, who destroy property, who shed blood, who erect such posters as "Hell roast the Pope " (this poster I saw at Killykeen on August 24th this year), on the occasion of a visit from their Comman- der-in-Chief.

The "Edinburgh Review," referring to these Orange Lodges, says: "The evil of these exclu- sive associations is the they engender, the resistance which they provoke, and the counter-associations which they call into exist- ence," The Protestant officials, .statesmen and writers, who have witnessed the -displays of the party, describe them as "Crowds of mis- creants," "The very scum of society," "A dis- grace to Protestantism," "A banditti of mur- derers," "A banditti of plundering ruffians," "A violent, ungovernable, mob," "Northern rebels, whose barbarity exceeded that of modern times, and brought back recollections of ancient ferocity and bloodshed."

Leck-y, the Unionist historian, referring to the enrolment of Orangemen as Yeomen in 1796, says: "They included the greatest fanatics,who inherited from their earli-er - stages traditions and habits of violence, which their leaders could not wholly suppress, and which the anarchy of the time was well fitted to encourage. The un- disciplined Orangemen, now armed, paid and lei loose upon the homes of Ulster, massacred and slaughtered their_ Catholic 'countrymen."

===== 0002601_19130913_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
DOES THE BOARDING-OUT SCHEME APPLY TO CATHOLICS?

Now, as the present-day Orangeman is no better than the "Mad Mullahs," who have given him birth, and is as liable to destroy to- day as he was a hundred and fifty years ago, we should like to know if Mr. Harold Smith, in his boarding-house scheme, extends its privileges to Catholic women and children? Does he still adhere to his letter in the "Warrington Guard- ian," when he said, in a reply to a letter that he "is not an Orangeman or a Rensitite"? Let a section of the p atholic community in Warrington consider whether Mr. Smith, whom they helped to send to represent Catholic inter- ests, has done so as well as the former member, Mr. Crosfield. and whether "they desire again to elect an .Orange sympathiser.

Demonstration.

The Tariff Reform arithmeticians have been at it again. Mr. Harold Smith has staled that there were 6,000 women members of the Tariff Reform League in Warrington, whereas the official account of the recent fete put the figure at 2,500 Mr. E. Wynne Jones exposed the inaccuracy, at the ProgressWe Labour League meeting on Monday. Mr. Jones also dealt very severely with the sug- gestion that Warrington women were going to shelter, feed and clothe the women of the Orange portion of Ulster. He said that prob- ably an explanation of the mysterious dis- appearance of the 3,500 'Warrington Women Tariff Reformers was that they had been sent to Ulster, to be re-exported, disguised as Ulster mothers.

Another large crowd assembled at the weekly meeting of the. Progressive Labour League at Bridge Foot on Monday, when. Mr. Joseph Elwell presided. Mr. P. M. Oliver and Mr. E. Wynne Jones were the principal speakers.

\IR. JOSEPH ELWELL AND TILE STATE OF

TRADE, Old-age Pauperism Abolished.

The CHAIRMAN gave an interesting review of the achievements of the Liberal party during the last seven years, and showed that the finances of the country were in a far more satisfactory Condition than was the case when the Conserva- tive party were wiped out of otlice. "We have," said Mr. Elwell, "been able to pay many of the bills the Tories left unpaid, and we have also been able to pay our debts as .we have gone along." In regard to social legislation, he thought all present would agree with him that there never was a period hi the history of the country when so much legislation had been passed in the interests ,of the workers. By Liberal legislation, pauperism had been reduced enormously', and outdoor pauperism had almost ceased to exist, so far as old people were con- cerned. The cry in 1906 was that there was un- employment on every hand, but now, he was glaff to say, that unemployment had practically ceased to exist.' (Hear, .hear.) In the cotton trade there were only about three in every thousand who were unemployed. With regard to other trades, work was standing day after day for the want of men to fill positions. (A Voice: "Especially in the iron trade.") Imports and exports had gone Alp by leaps arid bounds: The Tariff Reformer - told them that imports were an injustice to the working men, but the Imports ,had to be paid for by exports, and if they stopped things from coming in iliey would greatly affect the exports. There was not suffi- cient money in the country to conduct' the bust- ness,of the country if they had to pay for all their . imports in cash.- 'Fhey„must meet the. Imports With the .exports, (Applause.) . .

LIBERAL AIMS.

Mr. OLIVER said that they as Liberals must progress if they, were to hold their own. They must not simply look back in admiration on what they had dope in the past, for more re- mained to be dime in the future. (Hear, hear.) Though they went on, they must remember that their advance must be orderly and steady. Everything they did must be one great co-ordin- ated advance. The "Daily Mail" had had a great deal to say about the Insurance Act, and especially concerning the Post Office depositors, in a little book called "Hard Pacts about the Insurance Act." Showing that the hard facts of the "Daily Mail" had been falsified by the ,hard heads of Lancashire, Mr. Oliver said: In Warrington, of every .1,000 people who are in- sureci under the Act, 985 are members of ap- proved societies. In Wigan, out of every 1,000 insured people, 995 are in approved societies. Those who are Post/Office depositors must join properly before October 13th if they want to get full benefit. After further expounding on the various points in the Act, the Knutsford Divi- sion candidate dealt with the working of the Trades Boards Act in regard to wages. It was the intention of the Liberal party to bring the wages of the people to a more " civilised " stan- dard. Dealing with a firm in Liverpool, he said that, the average wage to women was Bs. 9d. per week. "Shame.") Before a woman could go to this "highly respectable" whole- sale firm, she had to bring a certificate from a master or mistress of a Sunday school, a clergyman or minister of religion. This "highly respectable ') firm expected to buy the hoI arMciur of Christianity for Bs. 9d. per week, and it eoilld not be done.. No one ()fight to ask a Christian to work for such a meagre pittance. (Hear, hear.) The question of hous- ing cOnditions was puzzling a large number of people at the present time, and the Liberal, party were about to do more than they had done in that direction:

THE LAND CAMPAIGN..

No Overcrowding Unless the Ulster Motherg • Come.

The land reform would be commenced on October 11th. When Mr. Lloyd George decided to tackle this question, he appointed a com- mittee. to inquire into the housing 6nditions of people both in the towns and in the, country, and very- extensive inquiries had been made. Some people said it was only a partisan in- quiry, and that only Liberals were asked to give information. Conservatives and anyone anxious for land reform had been asked, and had given information. Anyone could obtain a schedule and state their case. - Mr. Harold Smith might have sent for a schedule,, and he could imagine him-7- (A Voice: "Walton Halt.") .

"Yes," continued Mr. Oliver, " a schedule was sent to Walton Hall. But I can imagine Mr. Smith looking through the questions, some of which would puzzle him. He would come to the question asking him if there was any over, crowding in Warrington, and write down, 'Not at present, but wait -till I get my Ulster mothers. " (Laughter and a Voice: "They are not coming.")_ _

In conclusion, Mr. Oliver said that legislation passed on the information which Mr. Lloyd George had received, would be democratic and humane. They would "stick up." for the man who worked for the people. (Applause.)

Beer, after clearing his throat of, soot and smoke, addressed the assemhi He gm:10111y acknowledged the crowning honour the,s• con- ferred upon him by electing him to preside over such a large and repreSentadve gathering; He supposed the honour had•bakin, given) botcause of the fact that he was the biggest smolte•ot them

There were protests. against this statement being allowed to pass. by. Whiteeross, Daßarn, and Monks'.

A wire chimney asked it acid fumes and stenches generally w.ert.,, not to be counted to their credit. The Chairman said ti s would be allowed and the interruptions subsided.

Beer then explair+.2d; the °bled of the meet- ing. He said thes had gathered to make a united protest aga4nst certain ill-disposed per- sons in a local Rewspaper, who had niade attacks upon th.e: right \Odell Warrington chim- neys had enjeYed frOni time immemorial; to wit,

that of turning out as much blaek.smoke as they could, and as they liked. 'Their trade was ,in 'danger, and it they did not take active steps to protect themselves, Warrington would soon lose the proud right to call itself, in manufactures' smoke, the smokiest town in Lancashire. This was no time, he said, for words, but for action, and, as, tune was brief, and their fires were going out, with their kind permission he would ask leave to propose the following resolutions: 1. "That we, the Warrington Chimneys, in public meeting assembled, hereby resolve that we will maintain our vested interests and our ancient rights, and continue to exercise them by sedding out as much black smoke and other noxious, vapours as we like."

"That, inasmuch as the Warrington Health Committee had allowed the irksome time- limit of five minutes black smoke to the

half-hour to become a dead letter, its corpse . should be got .rid of, and it be hereby re- solved that it be forthwith chucked over Warrington Bridge, and its memory for- gotten."

The Chairman here said he felt called upon to explain that the word corpse in the second resolution referred to the corpse of the dead time-limit, and not to that of the Health Com- mittee. It would be far from the minds of those. present to drown such, an excellent Health Com- mittee, who had manifested their wisdom by years of masterly inactivity. (Loud applause.)

These resolutions were seconded by. White- cross, and carried with great enthusiasm. The Chairman then suggested tfiat three rous- ing cheers be given for the Mayor and Corpora- tion of Warrington. This was heartily re- sponded to, and accompanied by a special out- burst of black 'smoke, after which the band struck up a popular air, which the assembled company joined with great spirit in singing :

Dare to be a nuisance, Dare to smoke alone, Dare to make it black as pitch, And dare to hold our own.

The procession was then re-formed, the ban, ner and the prize band again in the front.

WHY WORSE THAN MANCHESTER?

We give to-day the result of a careful and exhaustive survey that has been made of the Warrington chimneys by an Inspector of the Smoke Abatement League. Perhaps the con- clusions to which this expert has come may rouse the town to action. We are held up as being worse offenders in the matter of smoke than the worst part of Man- chester, or any portion of the mirky neigh- bourhood of that great city. ' It should be borne in mind that all observa- tions at Failsworth and Warrington are on the half-hourly basis. We are solemnly assured that if action were taken in Warrington, comparable to the action taken by the Manchester City Council in its own area BY FAR THE GREATER PROPORTION OF THE SMOKE NOW PRODUCED COULD BE AVOIDED WITH- OUT INFLICTING ANY HARDSHIP ON MANUFACTURERS.

INSPECI OR'S REPORT.

Smoke that Puts us at Times into

"the Darkness of Night."

===== 0002601_19130913_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
ATMOSPHERE WORSE THAN ELSEWHERE,

At the request of a number Of citizens of War- rington, who were interested in the question of smoke abatement, the Manchester. and District Branch of the Smoke Abatement League of great _Britain, instructed their inspector to in- vestigate conditions iii regard to smoke in this town, and We now publish a report of the result of this gentleman's investigations, which has been handed to us by the League. - The inspector has had experience of very bad smoky districts in' the neighbourhood of Man- , chester, but he felt compelled to state that the condition of the atmosphere in Warrington was very considerably worse than he had ever, seen it elsewhere. In Failsworth, the worst district in the neigh- bourhood of Manchester, three sets of four chimneys observed on three days gave respec- tively 131 minutes black smoke, 311 _minutes, 35 minutes between them, . these being the worst records that the inspector had ever obtained. In --Warrington, three sets' of four chimneys observed qn three days, gave respectively, .55 minutes black smoke, 51 minutes, and 49 min,- utes between them. This gives an average of 8.4 minutes of black smoke for each of the Failsworth chimneys daily, and '12.3 minutes for the Warrington. Further, Failsworth is a district of many chimneys, and Warrington, in spite of the fact that there are many industries, has compara- tively few chimneys, apart from the wire and iron works and a large soap works. The atmos- phere is so darkened by smoke that our inspec- tor reports that at times it was like looking into - the darkness of the night. The inspector was of :opinion that the wire and iron works were the chief offenders. He was given to understand- that these industries claim exemption under the "special trades.", The large soap works show a clean bill, save only for one chimney. The inspector said he felt considerable sur- prise at the attitude of various persons in War- rington with whom he entered into conversa- tion. They appeared to regard the smoke as not harmful, though admitting its dirt, other- wise they seemed quite indifferent.

CHIMNEYS PROTEST.

Anitimt Rigbt to Smoke and Pam

Vindicated.

THE DEAD TIME-LIMIT._

On a certain morning during the weiit: there was a great demonstration of the Warnitigton Chinureys in Bank Park. It was held at, an early tour. So secretly had the arrangements been made, very few Warringtoniians knew anything, of it. The chimney stack,%canae and went like ghosts or bogey men. 1111le object of this unique demonstration is descaedi in the circular calling the gathering, a: copy of wliich was picked up in the Park afterwarcLs, written large in soot. It read as

Private and ConfideeliaL GREAT INDIGNATION, MEETING.

• .By Special Permisso of the MAYOR AND WARRINGTON CORPORATION.

Under the Distinguished Putionage of the WARRINGTON HEALTII COMMITTEE.

PROTECTION FOR BiAM SMOKE.

WARRINGTON CUININEY STACKS

ASSEMBLE IN YOUR HUNDREDS

At 5 a.m. in Bank Park.

BLACKLEGS INVITED.

NO SURRENDER.

Grim pickets were placed at the gates of the Park to prevent the admission of Alderman Bennett • ,and any ' representative of the "Examiner." It having been revealed to our reporter in a dream that such a Meeting was to be held, he was, as 'ever, on the Spot. Being d&lined admission at the gates, he climbed the railings and secreted himself in the shrubbery. From this hidden vantage he was able to get a good view of the company as they assembled. There were chimneys of every sort, shape. and 'size.

There were chimneys big, and chimneys tall, Chimneys thick, and chimneys small. Chimneys stout, and chimneys lean, Most were black, a few were clean. There were chimneys young, and chimneys

old, Chimneys hot, and chimneys cold, Chimneys single, and chimneys in pairs, Chimneys who aped fine lordly airs. There were chimneys of brick and chimneys

of fin, • Many were fat, and some were thin. Most were proud, a few were humble, Some were old and ready to tumble. Some were smoking as well as they can; One was panting because he ran. Two there Were, each like a lass, These surely must be chimneys for glass. Some were smart as smart could be, Most were dirty as here you see. some were gasping for want of their fanner, Two were holding aloft a banner. One blew loud a brass trombone— Great credit to him, he did it alone. One who was late took off his hat, [I fear he must have got .cold after-that.] Some there were who thoukht it a lark: What fun it will be to smoke iirthe Park.' And one was giving a small drum whacks, Glory and honour for chimney stacks!

The procession was led by two stalwart stacks who carried. between them a banner witir the

===== 0002601_19130913_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON IRISHMEN

Resent the Orange Board and Lodging Proposal.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
A BOMBASTIC SHOW.

Since Mr. Harold Smith's "latest" was men- tioned in Warrington, we have ben living in anticipation of the day when the women of Ulster 'will be under the roofs 4f loyal Tariff Reforrii dames, whilst their husbands take part in the great Irish battle. Whilst all along doubting the possibilities of such an event tak- ing place, we have aWaited further statements on this question, hut none have been forthcom- ing. The •absolute futility of his initiating a, "Women of Ulster League waS illustrated at a meeting of the 'John Maginn".Branch of the -United Irish League in Winwick,road on Sun- day. The members were to have had an address frau Mr. P. J., Kelly (Manchester) on "From Gratton .to. Redmond," bid that gentle- man, was Unavoidably Unable to attend..

===== 0002601_19130913_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
TIIE NEXT ELECTION.

After apologising for the speaker's absence, Mr. D. CURRIE (the chairman) spoke on the question of the " war" in Ulster. He mid there would be no fight in Ulster at all. (A Voice: "If there is it will be with wooden guns.") The only fights there would be would take place bet Ween individuals who had got the worse for liquor. Harold Smith had had a great deal to say on the matter, but he (the speaker) was quite sure that there never was a more bombastic show- than that of Mr. Smith during the last few weeks. (Hear, hear.) Con ,- tinning, he said: "The only excuse I can give Mr. Smith for talking as he does, is., that he is a bit of a play-writer, and play-writers have generally large imaginations. They create a number of queer creatures, and the matter about the Warrington- women taking over, feeding and clothing the women of Ulster, is one of the creatures of his creation. It is his dramatic imagination at the bottom of it. We need have no fear, Par we shall not be bothered with the women from Ulster." In conclusion, he said: "At the next election Mr. Smith will have nowhere to lay his head. He has now no excuse to make for the fact that the Unionist party has not a solid programme to put before the people and he wants to attract their atten- tion by some fiction, from the North of Ireland. He will not misrepresent Warrington any longer after the next election. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Mr. HANDLEY endorsed what the Chairman had said and explained that he had expressed the feeling of all his .fellow-countrymen.

OFFICIAL COMMENT ON REPORT. A Word to the Manufacturers.

The Smoke Abatement League of Great Britain, 20, Mount-street, Manchester, Septem- ber 2nd, 1913. Manchester and District Branch.

To the Editor," Examiner" Office,Warrington. Sir,—We enclose herewith observations taken by our smoke inspector in 'Warrington on August 12th, 14th, 15th, and September Ist, together with a report of his investigations. He had four full days there, and as you will see, obtained a very large number of observations, far more in fact than he has ever been able to obtain round Manchester, in a similar length of time. Apart from this, he reports that the fac- tory smoke in Warrington is, without doubt, much worse than anything we suffer from in or near Manchester. A study ,of the observa- tions will show that this is not due only to works in which, owing to the special nature of their manufactures, it impossible to avoid a certain amount of smoke, although the worst offenders are undoubtedly the wire and iron works (an observation of four typical chimneys Of one of these works actually gave an average of .over 15 minutes black smoke in the half- hour). Still there are others with bad records,'. who can offer no such excuse. There is no reason why brewers and millers should make anything like the amount of smoke reported. It seems probable that the effect of the dense masses of smoke poured out by the ironworks, is to make such emissions of black smoke as five or ten Minutes in the half-hour (which would be considered excessive in another town), appear quite within a reasonable limit to a Warrington manufactur.er, with the result that the whole average of smoke production is much worse than elsewhere. The observations taken during these four nays are, in our opinion, absolute proof that if action were taken in Warrington, comparable to the aeti)on taken by the Manchester City Council in its own area, by far the greater proportion of the ,snipke now produced could be avoided with- out :inflicting any hardship on manufacturers. We trust that the efforts made may arouse the ' Warrington manufacturers to a sense of their duty to the conmninity in the matter. Yours faithfully, E. D. SIMON, Chairman.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

MINIMUM WAGE OF 245.

The principle of a minimum wage of 245. a week for all Corporation employes was approved by the Widnes Town Council on Tuesday night, without a division.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
WORRY LEADS TO DEATH

Elizabeth Smith .(60), widow, residing with her married daughter at 6, Water-street, Wid- nes, was drowned in the canal, near Hutchin- son's works, on Sunday morning. She had been troubled because her son had been taken to the Royal Infirmary, Liverpool, suffering from some internal complaint. The woman was seen struggling in the water. She was got out and artificial respiration tried, but to no ,purpose.

BURNT BY EXPLODING GAS.

An alarming explosion took place' at the Calder and Mersey Extract Workp, at Ditton, .near Widnes. on Tuesday morning, and the fol- lowing men are suffering from extensive burns: Thomas Baird, of Lacey-street, Widnes; Denis Rourke, Muspratt-street, Widnes; Gilbert Ben- nett, Frederick-street, Widnes; and William Edwardson, of the Clapgate Inn, Ilalebank... The men were at work in one of the sheds, when a "blow-back" occurred from the pro- ducer of the gas plant. A sheet of flame, described by. one of.the men as about eight feet in width, shot out and readied through the building for a considerable distance.•

GREATEST EMPIRE: GREATEST REPUBLIC.

Dedicating, at Put-in-Bay, Ohio, a Cowering column of white marble, 335 ft. in height, which dominates the surrounding waters and country, commemorating a century of peace between the United States and Great Britain, Mr. Taft, who was the principal speaker, said: "We are here to celebrate the rearing of a beacon of perpetual peace. .We cherish the remembrance of the battle at this spot (or the teaching its memory brings of the practical possibility of unending love and peace between international neigh- bours."

Among the other speakers was Governor Cox, of Ohio, who mentioneci. "the Mutuality of interest which would cause the greatest Repub- lic and the greatest Empire to join hands across the seas and the circle of the globe, and, as messengers of peace, to control within their span of even balanced amity all nations."

"I have met your mother, you know, chil- dren," he answered, without le o:ing round— "at least, I used to meet her once." The children sprang to his side in astonish- ment. Graine was sitting on the low balus- trade that bounded the terrace. "Do you know mother, cousin? they cried in a breath. "How funny—how nice ! " added the girl. "But, of course, you were a friend of father's." Graine nodded. His eyes were sad and still fixed on the far-off hills. "I knew her, Nancy, Ni hen she was no big- ger than you are," he replied. "And wouldn't you like to see her again?" eried4Cecil Hardy. "She is so nice—nicer than anybody in Hillborough•" The man was silent for a moment, then a faint colour rose to his cheeks, a smile flickered on his lips. "Yes, I should like to very much indeed," said he, rising from the balustrade. " Will you two children take me to see her?" "But mother lives in town," objected Cecil, "and is ever so busy. And we can't go to London." "Let's put it another way," continued the Squire, laughing, though there was an anxious look in his eyes. " Supposing I were to take you two kiddies along with me, we could go and see mother together. I could look after you, and in return you might--" " Might what? " inquired the girl. Graine hesitated. " lie stammered, "reintroduce me— break the ice, you know." The children were mystified. Was their friend doubtful as to his reception? "Mother will be so pleased to see you," cried Nancy, reassuringly; "she'd be fond of anyone we're fond of. And I know she'd' like you. "But there's auntie," said her brother, who had an aptitude for seeing obstacles. "Oh, I think I can arrange that with Miss Hardy," observed Richard Graine, thought- fully. "And if I can manage it, of course you'll come. I must have you two with me." "Of course we':l come, Cousin Dick," cried they in a breath. "It will be such fun. We have never been to see mother in London." How Richard Graine persuaded Miss Hardy to allow her nephew and niece to go to town in his charge will never be known, but the Squire had a way with him that was hard to withstand, and succeeded in effecting his purpose. The very next Wednesday found Graine and his two young cousins in the morning express en route for Euston. The children were wild with excitement and in high spirits, their companion silent, preoccupied, and per has a trifle nervous. bu's mother ILOW We're Coming, ousin Dick ? " said Nancy, as the train ran through the north-western suburbs, and the hour and a-half's journey was all but ended. "No, I don't think she does," admitted the man guardedly. "I thought it would be a surprise." The children were delighted at such an ex- cellent idea, though the originator was scarcely .as satisfied. "Then we'll go straight to Drayton Man- sions," cried the girl. I know where she lives; it's close to Sloane-street." But Cousin Dick was not so precipitate. "I think we three had better have lunch together, first, Nancy," said he; "your mother is so busy, I'm not quite sure that we should. find her at the flat.'

The young faces fell. "But we shall see her early in the after- noon," the Squire continued, and then you shall have tea with her, even if I can't. Eh, little ones? "

They were a trifle disappointed, but, after all, lunch alone with Cousin Dick at a big London restaurant they found -to be great fun; and when the bill had been paid came the prospect of meeting their mother, of giv- ing her the surprise of her life. Into the han- som they scrambled, and away they went through the maze of traffic. 'Are we going the right way?" demanded the boy, to whom London streets were a re- velation. ' "Yes, Cecil," replied Graine absently. "To Sloane-street?" inquired Nancy. A smile broke on the thin, pale features of her cousin, as he answered. "Well, not to Sloane-street. Mother is at a much bigger house this afternoon, and per- haps we'll have to wait a little while to see her, but she'll be there, never fear. Why, here we are; let me get out and help you two down." It was a very splendid house indeed at which the cab had stopped. There were ser- vants in gorgeous livery, heavy carpets on the floor, wide steps and any number of lights. And besides, the place in which they found themselves was so big and full of such lots of people, and there was a band playing close beside them. But it seemed all very strange as they sat in those nice comfortable seats, one on each side of Cousin Dick, facing a great curtain on which was a picture of a blue lake with ships upon it, and in the distance snow-capped mountains. But alas! where was their mother—she whom they had come all the way from Hill- borough to see? The boy turned and stared about him anxiously. There were ever so many people—men, women, and other chil- dren—rows on rows of them—but where was mother?

__- Brother and sister glanced at the man be- tween them; tears were rising to their eyes; there was a lump in the throat of each. Had Cousin Dick played them false? Graine read the look of doubt and appeal in the upturned faces. For a moment it seemed that a, mist was rising before, his eyes too, as he whispered softly: "You will ,see her in a ininute, children.. . . 'Look ! " . . _

. . As h-spoke h band, u• —a ,e the w..ich. Was some- where just in front of them, began playing very softly. Then suddenly it grew quite dark, and as instinctively the little ones' hands sought his that gorgeous curtain, pic- ture and all, quivered, curled, and rose silently and swiftly upwards, and there stood the mother before her children, with her brown eyes as bright as ever,, and the sweet smile on those parted lips that so often had givea them a good-night kiss. And oh! how pretty she looked in ipite of that queer old-fashioned dress, and how the people in the darkness behind them clapped their hands as she came close to the row of dazzling lights and began to sing in that deep clear voice whose cadence had often-, time lulled brother and sister 'to sleep. The boy sprang up. Cousin Dick clutched at his arm; the child was too quick for him, but atria! there was a barrier beyond which the band was playing. The funny bald-headed man who was wav- ing the short stick glanced behind him; he fancied he had ,heard a voice cry "Mother." And perhaps, too, that winsome lady in the Louis Quinze costume caught ' the exclama- tion, for she looked downwards for a moment across the footlights, for a moment hesitated, then, smiling, sang as she had never sung before. Richard Graine's cheeks were scarlet as he drew his -cousin to his side, the people to right and left of them were smiling, the chil- dren's faces were alight with pleasure. The children could never remember a tithe of all they saw that afternoon. To them it seemed like some long dream' a very terrible dream in parts, but one that ended happily afteK all. But there was their mother before them all the wbile, talking, laughing or singing. And at times she crie too—and the tears rose to the little ones' ey s as well—but when at the last the man she had saved from all kinds of peril held her in his arms, their joy knew no bounds, though Cousin Dick bit his lip and seemed strangely out of temper. And then ' the people clapped their hands again, and Ethel Harding—few in the theatre knew her as Constance Hardy—smiled and curtseyed, with a special curtsey for the very middle of the front row of the stalls, and then the great curtain sank down once more as, amid the stir of : countless feet,. the or- chestra played "God save the King." "They all love mother," said Nancy, as Graine led the children from the main en- trance and down a side street to quite a dingy little door.

The man nodded abstractedly. "But not so much a we do, do they, Cousin Dick? " chimed in the boy. ".No, I think not," answered Cousin Dick, as he handed a card to the doorkeeper. - -

It was quite a small room where they found their mothcr, and somewhai, to the children's disappointment, • she had cliscanled those gorgeous clothes, and wore just an ordinary dress the same es • other reople. Eut

looked very pretty none the less, al d her face was aflush with happiness. "Oh, it was nice," cried the boy and girl. "And it was good of Cousin Dick to bring US, wasn't it? He likes children, you know, and is ever so fond of us."

"It is good of him," replied Constance Hardy, and deep grew the scarlet on her cheeks as she glanced at the silent men be- side the door, "and I am so glad to hear that Cousin Dick likes children; I used not to think so once, but now I'm sure of it." So the four drove back to Drayton Man- sions together that evening in early summer, and at tea the children bore the burden of the conversation, for their mother and cousin were strangely silent. And after tea, when Cecil and Nancy were thinking ruefully of the Laurels and their return to • Hillborough, it suddenly struck Constance Hardy that perhaps, after all, a second journey would be too much for her children in one day, and suggested to Cousin Dick that he should send a wire to Aunt Margaret saying that the two were stay- ing in Drayton Mansions for the night. You can put up at the club, you know," said she, "and can take them home to-morrow."

Never was proposal received with greater approval, and later on the four had dinner to- gether; then their .mother, having left strict orders with the maid that the children should go to bed at eight, drove away with Cousin Dick.

It was very late when Richard Graine and Constance Hardy returned to the Chelsea Hat, but for all that Cecil and Nancy were wide awake, and had been running in and out of each other's room as the maid told their mother.

"Oh ! you naughty children," cried the latter; . but she did not seem very angry ,after all, and, Cousin Dick, who was standing in the passtige, came loyally to the culprits' rescue. "But it is a special occasion, Constance," said he, with a smile, as Cecil seized him by his long dust - coat. "She's not very angry," said Nancy, sit- Ling' Up in bed to take stock of the visitors— the two grown-ups, and the boy between them—" are you mother?"

The woman smiled. "No, since it is a very special oceastOn, as Cousin Dick says." "And we may come and see you act agai4l" cried Cecil; "you do look prett:f up in London.L But their mother shook her head. " I dub": know. I think not," said she, with a glance at the man at her side. "I don't think I shall act much longer." "Oh, but you love it, mother. Cousin Dick told_ us you did."

"Yes, -I do, perhaps," she admitted. "Then, why—" stammered Nancy; but in a moment she had read the look on her mother's face. "But you love us better." Constance Hardy smiled. "Yes, dear," she replied. "You see it was for you that I acted." Her son, who held her fingers and the hand of the man beside him, looked up in her face questioningly. "Then why should you give it up?" The flush on the cheeks of the actress grew deeper, her dark eyes were bent upon the aoor, as she answered, softly: "For you, children, and—for Cousin Dick." No fewer than fifty members of the Royal Family will be present at the inzirriage of Prince Arthur of Connaught and the Duchess of Fife. The Empress Marie of Russia will ack-..ompany Queen Alexandra and the Princess Victoria. The bridesmaids will be eight in number—Princesses Ntiry, Maud of Fife, Patricia of Connaught, Mary of Teck, and Lady Mary Cecil being among the number. Prince John and Prince Olaf of Norway are to be pages. As the Chapel at St. James's Palace accounnodates only three hundred people—and many more will attend the ceremony—stands NO I I be erected in the forecourt of the Palace for a number of the guests. Space cannot be found for the men of the Prince's Regiment, who are anxious to line the aisle of the Chapel, but they will form a guard outside, and a travelling escort. There will be eight separate processions of Royalties, with an interval of five minutes between the arrival of each. MOTOR CYCLING, FOR WOMtN. The motor cycle and the sidecar gain in popularity every day, and the question of its housing has received the attention of builders of small suburban houses. Formerly the latter had very narrow side entrances, which accom- modated a bicycle, but were not wide enough for the passage of the newer machine and car. Consequently the entrances of most recently- built houses are very much wider. Women riding on motor cycles in not yet seen every The day either in town or n the country, but exercise is daily gaining their favour, as is proved by the fact that there were three lady competitors in the recent Scottish reliability tests, each of whom achieved success, and ap- peared none the worse for the six strenuous days, during which they accomplished thousand miles, miles, over very trying ground,

CROSS CANADA ON A BICYCLE. An Englishwoman has undertaken a remark • able feat, which is nothing more or less than the crossing of Canada from east to west on a bicycle, a distance of some three thousand miles. she is also earning her expenses by sell- ing magazines and writing descriptions of her adventures to papers. So bad were the roads in Quebec that she often pushed her bicycle ten or twelve miles a. day. But if the roads were bad, the people were most hospitable. She seldom stayed at hotels, but,stopped at cottages, ivhere..she was well treated. Ort;coaching',on river she found the bridge was Own, but nOth- jug daunted got her bicycle on il(raft, and 'so floated across. She cortunenced her adven-• turbus journey towards the end of June last. AN EXPERT ON DRESS OF Me. DAY.

It is gratifying to find that the prevailing, styles of feminine attire have met with the whole-hearted 'approval of an eminent health expert, who asserts that .slit skirts and open corsages admit of a freer circulation of air about the body, which acts as a stimulant and tonic on the skin. The absence of weight on tlie hips (the result of banishing petticoats and underskirts) is of great assistance to the organs, and accounts for the diminution of various aches and pains that formerly attacked the feminine back. Then the pores of the Man are Opened by more frequent bathing, and the body is relieved of much unnecessary work, because perspiration is more free. The prevailing taste for diaphanous materials comes in for approba- tion. whilst fewer undergarments make less de- mand on the energy of the.wearer, and more- over they require less attention in the matter of making, mending and lvashing. BETWEEN-SEASON FASHIONS.

Many new modes make their appearance between the seasons, and among 'these, two de- serve.notice;,although it is possible that neither will become popular, as they now appear. They will, however, be subjected to many modifica- tions, but they show the trend of fashion for the future. One is the three-tier skirt, which will be seen later on in satin and other tailored fabrics ;_the,other ,is-.the„ peg-top trouser .skirt.. Already autumn suits ,have flounced effects brought about by means of The tunic, and as thw make a change from the straight, skirt, which yin. still be-worn. doubtless many on en will .aclopt them. The'peg-top trouser,i‘kirt. is full at "the waist, with a long. tapering,line to the ankle. .A:n3ttsauline-witisteont accotapintes the coat, Whic.h as -a-rule is ef-thef.ton;•srarlet. The three-quarter ,cut-away ,coat tlfat dips'at The back is another' change from 'the short coat, worn hitherto with the 'tailored suit.

TAILORED NET BLOUSES. • • Tailors make further invasions into the domain Of the dressmaker, every season, and encroach on departments formerly, considered without their realm. This year they have made very pretty tucked net blouses, which-have been sent out with linen or other summer suits; and for autumn tweeds and cloths, the daintiest of diaphanous materials are utilised, not only in white, but in colours, to accompany the coats and, • skirts. Almost the A latest -garments t& be annexed by the tailor are the fashionable opera cloaks—already being trimmed With fur. Very useful and pretty are the lace coats, that fall in long graceful lines, originally designed to be worn with various rest gowns. A new use has been found for them. They are arranged tunic fashion over, evening gowns, that are no longer in their- prestine freshness, and thus :bring :them quite up_to date. - •Sometimes these mils-tare of a filinpmaterial, or of net trimmed with lace, 'but any ..case they make useful , . and serviceable garments, whether adapted, or used with the gowns' for they ~were de- signed. ,

'WOOLWORK REVERS AND WAISTCOATS. The waistcoat is one of the principal features of autumn fashions. and is often worn over a blouse of net or other thin material, and re- moved with the coat.. One of the smartest -is of woolwork. stitched on fine 'canvas, and re- sembles the favourite fancy work of early Vic- torian times. The same work is used for revers and cuffs, on. tailored suits, and has the .merit of durability as well as novelty. In brocade. fhp waistcoat is a most desirable accessory. but it tends to accentuate the present shapeless waist awl to increase the size of the hips when if is of the Directoire fashion, and is made with a basque.-- •

COUSIN DICK'S ALLIES.

Br NORMAN INNES Author of "His Ghost," "Parson Croft," "The Surge of War," &e.

They stood in the drawing-room of the small Chelsea flat, the man looking out of the window at the blackness of a November even- ing, the woman standing beside the mantel- piece staring into the fire. Both were young —the visitor rhther more than thirty, up- right, clean-shaven, blue-eyed; his hostess some three years less, tall, brown-haired, with eyes and lips that, as a rule, were for ever But there was little laughter in that pretty face that foggy afternoon, in spite of the brightness of the room and the fact that its occupants had been boy-and-girl lover's. One of them would have resumed the old- time state of affairs; but the other demurred.

"No," faltered the woman, without lifting her glance from the blazing logs. "It can't be; you see, circumstances are so changed." "And people with them," muttered her wompanion, taking a sudden interest in the verations of a lamp-lighter in the street below. "It's too unkind of you to say that," cried his hostess, almost fiercely. "You know it isn't true at, all; and besides "—and ehe broke into a nervous laugh—"you don't like children. I've heard you say so a dozen times."

The man swung round; there was a look of reproach on the thin, keen features. "Some children I like," he answered. "But, after all, they are nothing to the point. Yours is a poor excuse, at best." In her doubt she had hazarded a flippant reply to break the spell of his earnest plead- ing, and was as much annoyed at the weak- ness of her attempt as at its failure. Notwithstanding the falsity of her position, she affected a fine passion. "No," she cried, "I can't—l on't. I can never forget my duty to my children." She vas fac!ng• her visitor, but her glance had wandered to the portrait of a naval officer in a 'silver frame upon the mantelpiece. The man was silent for a moment as he gazed blankly in those tearful eyes, at the flushed cheeks and quivering lips. • "Perhaps you are riiht," he replied. "I am sorry. Good-bye.' He held out his hand, and added, in lower tones: "But if ever you should—" Vehemently that pretty lady ansvered, with • petulant stamp of her feet: " No, I shall never ! "

And the man went without another word, closed the door a trifle sharply, nor heard the sobs that grief was wringing from a gentle, aching heart.

Tlillborough is a sleepy country town, dull to a degree, and the Laurels was per- haps the dullest house in the place, as its owner, Miss Margaret Hardy, vc as the prim- mest of the mealy prim maiden ladies to be found in the neighbourhood. Even the pre- sence of a couple of children had failed to drive the air of gloom from the dingy win- dows; though, to do Nancy and Cecil Hardy justice, they did their best under most de- pressing circumstances. The girl was nine, the boy nearly eight, and since their father's death, within four years of his marriage, they had lived ith their aunt under the care of an old nurse who had been their mother's servant.

They were lonely children. Their father, a navel officer, they had never known; their mother they saw but at the rarest intervals:— she was so very-busy; and Aunt Margaret, if kind in her stern, old-fashidned way, .knew nothing of the sympathy for which a child's heart longs. It was oi.ly upon Mrs. Hardy's very occa- sional visits that an atmosphere of life and of gaiety reigned triumphant in the Laurels; even the dulness of Hillborough vanished before her radiant presence. Her manner was as irresistible as the smartness of her hats and frocks. Aunt Margaret herself confessed, with some regret, that her sister-in-law had but to trip down the main street and every respectable tradesman and disreputable er- rand-boy would stand agape. To tell the truth, the children were not quite sure that their prim old relative was as fond of their mother as she ought to be, of that vision of bright eyes, waving brown hair, and laughing .lips. What _a pity it was `bat she was always so busy, this fascinating mother of theirs, away in far-off London, where everyone, it seemed, was either busy or beautiful. Not that Constance Hardy was forgetful of her little ones buried in the country—though a woman may be very beau- tiful and ever so busy, yet she has a mother's heart even in London town. And she sent them sweets and toys, and for the girl the daintiest frocks—frocks that Aunt Margaret altered sadly before her niece might wear them—and at times quite a long letter. But oh! the writing, even nurse could hardly read it.

Her visits to Hillborough were the brighte.st moments in the children's lives, more eagerly looked forward to than a birthday, lq,nger remembered than the summer trip to' the seaside. There were few young folks of their own age in the place, and to Cecil and Nancy these seemed neither as young nor as full of life as their mother, with her smiles and her kisses, and the long, long' stories she would tell them. "' ;

Besides, she never found fault like auntie, who was for ever fussing. Nurse was almost as bad—for ever holding before Them the dire results of Wet feet. the enerinity of muddy boots or torn clothing. In conse- quence, their • daily walks were confined to the toWn itself or to the_high road; but rarely were they allowed to wander into the country wer the fields and commons. • , However, one friend the children had—a friend they valued highly, who• would stop for a moment when he met them, who at times took them for long walks, and often asked them to tea at his big house and where there were always lots of people, who were quite different from the majority of their aunt's friends.

Richard Graine—Cousin Di& the children called him—was a distant family connection. Like their father, he had been in the Navy, but had retired before reaching the rank of Commander. He lived at the Grange, a large house on the outskirts of Hillhorough hunted, shot, and did a little amateur farm- ing. He was rich, spent much of his time in tciwn ; but was fond of the country, and was never absent very long from home.

He was a great faviCurite in the neighbour- hood of Hillborough; everyone knew the young Squire, with his swinging stride, and his bluff, open-hearted manner. There was no more brilliant performer 'cross -country than the master of the Grange, and every :form of rural sport found in him an ardent supporter. - ..• : , . Cecil .and _Nancy Hardy numbered him among their earliest friends; he had been a; shipmate •of their father's, • and had struck the children as being so different from the ma- '-irity of their elders, who too often seemed dored or embarrassed by their presence. That spring they had seen much of Cousin Dick, and had spent Many a long day at the Grange when the primroses were shewing yellow in the park. Graine had left Hill- borough but once since Christmas; for him it seemed that London had suddenly lost its attraction. Nor did he entertain as much as had been his custom in the past; few visitors came to the Grange, and the Squire's interest seemed centred in his farm. Rumour had it that all had not gone well of 'ate with the .young man's affairs; hints of rash specula- tion went the round of the neighbourhood. "You must be very fond of boys and girls, Cousin Dick," said Nancy Hardy, one sunny afternoon tow ards the end of May, as the Children sat at tea upon the terrace. "Or perhaps of me and Nancy," sug- gested the brother. Their host looked at them with a smile. "I think I am fond of children," he replied, "and, of course, of you two kiddies." "And do you like us very much," de- manded the boy. "More than other people?" "Well, that's rather a big order. There are a few other nice people in the -world," said the Squire, as the smile faded from his face .

"Of course there are," assented Nancy. "There's mother. . I'm sure you'd like mother, Cousin Dick." - The man had turne& and was lookiug out

POINTS FOR TARIFF REFORMERS.

SAUL AMONG THE PROPHETS.

Tariff Reform newspapers are not always cautious in their admissions, and not infre- quently they bear witness in one .column to a truth they attack in another. The following extract from the " Standard " of August 21st gives the lie to the oft repeated statement of Tariff Reformers that Protection secures that any increased cost of living shall be counter- balanced by a corresponding increase in wages: The problem of the rising cost of living is attracting considerable attention in all the States just now. Increased wages have not proved effective in off-setting the higher prices of necessities, as the,cost of living appears to go up concurrently.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
TILE FARM LABOURER IN GERMANY.

Herr H. Kaufmann (Hamburg), speaking at the third day's session of the International Co- operative Alliance Congress in Glasgow, on August 27th, pointed out that. in spite of .the high protection afforded to agriculture in Germany, the farm labourers were in a very unsatisfactory condition. Said Herr Kaufmann:

in Germany many thousands of agricultural labourers came to the towns, and brought down the wages of industrial workers, and so undermined the standard of wages 'which organised industrial workers had gained. They are not permitted to enjoy the luxury of emigration, and, consequently, instead of a "rush to Canada," there is the "rush to the towns."

and W. Furness. THE MAYOR'S HOPE.—Sarah Agnes Broderick, who said she was a native of Prescot, appeared on remand on a charge of neglecting her child, which was six months old.—Mr. Browne, who prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C., said the woman had taken the child to the workhouse hospital, where it was found to weigh whereas it should have weighed 1111 b. It had gained one pound. The child was improving, and he asked that the case be further adjourned for four weeks.—Prisoner:. I am not going to stay in for life.—The Mayor: I hope you will not die in four weeks.—The case was adjourned for four weeks.

LODGER VOTES.

Sons Who Can Turn Parents Out of Their Rooms.

SOME NOVEL ARRANGEMENTS.

A lodger vote was granted 'to Robert William Beswick in respect of 229, Wilderspool-causeway. Claimant said his mother was the tenant of the house.

Mr. Sanders supported the claim. The house was practically the claimant's. His mother- in-law lived with him and his wife, and natur- ally she wanted to keep the house in her name. He had full use of the bedroom and sitting- rooni.

MR. SANDERS LONGS FOR A FEW PUBLICANS.

In respect of the Feathers Hotel, George Hill Rogers claimed a lodger vote. In objecting, Mr. Sanders said that a similar claim was iiiade by the claimant's brother last year, but Without success. The present case was evidently an attempt to see whether his Honour had forgotten the circumstances. There was no alteration at all.

Mr. Wharton: As long as I have the assistance of Mr. Woodcock enterprising lodger claimants may keep away, because I know everything. (Laughter.) Mr. Hayes: A few years ago you granted a vote for this house to a son who has since :o Australia.

claim was adjourned in order to give Mr. 3 an opportunity of attending. T applicant attended and said lie ed a bedroom and sitting-room exclu-

TWO HOUSES, THREE SISTERS, AND A BROTHER.

Thomas Lewis Robins claimed a lodger vote in respect of 16 and 18, Thelwall-lane. Mr. Sanders said the value was very good, and this was the most convenient place where the man could live. He kept the house going, and it would be a calamity for the family if he left. Mr .Woodcock said that the two premises were separately rated and tenanted, although he understood there was internal communication between them. They were tenanted by Eliza- beth and Annie Sherlock respectively, and their gross assessable values were .r,tl4 and 11. Mr. Sanders: It is knocked into one.

Mr. Wharton said a man had to O'C-c-Upy lodg- ings in the same house for the qualifying period in order to obtain a vote. The question that now arose was whether the two houses could be taken as one for the purposes of the Act. Mr. Hayes: For the municipal vote Annie and Elizabeth Sherlock are on.

Mr. Woodcock said the claimant had a study in number 16 and a bedroom in number 18. The gross value of the two buildings was only £23, and allowance had also to be made for the fact

that one was a shop. Three sisters were living in the houses. .

M. Sanders: Although separately tenanted, the houses are joined together, and claimant has full use of both. He is an acknowledged lodger and has heel,. in the houses for years. Mr. Wharton: He is the tenant of one person for one place and tenant to another person in another place.

Mr. Sanders : a sitting-room in- one house and a bedroom, in the .other. He has. not to leave the building in order to.go from his sitting-room to the bedroom. Mr. Wharton :• To whom does he pay the rent? Mr.-Sanders :To one of the sisters. The two houses belong to one of the sister's.' He is an. 'old man and would like the vote. If he does not get it now it looked as though he would not get one, until he got to the Cemetery—(laughter) —as he, cannot obtain suitable rooms elsewhere. The meney he pays is pooled between the sisters. Mr. Wharton: It is establishing a precedent. Mr. Sanders: It is, but I think you have power to grant the man the privilege of a vote. Mr. Wharton: You say he cannot get rooms elsewhere?

Mr. Sanders: No. It is just a convenient car ride to where he works.

Mr. Hayes: He works at Pearson ind Knowles —about two miles away.

Mr. Sanders: Even if other rooms were avail- able he is so • well looked after that he would not remove.

Mr. Wharton: Well, it is worth his while to remain without a vote. I cannot allow it.

In the evening Mr. Robins said he paid .21 per week for board and lodgings to Elizabeth Sher- lock.

Mr. Wharton said it was a bit unusual, and he could not see why he did not have the sitting. room and bedroom in the same house. He should not allow the vote.

A LATCHFORD WITHOUT CLAIM.

A lodger vote was claimed by Walter Carter, clerk; Latchford Without. Mr. Joseph Smith (assistant overseer) said he was satisfied that the claimant had for over three year's had the exclusive use of a bedroom. With regard to the sitting-room, the bulk of the material in it belonged to him, and other mem- bers of the family .v.ery- rarely used it. The claimant would not, however, deprive them of access if they desired it. He did not pay a separate rent for the rooms, but gave his mother 21s d week.

Mr. Wharton: What arrangement is made with regard to this Sitting-room as to whether he can exclude the rest of the family., Mr. Smith: He is a good-natured man. Mr—Sanders said claimant had not sole use of the room.. lie was treated just as, a 8011. -His -man had visited the house an&he was told that the claimant had not the exclusive use of the sitting-room., Mr. Wharton said that the Overseer's evidence did not go far enough. There must be evidence not , only that the claimant had the right to the exclusive use of the rooms, but that he exercised it. He would adjourn the case to give claimant an opportunity of appearing. At present he was against the claim on the grounds that the value was small and that there was grave doubt on the point of exclusive use. On Tuesday Mr. Carter appeared and said he paid a certain sum for the exclusive use of the rooms. He could lock the other people out if , he wanted to.

The value was .217 10s., and, in. granting the vote, Mr. Wharton said it was very near the line.

A COMING-OF-AGE AGREEMENT.

On Monday evening a lodger claim, supported by the Conservatives, was made by Sydney Her- bert Collins, 111, Bewsey-road. The father, William Edward Collins, said claimant had the exclusive use of a sitting-room and bedroom. The parents were the only, other residents in the house, and there were two addi- tional sitting-rooms.. Mr. Wharton: Could your son turn you out of the sitting-room if he liked? Witness: Yes; he could lock the door. _

An agreement was produced for the use of the rooms. It was dated December 16th, 1912, when claimant came of age. • Mr. Sanders said that the date of the agree- ment was a fatal objection to the claim. Mr. Wharton: Yes; it is fatal. Mr. Sanders: He is not 22 until next Deeem- ber.

It was pointed out that claimant had held Ihe rooms on similar terms for over 12 months before the agreement was made, and the barrister allowed the vote. '

THE HO.RSE AND JOCKEY.

A lodger vote was claimed by the Conserva- tives for Harry Rushworth in respect of rooms at the Horse •and Jockey Hotel, of which his father is the licensee. Claimant said he had the 'excl*ve use of a bedroom and a sitting-room. - • • Mr. Sanders contended that it was impossible for the claimant to have a sitting-room to him- self. Mr. and Mrs. Rushworth, two sons, a daughter and a servant were living in the house, and, in ()icier to comply with the requirements

of the Licensing Act, he did not see how the claimant, could be given the sole use of the two

MOMS. The claimant said another sitting-room and a kitchen were available for the other members of the family. Mr. Wharton: Do They all sit in one place while you have a room to yourself? • Claimant: Not always. I ha:ve the room if I care to go into it. Mr. Wharton: Your sitting-room is not used by them? Claimant: No, sir. He added that he was a draughtsman and had nothing to do With the public-house business. The claim was allowed.

Land in the Market more Expensive than for the Dearest Shop in Town.

WHAT IS THE "REAL" RENT?

Interesting Sidelights on the Qualifications of Non-

resident Electors,

The most interesting feature in this week's Revision Court proceedings has been the claims of market stall holders, who live out of Warrington. An interesting argument took place with regard to one of the stall holders, who paid a rent of only Gs. 9d. per week. The argument against the granting of the vote was that the element of toll, extra cost of scavenging, and other charges, brought the real rent down to below the necessary £lO per annum to cover a vote. The Revising Barris- ter ruled that the real rent was inadequate, and disallowed the claim. In two cases it was urged as fatal to the claims that the occupiers of the stalls resided beyond the seven-mile limit from the borough bound- ary. It was proved that they were both 200 yards outside the area, and the votes were dis- allowed,
