===== 0002601_19130913_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DECISION.

The claim was disallowed in Millar's case. On Tuesday. the Revising Barrister gave his decision in regard to the claims. Mr. Wharton said that the vote mast be either for land or tenement, and he could say that in no sense were the stalls tenements. The question of the value of the land in. Millar's case had been *decided. * Mr; Woodcock had been able to separate the value of the. land itself from all the incidental expenses connected with the stall. He had come to the conclusion that 40 per cent. might very well be deducted in respect of certain things, such as lighting, police, scavenging, &c. He (Mr. Wharton) was not quite certain whether Mr. Woodcock had not rather over-stated it in saying 40 per cent., or whether he had taken into consideration the position of the particular plot, because unques- tionably the position was a very important ele- ment in the valuation of the land. In different parts of the borough the value of land varied so far as its position went. In the cases, except' Millar's, they found that after deducting 40 per cent: the value was considerably over ..tlO. The others; with the exception of Hart and Robin- son, qualified as regards the distance, but the two mentioned were not within the area. Whilst both satisfying the requirements. as, to Occupa- tion and value, they did not satisfy the require- ments as to distance. The Surveyor had gone into the distances very carefully and measured them on The-ordnance survey map. which was recognised as the correct way, and he said that both claimants were 200 yards out of the bound- ary. Those three would, therefore, be disallowed.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
PARISH CHURCH CURATES.

In respect. of rooms at Fairfield House, Man- chester-road, the Rev. J. :VI. Buckmaster, curate at the Parish Church, claimed a vote. He did not appear in Court. Vickers already had a lodger vote at the same house, representing £10; there was £lO to be deducted for another vote, and there was an additional lodger in the house as well as Mr. Buck Master. Mr. Hayes said the net value of the house was £3O. Mr. Buckmaster had taken the place of another clergyman, who was on the list. Mr. Sanders : I could have put in a lot of lodger claims under £2O value, but I took your

Honour's ruling,last year. The Barrister said there was only one lodger on the list for the house at present. He allowed the claim.

DISALLOWED.

Sydney W. M. Davey, living with his mother, at 5, Grappenhall-road, claimed a lodger vote. It was stated that there were two lodgers—a lady and gentleman—in the house, hut claimant had the sOle use of two rooms. There was no Par- liamentary vote in respect of the house. Mr. Sanders said it was the only place claim- ant could live in. His information was that the tvzo hoarders had their meals- together, and claimant had a room entirely to himself. Further evidence as to accommodation and value having been given, Mr. Wharton dis- allowed the claim. The value was £l4 gross.

REVISION ODDS AND ENDS.

On Monday, Tuesday and Wednesday, Mr. C. H. Marriott Wharton attended at the Town Hall to revise the voters' lists. Mr. E. A. San- ders appeared for the Liberals and Mr. H. Hayes for the Conservatives. There were 23 claims for Division I. (Parlia- mentary), two for Division 111. (municipal), and 11 lodger claims. There was not a single objection to votes on the borough list.

OMITTED AND RE-INSTATED.

Mr. Hayes said that the names of nine persons had been omitted from the list for Winwick- with-Hulme. He asked the Revising Barrister to allow the names to be re-instated, on the overseer's correction, without putting the parties to the trouble of coming three miles to Court. With the exception of one—Mr. Harold Smith—both parties were agreeable. Mr. Wharton: I suppose I have power to do it? • _ _ _ _

Mr. Hayes: With the exception of Harold Smith, who will have to come to Court. Mr. Wharton: Who represents Harold Smith? (Laughter.) Mr. Hayes: He has had notice to appear. hut with that exception they are perfectly right. Mr. Wharton: They are allowed.

Claimant said his sister and he held the house under a joint tenancy, and they had an agree- ment for five years. The claim was allowed.

NOT QUITE IN ORDER.

George Henry Toft, 31, Arpley-street. made a claim for a Parliamentary Tote as tenant of the house. _

Claimant said that he had been flaying the rates since his return, two years ago. but he had not called the attention of the officials to the fart until August of last year. Mr. Wharton disallowed the claim. UNUSUAL BUT REGULAR.

In respect of his tenancy of the house 1, bramhell-street, Cuthbert • Sydney Highley claimed a Parliamentary vote. SALVATION ARMY CAPTAIN SUCCeEDS. William Thonias ,Fenwick, a Salvation Army officer, claimed to be placed on Division I. (Par- liamentary and municipal) in respect of his occupation of 25, Froghall-lane. In supporting the claim, Mr. Sanders said Mr. Fenwick was a captain of the Salvation Army, stationed in .Warrington. The Army paid the rent of the house, and the question was whether he should be on the list, as occupier or tenant. He was not compelled by the Army authorities to live in any particular house.

Mr. Woodcock said Mr. Fenwick's name had been left off because he was not the tenant. The Salvation Army was the tenant, but he saw no reason why claimant should not be on Division 11. (Parliamentary) as service voter. When claimant had selected a .house that was suitable to him, the Army paid the rent.

Mr. Hayes: The person who pays the rent is the tenant. It was stated that Mr. Fenwick could Aot attend to support the claim, as he was a patient in a hospital. Mr. Wharton allowed the claim for Division 11., not for Division I.

_ Mr. Hayes: I contend that the claim is bad in ;hat case. He claimed for Division I.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
THANKING THE BARRISTER.

Compliments for Mr.' Woodcock and his

UNEQUALLED WARRINGTON REGISTER.

At the completion of the business on Wednes- day, a vote of thanks was accorded the Revising Barrister. In proposing the vote, Mr. Hayes commented upon the kind way in which Mr. Wharton had carried out his work. He noticed that the work became lighter every year, and this was due to the excellent work done by Mr. Woodcock and his staff. They ought to be complimented on the excellent manner in which they had pre- pared the list, which reflected great credit upon them. They were also obliged to the Revising

Barrister for the assistance he had given them

as agents. Mr. Sanders seconded, and endorsed all Mr. Hayes had said respecting the Barrister and Mr. Woodcock. In many cases, where they had tried- to push- for-claims, the Revising Barrister had strictly adhered to the law, and had allowed them more latitude in\ some of the cases which had been brought forward. He was deeply in- debted to Mr. Woodcock and his staff for what they had done. The work was lightened very 'considerably by having such men in charge of the revision list. He did not care what the Government did, but he was sure that it would be a great loss when ho had to stop coming to Warrington as the Revising Barrister. Person- ally, he would miss him very much. When he (Mr. Sanders) first went to the Court he felt he would be dreadfully in the. way. On occasions when be had conic prepared in a law, case to prove his point, the Revising Barrister knew the law so well that he had never had the op- portunity of making a speech.

MAKING THE REGISTER "PURE." Mr. Wharton thanked the agents for the kind- ness they had extended to him. He could not be too grateful to Mr. Woodcock and his staff' for the excellent way in which the lists were put together. fie did not know any place where it was done so thoroughly. and correctly. All he had to do was to satisfy himself in dealing with matters which might be in dispute between. the parties. He had satisfied himself that he had acted justly and held the balance evenly between the parties. He had done the work for a few years, and he was not aware of a single instance where a party agent could say that he _ _ _ _ .

had acted otherwise than perfectly fair. If, of course, the law was not agreeable to one side or the other, it was not his fault. He had to administer it as it stood. In the old days a great deal bf 'give-and-take was allowed, and a food deal more might be. said. There was not 'quite "tossing up," hut settling matters between themselves. He did not approve of such a system. It should-be-treated as a serious matter: • Every name that was wrong should come off the list, and every name which should be on, should be included in the list. His ten- dency was to put names on, rather than take them off. He had never allowed any question of mere sympathy or sentiment or anything of that kind to affect him One iota. He had en- d

_ eavoured to get a pure register, and having such assistants as Mr. Woodcock, he was satis- fied that there was no register anywhere equal to that for Warrington. They had settled the question of lodger franchise, and he did not come to a conclusion without careful considera- tion of the law. He had gone through the whole arguments used in the House of Commons when the matter was brought before the House, to see whether he could gain any information from those arguments based upon the careful reading of the Act. He was satisfied tha the system he adopted was strictly right. When first he came to Warrington he insisted on strictly legal facts being given. He had had many objections to the attitude he took, but if they would look at the expressions of opinions by judges, they would find that in almost e.very case they had borne out the prinoiple he adopted, viz., that legal evidence must be used, and no question of sentiment allowed to enter into it. He only hoped that wherever he went he would meet with the same kindness -which was extended to him in Warrington. It was very seldom there was any trouble at all. Al- _ _

_ though. he had a little friction at first, till the people got used to him, he could say that there was practically none of it now. In the Leigh Division he had the assistance of men like Mr. Woodcock, and if one could guarantee :to get such assistants his business would be reduced to deciding one or two points of law, and going through the obnoxious praetice of stamping papers like a machine. He thought that might be left to someone else to do. He supposed there must be some solemnity attached to mark . _

_ ..-- ing off the list, and if it was not carefully done by a, barrister, it might be looked upon as an irresponsible thing. It ought to be done care- fully. He was not at all certain, if they came to deal with the whole question. whether a Court to deal solely with the questions of law, might be established, which would take in the whole of the county, or several counties, and that the mechanical work could be done out- side the Court. He thought there was no doubt that they would meet next year, if they were alive.

THE ADULT SCHOOL.

Annual Field Day Proves a Most Interesting

Function.

PLETHORA OF ENTERTAINkiENT.

The members of the four Warrington' Adult Schools gathered under ideal summer skies for their annual outing on Saturday, at Shirley, Hill Cliffe, the residence of their popular Presi- dent, Mr. John A. Ransome. Assembling in the large field, they.soon, found that thoughtful preparations had been made for their enjoy- ment, and. it was not long before the hundred and fifty members present were absorbed in the fun.

The games were of sufficient variety to satisfy young and old. The more vigorous had the choice of tether ball and quoits, and those who desired something quieter had clock golf, ladder golf and skittles to pick from, but the bowling green was the favourite spot. In a corner of the field a hoop-la stand did good business, the prizes varying from the penny packet of "Wood- bines " to the handy brush and-comb. ~.'s

Not Quite So Simple.

Adjoining this was a game not seen often in these parts, " Disco " by name. It consists of a: large card, with a black circle on it, and five metal discs; the problem is to cover the circle wit Ii the .discs. U looks so alluringly' simple, but repeated tries at a penny a time soon taught' many that it is not so easy as it rooks. It. added considerably to the funds. At four o'clock the first half of the sports; under the able command\ of Mr. Frank H. Knight, of York, took place. After an hour's exhilarating and exciting fun and contest, the tea bell rang. Alnost excellent al fresco repast was served by the Atkinson Cafe Company. The next hour or two were spent in more leis- urely ways, nothing more exciting than a smoke, a chat, " Disco " and "I -loop-la."

The Sports And Results.

The finals in the sports proided many excit- ing moments, each school cheering and vocifer- ously encouraging their own champions left in the races. The results were as follows-:.—

Potato Race.-1, Mrs. E. Schofield, Sankey Bridges; 2, Miss Paul, Bewsey. Three-legged Rare.-1, Messrs. Mather and Boucher, Bewsey ; 2, Messrs. A. Morrison and T. IstierwoOd, Stockton Heath. Threading Needle Race.-1, Mrs. E. Schofield, Sankey Bridges; 2, Mrs. T. Isherwood, Stock- ton Heath.

Jockey Race.-1, L. Elwell (horse) and E. El- well (jockey), simkey Bridges; 2, L. Forster (horse) and W. Turner (jockey), Bewsey. Ladies' Team Race.-1, Sankey 'Bridges Women's School ; 2, Bewsey Women's School. Flower Pot Race.-1, Mr. T. Isherwood, Stock- ton Heath; 2, Mr. F. Lynch, Stockton Heath. Tug-of-War.-4, Stockton Heath Men's School; 2, Academy-street Men's School.

„ At the close the prizes were distributed by Mrs. RansoMe. A very hearty vote of thanks to Mr. and Mrs. Ransome and Mr. Iknight was carried with acclamation. Three rousing cheers for the host and hostess for their kindness closed one of the happiest days in the history, of the Union. .

HALLETT, io, BRIDGE ST., Warrington.

llokT THE FARMERS HAVE BEEN "SOLD."

The Tariff fieformers are trying very hard to induce the British agriculturist to accept a sub-

stitution for the Protectionist policy of food taxes. After ten years of noisy advocacy of that policy they have now. temporarily dropped it. The sorry story was told at length in the July issue of the Free Trader." The repre- sentative of the agricultural constituency of liorncastle, Captain A. G. Weigall, M.P., writes to the "Lincolnshire Standard" (August 16), to complain that the Lincolnshire Farmers' Union, "in their attitude towards the present positiOn of Fiscal Reform as outlined by Mr. Bonar Law in his -Edinburgh speech, are straining at a gnat to swallow a camel." After all, farmers must belong to one party or the 'other, he thinks. That is quite true. But it does not alter the farmers' disgust with a party that has made him a long series of glowing promises-- 6s. an acre 'tore profit, and soon, and has now withdrawn those promises because the country has set its face strongly against anything that involves a tax on food. Captain Weigall spends a good deiil of. his strength in describing the alternative agricultural policy of the Unionist party. With that- the "Free Trader" has nothing to slo, except to draw attention again and again to the fact that the more the Tariff Reformers talk about it the oftener do they emphasise the fact that they are trying to creep into power by pretending to have for- saken the very policy- which for ten' years they promised to carry into effect to " save " agricul- ture. At the present time agriculture 's busy saving-itself; without help •of any kind from weathercock Tariff Reformers.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
LORD NVOLNIER AND MR. LLOYD

GEORGE.

Lord Wolmer, at a recent meeting at New- ton, stated that Mr. Lloyd George had been "detected in • a. transaction which, --I venture to.say; no Chancellor of the Exchequer that has evel• ,sat ,in . Downing-street; has ever been guilty- of—that, is-to say, accepting a tip from a Government contractor out of which he might have made £30,000, and the only reason why he did not was that he wanted to make more, and held' on too -long." When Mr. Lloyd George 'called Lord Wolmer'S attention to me untruthfulness of the. ,statement—taking the facts, apart from the opinion—Lord Wolmer said he had been wrongly reported, that he had not referred to Mr. Lloyd George singly, but said "he and his friends accepted a tip from a Government contractor, out of which they might have made £30,000, and the only reason wriy, they did not make anything was that they wanted to make more and held on too long." The reporters thus meanly attacked are unani- mous in stating that Lord Wolmer used the singular. and not the plural pronoun, and that only. Mr. Lloyd George was mentioned. Now Lord Wormer says it does not '.matter about the "amount.," the point at issue being whether it is right for a Chancellor, and so on. There is a'moie important question, so far.as we are concerned, than the point, at issue be- tween Lord Wolmer and the Chancellor. It is the readiness with which sortie people are ever ready, to absolve themselves from blame'. by casting aspersions upon the professional ability of those who report their utterances. Had tord Wollner been able to prove the truth of his statement that a certain reporter had been so incompetent as his statement alleged, that reporter might have been seriously dealt with; but fortunately three men had -taken the same' note and had made the same transcript. At a meeting of the National Union of Journalists, A to be held at Manchester to-day, the following resolution will be moved :

That the Manchester branch requests the sub-committee ef the Executive of the National Union to make full inquiry into the particulars of the charge brought by Lord Wolmer against the journalists who reported his meeting at Newton-le-Willows of having distorted his speech, with a view of obtaining from Lord Wolmer a retraction of this charge and, if necessary, an apology.

The public, as well as the journalistic pro. lession; will await the outcome of this action with keen interest.

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 13, 19131

1

f'

MR. BALFOUR'S ADMISSIONS.

Mr. BALFOUR, in his Haddington speech, made, by implication at any rate, some admissions of the greatest importance.. There Was the impliCit admission that at 'the last 'General Election the majority of the voters at least acquieked in the proposal to give Ireland Home Rule. "1 am afraid;" said Mr. BALFOUR, "that many of the.constituencies in England and Scotland supposed that no doubt Ulster was Unionist, but that Ulster Unionism was not of that type which- regarded connection with this country. and freedom from the domination of the -rest of Ireland as a matter of .life.and death to them." There was the implicit admission that the British electorate is still disinclined to take the-Tory view of the Home Rule question, and that it has no desire for another General Election on a question whictr, it regards as settled. Such a demhnd, Mr. .BALFOUR said; could only be provoked "if we, the minority, stir the somewhat sluggish imaginations of our fellow-countrymen." And there was the implicit admission that there is a real Home Rule question, that "the traditions of British liberty" do not recognise any government as satisfactory which is not government by con- sent, and that, on the other hand, serious dis- content is one of, those "elemental political forces" which statesmanship ,cannot ignore. Mr. Baron:, therefore, has pronounced a final condemnation on the policy of coercion and a. "Protestant garrison" with which he, , once identified his party, .and when he recognises 'elemental political forces" he cannot Pretend to believe, in any solution of the Irish problem which would bow to the claims .of a _small minority and oppose nothing but coercion to the claims of an oirerwhelming majority. If Mt. BALFOUR'S implicit admissions are impor- tant, one .direct avowal is not less .important. Even if the Liberal Government•weretci go to a superfluous election on the Home Rule question, "I do not say," Mr. BALFOUR exclaims, "that they would reconcile Ulster." What, then, is the Tory position? It is one which involves a flagrant injustice to those Ulster Unionists whose cause the Tories pretend to .be earnestly championing. •

It is hardly necessary even to touch upon the insinuation, now rather stale but .revived by Mr. BALFOUR, that the Liberal Government con- spired to reduce the House of Lords to a state .of suspension in order to smuggle through Home Rule. The reformed Second Chamber,

to the principle of which/ the Liberal Govern- ment is .pledged, Would not be giyen .back the powers of. veto of the unreformed House of -Lords. • It would be a real revising Chamber, 'giving ascair a chance to Liberal as to Tory legislation.' But when Mr. BALFOUR pretends that the Liberal GoVermnent .would lose noth- ing:by submitting to another General Election on Home Rule, the plea throws such a light upon the extraordinary 'atmosphere in which Tor2„-isin lives and moves that it cannot be,dis- missed so summarily. Mr. BALFOUR actually thinks—and is probably quite sincere in the statement of his case--that the Liberal Govern- ment is both "imprudent and immoral" in not dissolving whenever the Tory party calls for a dissolution. A Liberal Government which was so daunted by Tory attacks that it consented to have General Election after General Election .imply because the Tory party thought that the electors were "sluggish," would deserve to be banished from power for ever. . That is what the Liberals would lose if tiv allowed their ,oppqnents to become judges of NNAlether any appeal to the nation had been sufficient. 'What would the Liberals gain by another General Election? Let us suppose an overwhelming verdict for Home Buie. What would the Lords do then? Lord Curzon has told us. , They would proceed to make the Home Rule Bill more "palatable."

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER is, 1913. THE PASSING WEEK.

WHAT WARRINGTON NEEDS.

There are many things that Warrington needs,. but the most pressing need is that our atmosphere should be cleansed of all super- fluous smoke, and its other impurities. Great advances have been nuide in many directions of late. Our streets have been, are being, and will be widened; we shall have, in the course of time, One of the noblest bridges in the country,; our insanitary areas are being tackled to some effect; and such steps have been taken that ill-health in many forms may be success- fully combated. Altogether such strides have been made in the work of improving the town in appearance that it really ranks high now among the towns of the North of England. If the reader wishes to know what Warrington might look like let him note its appearance,op Sundays, when the works are at .rest and the sun is not obscured by the smoke that ,is sent forth by the many chinmeys on week-days. Last Sunday, for instance, was a fine, sunny day, and the blue sky that snliled upon us would have done credit to Switzerland. The air was ..clear and pure, and one felt the joy of living even when walking through the streets of Warrington in its More crowded parts. The problem with which we are faced is whether we can lessen our output of smoke, and so brighten our lives without doing an injury to the industries by which we live. Our conten- tion is that we can, and that we ought to make the effort.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FACTS OF THE CASE.

There. has been a ,deal of loose talk Upon this vital question, and, to Tut the discussion on something like a scientific foundation,•we have sought the services of experts, and. to-day give, on our third page, the results of observations made by a smoke inspector, along with com- ments on that expert's findings made by Mr. E. D. Simon, the chairman of the Smoke Abatement League of Great Britain. The latter gentleman is a -prominent citizen of Man- chester, a member of the City Town Council, and the head of a large mercantile house. He has had long exPeiielice Of the Smoke'question and thoroughly understands both the possi- bilities and difficulties of smoke' abatement from the manufacturer's point of view. He speaks, indeed, with the. authority of a practical man, whose desire for reform is tempered by sober judgment and practical knowledge. What do these experts say? In brief that Warrington smoke, which puts us 'occasionally into the blackness of night at mid-day, is worse than the smoke which the worst neighbour- hood of Manchester can produce; that this can be remedied, as it has been remedied at Man- chester; and, most important of all, that by far the greater proportion of Ihe smoke now produced 'could be avoided, without inflicting any hardships on manufacturers.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR AIMS.

What we desire to do is to set the manufac- turers and the Health Committee to think whether they. can produce a scheme which , would reduce the smoke turned out to a minimum. Before publishing the list of obser- vations 'which the inspector has furnished which appears on our third page, we forwarded a copy to each of the firms mentioned. One firm write to say that before making any com- ment they would•desire "to understand clearly the motive -which has prompted 4he-investiga- lion:" The motive appears on the face of our report, which we hope will be carefully studiad by-the ;whole town. It is, of ceurse, simply to secure a purer atmosphere than the one we are at present getting. Another firm write to say that they believe none of their chimneys come under the operation of the Act, iron and steel works generally being at the time specially exempted. One firm, Messrs. W. D. Houghton, express surprise that they are mentioned, see- ing that they are not in the borough. When this question is being discussed the vital point is always, or nearly always, missed. What all ought to be concerned about is. Can the air we breathe be made purer by the aid that careandscbieenceeffeccatnedsupply, and can the im- provementprospepity of, the works upon which the town depends for its livelihood? We refer Sour readers to the reports on the subject we -pro- Vide this week, which take a decidedly affirma- tive attitude.

REVISING THE VOTERS' . LIST.

The Revision Court this -week . was a very harmonious affair, the party agents; as well as the' Barrister, relying very largely indeed upon the opinion of Mr. W. 11. Woodcock, the assist- ant 'overseer, who appears to have so perfected the list that the Court has become more or less of a formality: The .most interesting cases that came before the Court were the. claims of several stallholders in the Market, who live outside the borough boundary, but claim votes in respect of the stalls they rent from the Cor- poration. Two of the applicants were ruled IO live 200 yards further from the boundary than the seven miles limit allows, and their claims were not allowed. All the others, with one ex- ception, were granted. The exception was a tenant who paid only Gs. 9d. a week rent. This amount in ordinary conditions would much more than cover the ,tlO annual value neces- sary to qualify, but. Mr. Woodcock pointed out that the amount represented £2 12s. per square yard of land occupied per annum, which obvi- ously included much besides real rent. No shop ,in the best position in the town pays any- thing like this, and Mr. Woodcock's contention was that the ,element of toll, the charges for, policing and scavenging, and other extra- ordinary, .services,..redUced. the actual rent tci below the. necessary amount. The .Barrister agreed and disallowed the vote. It was stated .during, the hearing of, the case that last year .the profits made by the Corporation out of the Market amounted to £1,720, and the suggestion that thiS' proved that the bulk of the tnoney taken was profit and did not represent expenses did not save the vote.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
LOGDERS IN THEIR OWN HOMES.

If any one thing in the registration laws cries aloud for, their reform, it is the way in which they allow for the admission of lodgers to the privileges of the -franchise. Something like a sensible arrangement can be made in respect of men who lodge in other people's houses, though even then curious questions of value arise; but we,are in a perfect maze,when we come to deal with young gentlemen who are lodgers in their own homes. Injustices and anomalies come along in quick succession in these cases. One -surprising result of the ability to claim a vote in the house of one's father is that so many sons have acquired the right to turn their par- ents out of their own sitting-rooms. "Could your son turn you out of the sitting-room, if he liked?" asked. the Barrister of a father, who was supporting his son's claim. "Yes," was the reply, "he could lock the door." "Granted," Said the Barrister.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====

Promised electoral reform was prObably in the mind of the Revising Barrister when, in thanking Mr. Woodcock for his valuable assist- ance in making the Warrington list one of the purest in existence, he expressed the belief that they would, if all were wen, meet again next year.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
THE JUNIOR LIBERALS.

The Junior Liberals are to be congratulated on the unqualified success that attended their first garden fete, which was held last Saturday in the grounds of Captain Crosfield, Tower House, Lodge-lane. Not only was the attend- ance more than double the number anticipated, but the speeches, without any outside aid, were much above the average in point of ability ,and variety. The happy rural scenes amid which the fete was held on a glorious afternoon in early September, inspired the Chairman, Mr. John Griffiths, to draw a picture of the pros- perity, industrial and agricultural, that has waited upon this country during the seven years the present Government have been in office. There may be very little in the point, but it is a glaring contradiction of that cheap criticism which never tires of telling us that when the Liberals are " iit" we have 'bad times, bad harvests, bad evefything." The Junior Liberals have a bright future before them.

Last night's meeting in the Co-operative flail was well attended and was most enthusiastic.

OUR GAS UNDERTARING.

As a result of the discussion in the' Council last week, there can be little doubt that " cheaper gas" will be supplied to the con- 4utners. During the, course of that discussion, Councillor Peacock (we believe) hinted that it

alight be wise to..make.changes in the adminis- tration of this department. For °some years it has been obvious to business men in the town that it is an exPensive " anomaly " to maintain the Mersey-street office; and that the. greater .portion of the work. shouliie. carried on. at the Sankey-streeV-offices. Experts have gone So far as to-staterthat -if-saving of, from £5OO lo A:750 a year coulii. easily be effected if this were done. it has been a matter" of surprise Why this' has not been .carried out, years ago. We have no hesitation in affirming that if this matter were in the hands of a private firm it would at 'once be carried. out: Not only would a real "economy" be effected, but the con- venience of the public would be served as well, for the Sankey-street offices are much more central than are the Mersey-street offices.

PROSPEROUS PEARSON AND KNOWLES.

As will be seen by the official dividend an- nouncement, which, by the courtesy of the firm, we publish in our local news, the Pearson and. Knowles ,Coal and Iron Company; Ltd., have had another prosperous year—indeed, we should have to go back some way to find a better year, judged by the dividends to be paid, than the one which ended on the Kith of June. For immediate purposes the substantial divi- dends that go to the -preference and- second preference shares may be forgotten, as the pay- ment in -their case is fixed. A better index of the business done during the year is found in other items. For. the last half-year a dividend on the ordinary shares of ten per cent, is recom- mended by the directors. The dividend on the ordinary shares for the preceding half-year was six per cent., so that for the whole year the dividend paid to, ordinary shareholders will be eight per cent., which shows not only that trade is brisk, but that, profits are good.. Nor. is .this.satisfactory dividend all. A sound policy over a course of years has induced the directors to pay off all debenture and similar, charges, and there is. a reserve fund which last year stood at- the Substantial 'figure -of -X352,500, after haying had £l2,393:added to it out of that year's trading. This year another £30,000 is to be added- to the reServes, which will' Men stand -at £382,500, a .ooWing "SUM, which is. Well on the way to the half-million point. It. would be un- fair further to anticipate, the interesting state- ment Sir J. Harmood Banner, M.P., will have to make to the forthcoming meeting of the corn-. pany ; but we may add that last year the divi- dend on the ordinary shares was five per cent., which -absorbed £5O/755. We cangratulate the firm on its continued success, and the town upon having so beneficial an undertayng in its midst. As the Chairman often points• out, the wages bill—not all paid in Warrington, of course—is of huge dimensions, and huge wages bills are among the best products of a manu- facturing town.

STILL BOOMING.

Our trade is still booming, and we seem well assured of still one more " record " year. The "Sunday Chronicle," which, in the main, thinks more of sport than politics, welcomed the ad- vent or the football .season with a splendid cartoon headed- ." The Old Champion- again." The old champion was no less a personage than John Bull, who was depicted in the act of kick- ing a goal, the .huge ASsociation. ball he was directing to the net being labelled "British Trade, 1913." Along the cross-bar ran the legend "Highest \ Record," and imps called "Cost of Living," ".Cotton Trade Boom End- ing," and "Labour Unrest," were/ grimly de- fending the goal. ". The descriptive verses by " DemocrittA " went through the various panic cries of blight, ruin and death, but, pointing to John Bull and the British Trade ball at his feet, they concluded :

Witli strength in eternal revival Behold him, the ball at his feet, The player whom ,every rival Strives vainly to beat.

Once more, a redoubtable figure, He toils at his wonderful trick, Although every year tbe ball's bigger And harder to kick.

Black imps rise to scare and repel him, Weird pessimist threnodies roll, But no one on earth can excel him, He'll get through the goal.

The Weary Willies and Tired Tims of the town have been described in many ways, but a somewhat novel description of one of these gentry was given by the Chief Constable at the Police Court on Saturday, when he described a prisoner as "one of those who go the farthest way to work and the nearest way from it."

Liberty's Claim. A claimant at the Revision Court bore the name of Liberty. Possibly he will vote- for the party which believes, in Liberty for. the People.

Thought it was the Lock-up. The Liberal Agent told the Warrington Re- vising Barrister that a lady claimant had said she was too nervous to attend the Court. She. thought it was the lock-UP.

Proof Positive. A woman who gougUt a municipal vote at the Revision Court was asked why? She replied that she knew her Msband died 'seven years ago. She got it.

THE Harold.

"Harold Smith; who represents Harold Smith?" asked the Revising Barrister at the Warrington Revision Court. The gentleman was brought .in, and he asked for a vote, but he was not THE Harold Smith.

Not Just Yet.

"A General Election Inevitable," said a "Daily Mail' bill displayed in Warrington. Of course, everybody could prophesy similarly, but itis n6t coming just yet. Perhaps two ‘years 'hence, .

"A Roaring Lion." "You were like a roaring lion, seeking whom you might devour," quoth a Warrington Police Court witness about a prosecutrix. If the wit- ness had said lioness she would have hit the mark, but she was an Irishwoman, and made an unconscious " bull " about a lion.

He Might. "Can I take this tram to Latchford?" asked a visitor to Warrington of a tram conductor. The ticket_ puncher was in a facetious humour, and retorted: "It weighs three tons; and if you tan carry it you might try to take it, but, per- haps, it would be best to let the tram'take you."

"Mrs.', Bangs has the worst habit." "What is it, dear?" "She turns around and looks back every time we pass in the street." "How do you know she does?"

The Lawyer. "And now I mean to handle your witnesses without gloves," said a counsel whose witnesses had met with rather severe treatment froth the other side.

" Indeed ! That's more than I should like to do with yours," smilingly retorted his learned friend.

Work for a Good Lawyer.

Why are you so sad?" an acquaintance asked a young man whose aunt had just died. "You never appeared to care much for the poor lady.' . "I didn't:" said the yoUth dolefully.;, "but I was the means of keeping her in a lunatic asylum during the last five years of her life. She has left me all her money, and now I've got to prove that she was of sound mind."

Must Promise Not to Marry. Hereafter any girl who goes to Panama to be a stenographer, for one of the companies working there, must sign an agreement not to get married for five years. Three of the steno- graphers the company has-brought down there have married supervising engineers, four have married foremen, one eloped with a native, and others have returned homesick. Large salaries are offered if the promise will be made.

How Prizes Are Won.

The village gossips were discussing prospects of prize-winning at the approaching flower show. . One rather fancied, that Bill Smith would do well, because Bill's brother-in-law was employed at the Hall gardens; Toni Brown seemed a probable winner, too, having been seen "in the nursery-gardening district of Che- shire at early morning, and Joe Robinson was notoriously friendly with a certain noted grower who was not showing this year. • When all, or nearly. all; had thus "spotted the will- ners," an ancient who had not yet spokeni re- marked: " Yo're aw rung; none of them's got a. chance. Capital to be Increased Shortly to £1,300,000.

GOOD TIMES AHEAD.

Next Friday a meeting of the shareholders of the firm of Messrs. J. Crosfleld and Sons, Ltd., will be held to cpnfirm an arrangement that has been found necessary to increase the capital to £1,300,000 to meet the increasing business that is being done by the firm. On the Lancashire side of the river every available inch of land has bjen occupied, and over the river, on the Cheshire side, the new part of the works has bee 4 growing rapidly. Almost every department—soap, oil, glycer- ine, &c.—has contributed to the need for these extensions on a lavish scale, and there is every promise that, the growth will continue much to the advantage of the town. One of the principal causes for the expansion is the working of the process for hardening soft oils, particularly fish pHs, to make them suitable for the purposes of soap manufacture. The pre-Preference and Preference .RlO shares are being sub-divided into £1 shares, and 250,000 ,t 1 six per cent. cumulative prefer- ence shares at 21s. each will be issued, but will be confined to, existing shareholders in Cros- field and Sons, and Brunner, Mond and Co, land to the customers of Crosfield and Sons.

Messrs. Brunner, Mond and c0.,-who are en- gaged in a big extension, of their Wirmington Works, own all the ordinary shares of Cros- field and Sons, and they intend to take all the shares of the new issue not ptherwise applied for. Messrs. Crosfield's shareholders are authorising the increase of the nominal capital to £1,300,000, of which' 400,000 pre-Preference and 300,000 ordinary shares have been issued. The 250,000 cumulative preference shares men- tioned above are part of an issue of <R500,000.

OUR ART GALLERY.

Can it be Made More Worthy of the Town?

COUNCILLOR BIRTLES' SUGGESTION.

Councillor I3irtles, who recently visited Hull as a Warrington delegate to the meetings of the Museums Association, made an interesting report on his return.

While in Hull he visited the new Art Gallery 'there, and was much impressed by the perfect state of • the institution. There is a gallery of fine proportions, with a choice collection of paintings .and drawings on a limited scale. These choice works are the gift of one citizen of Hull, and one of its members of Parliament. He takes a great interest in the art collection of the town, and out of his generosity the city of Hull have got together he rich collection under its dome.

"I am told," says Councillor Dirties, "that on the completion of this new gallery, he was so pleased with the effort put forth, that to assist in this good work he would give £l,OOO per annum for five years. for the purchase of

pictures. 'Three years have gone, and he has made a further promise to continue this gift for another five years, making it £lO,OOO. What this will mean to the people of Httll as a per- manent collection of pictures, and tfm pleasure .to the multitudes of 'peOple visiting the gallery, one cannot measure."

Last week', in the Town Council, Councillor flirtles expressed a hope that there might be in our midst a gentleman or gentlemen who would show the same public spirit as the citizen of Hull, to whom he had referred. In that case we could have an uncrowded Museum and a' picture gallery, in which we should be able largely to substitute originals for the many copies that now adorn the walls of the art room at the Museum.

MISS HELEN M. L. TAYLOH-MR. T. J.

110PKINS.

A pretty wedding was solemnised at War- rington Parish Church on Thursday. The bride was Miss Helen Maud Leese Taylor, of 33, Legh-street, and daughter of Mr. James Taylor, Newcastle-under-Lyne. The ,bridegroom was Mr. Thomas, James HOpkins, son of the lare Mr. Edwin Hopkins, and of Mrs. Hopkins, Manchester-road, Warrington. The officiating clergyman was the Bev: J. M. Buckmaster, and the organist, Mr. F. M. Darby, during the ceremony played appropriate music including the Wedding March from " Lohen- grin," and Mendelssohn's Wedding March. The bride, who was given away by her father, looked very pretty in a dress of white satin charmeuse, trimmed with Brussels point lace. She wore a wreath of orange blossom, myrtle .and silver leaves; and a Brussels net veil. The bridal flowers were a sheaf of lilies and maidenhair ferns. The bride's jewellery consisted of a gold turquoise and pearl pendant, the gift of Mrs. Hartley, Skipton. Three brideSmaids..•and a page-were in attend- ance upon the 'bride. The bridesmaids • were Miss Edith Taylor (sister Of the -bride); MiSs Lizzie Hopkins (sister of the bridegroom), and .a smart young lady, Miss Tiny ,Eyrl Astbury Booth. The page was Master Jacky Hopkins. The bridesmaids were prettily attired in shell- pink coloured troubadour silk crepe dresseS, trimmed with shadow net, passementery orna- ments .and pink silk sashes. The flowers of the two elder bridesmaids were bouquets of pale pink carnations and white h'eather, while the young bridesmaid and the page carried baskets of flowers. The bridesmaids wore Pearl and tourmaline brooches, the gifts Of the bridegroom.

The duties of best man were ably performed by Mr. William J. Hopkins, cousin of the bride- groom, and those of groomsman by Mr. Rpbert Eastwood, of Blackburn.

A reception was given at the King's Cafe, and later Mr. and Mrs. Hopkins left for Aberyst- wyth, where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride's travelling dress consisted of powder blue, with a pale, blue chiffon hat, trimmed with Honiton lace and pale blue .lancer plume. The presents, which were numerous and costly; included a silver tea service from the- chairman and directors of the Harrington Cafes, Ltd., and a tea -service from the staff of I,be King's Cafe.

Typewriter for the Infirmary.

The Secretary gratefully acknowledges the gift of a No. 7 Model Remington typewriter from Messrs. Rylands Bros., Ltd.

Progressive Labour League. The speaker at the open-air meeting at Bridge Foot on Monday evening will be Mr. R. Blake, a the Land Reform Council.

Evening Schools Re-opened. The evening continuation schools were re- opened on Monday night, when a large number of students enrolled.

The Borough Gymnasium. The Warrington Borough Gymnasium re- opens for the winter season on Monday. Special classes are being formed for men, women, girls and boys.

Salmon in the Mersey. A 1241 b. salmon has been caught in the Mersey at Widnes. We may yet have salnion fishing in the Mersey •at Warrington—if we wait long enough until the stream is less polluted.

nig Worship the Mayor (Dr. Joseph) has gone to Colwyn Bay for a short holiday. Urgent business connected with the office will be at- tended, to by the Deputy-Mayor, Alderman Forshaw.

Councillor Skelland.

. Councillor Skelland, who has just benefited by a short holiday on the East Coast, did not forget the poor children while he was away. Ue brought back with him two gross .(of sticks) of Tynemouth rock, which were much appreci- ated by the children, at the Ragged 'School.

Cross-Country Running. The Warrington cross-country runners are preparing for the season, and the club has several vacancies for new members, who should join at once. The West Lancashine Cross- Country Association meet at Liverpool on Mon- day to discuss the season's programme.

Golfer Kills a Swallow.

An unusual episode happened on the Appleton Golf Links on Thursday afternoon. Mr. Walter Dutton, who is a member- of the club, was driving from the second tee, when the ball struck a swallow, "killing it immediately. Even after the ball struck the bird it travelled a dis- tance of about GO yards.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
Illness of the Rector's Sister.

Much sympathy will be extended to the Rector. (the Rev. Canon Willis) in his anxiety occa- sioned by the serious illness of his sister, Miss Emily Willis, sof Sandfield, Halsnead, Prescot. Miss Willis returned to her home a fortnight ago after a sojourn at Bournemouth, but had almost immediately to take to her bed. Her many friends in Warrington wish her a speedy return to health.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
M&lical Congress and rsperanto.

The 17th international medical congress was of particular interest, because Esperanto, writes the secretary of the Warrington Esperanto Society, was introduced as the means of inter- communication between the different nationali- ties. The Esperanto section met on Friday, and was attended by Esperantist doctors of differ- ent nationalities, and as non-Esperantist doctors were also present, occasion ,was taken of this for the several doctors to speak each in his own tongue, advocating Esperanto.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
Warrington Jockey Injured.

Steve Donoghue, the Warrington jockey, sus- tained injuries whilst riding in the first race (Pork Hill Stakes) at' Doncaster yesterday Donoghue was riding Lord Falmouth's filly, Isolde, and after 'going a quarter of a mile the animal Stumbled, 'and the rider was shot over the rails. On medical examination it was found that the Warringtonian was suffering from shock, slight concussion, bruised ribs and a lacerated foot. His condition is not critical.

Still Without Insurance Doctors-. There are still a number of persons in War- , rington, we are informed by the Clerk to the Insurance Committee, who came into benefit before 30th June last, and who have not yet exercised their choice of a doctor. They cannot now make their own choice, but if they apply early to Mr. Tilling, the clerk, at the Insurance Office, Trinity Chambers, Market Gate, they can still have a limited choice from eight or nine panel doctors, and unless they make imme- diate application they will be allocated to a. doctor by the Insurance Committee. The per- sons who kave recently come into insurance can have choice of any of the 25 doctors on the panel, provided the doctors are willing to accept them.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
P.S.A. at Picture_ Palace.

- - - In the Picturfe Palace, Sankey-street, on Sunday, a very successful service was held in connection with the Warrington- P.S.A. The President (Mr. Nelson) was in the • chair, and spoke on the value of music and its effect on the minds of the people. . He showed how a good tune, well sung, had a" tendency to give a Spiritual tone, tO a meeting, and: often help to lift a , man's thoughts to A higher plape of. joy and _happiness. The address was delivered by Mr. William Swain, who took for his subject "The Bible' as , a guide!' He compared the Bible to a Brad- shaw. A man, he said, hears-of a good situation in a distant town,- but, having no guide, he goes too late and misses the post. In order to travel with safety we must carry our Christian Brad- shaw with us. There are many trains running into the same station; we .must be careful to board the right trains, And see that Christ is the driver. We must avoid trains bearing the names of worldly matters.' such as dishonesty; drink, &c. If we board the right train it will save us from many of the pitfalls that lead on to ruin and destruction. The lesson was read by Mr. Clegg, and the prayer was offered by Mr. Minshull. Mr. Richard .-Lee will be the Speaker to-morrow.

Warringtonians at Liverpool Exhibition A large number of Warrington people at- tended the Liverpool Exhibition on Thursday, which day had been specially set aside for War- rington visitors. There was quite a local touch about the exhibition and grounds by the Presence of many Warringtonians. Several musical competitions were held, but the entries were not as satisfactory as one - would, have liked them -to be. The choir of Bold-street Wesleyom Church was the only choir to enter the choral competition, held in the Carnival Hall. The silver cup has, therefore, been awarded them, and they will provide -a concert at the Exhibition to-night, under the - conductorship of Mr. Frank Hickman, the choirmaster. The soloists will include Mrs. A. Booth, Miss Emily Taylor, Miss Carrie Hastings and Miss Kershaw. After singing. the test piece, "0 Father, Whose Almighty Poiver," they were in- vited by the adjudicator (Herr Blattner) to give another item, and responded by singing "My Bonnie Lass" (German). In presenting the prize, a silver cup, to the conductor (Mr. Frank Hickman), Herr Blattner paid a compliment to the choir, and expressed the Opinion that he doubted if there was one as good, even in Liver- pool. In the brass band contest, the bands of the League of the Cross and the Pioneer com- peted. The ,bands rendered different nieces, and at the ,close each band was presented with a silver cup. There were eight competitors in the solo competition. and the award was as fol- lows : 1, Miss Carrie Hastings, Bold-street: 2, Mr. McArthur, Bold-street; 3, Miss M. Whit- more, Bold-street.

WATER FAMINE IN LANCASHIRE. Many mills in Lancashire which depend on water power will have to close down if the drought continues. At Nelson the reservoirs are nearly empty, and the town has only nine days' supply in hand. At Brierfield the people are getting water from a historic trough which has been used by cattle for centuries.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAt, SEPTEEit l3, 1913.

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

"HER SIDE OF THE HOUSE."

A Clever and Original Comedy at the Royal Court.

Seldom has Mr. Loader presented abetter or more taking play than that which he has brought this week:. direct direct from the Aldwich Theatre for the edification of his Warrington patrons. "Her Side of the House," by Lech- mere Worrall and Miss Atte Hall, is decidedly original. It is so original, in fact, that it shows signs of straining after effects to an extent that give an air of abnormality to many of its features; but the demand for something new has been met, and met well. It will not be detracting from the merits of the play as an entertainment as a whole if we say that its strategy is not of the brilliant type. But upon a foundation of domestic absurdities and moral improbabilities is built a superstructure of smart, not to say wise, sayings. Those say- ings are retailed by actors of the very first magnitude, so that, in the end, we have a highly satisfactory state of 'affairs. That the audiences have been large speaks volumes in these late summer days, when a theatre must Offer something out of the ordinary to beat all attractive. In the circumstances the audiences have been of the discriminating kind, and their attitude towards the play may be taken as a fair index to its character. It was on very rare occasions that' an interruption was caused by applause; all demonstrations were left for the end of a scene, or tip end of an act, and then the cheering was loud and sincere. In other words, there appeared to be little to single out for special note, but 'at the natural breath- ing places, when the production could be judged as a whole, or in complete sections, the acknowledgments were unstinted. The plot reveals a marriage of the sordid kind, a young and innocent heiress being mar- ried, "by order" of an imperious grandmamma, to a lord, whose fortune is very much down

at heels. A compact to have no honeymoon, but to live in their respective sides of the house, till ,they see whether they love each other, nearly er,ids in disaster, as a third person appears as a rival to his lordship, and keeps the fear Of a Divorce Court denouement before the minds of the audience almost to the last. Of course, this tragedy is avoided, a particularly cold-blooded .heroine eventually unbending her- self so'rrineh as to admit that she' roves her' hus- band, who is not slow to show his -satisfaction at the result of his weary wait. Miss Marjorie Murray as Cecile acted the part of the heroine with almost aggravating candour and it is to her artistic treatment of a difficult part, seconded with admirable' judgment by Mr. Philip Anthony as her sadly puzzled hus- band, that the play is elevated' to the height it undoubtedly attains. Madame De 13renne, the autocratic grandmamma, is also a character that requires a superior kind of treatment, and this it undoubtedly receives at the hands of Miss Moira Creegan. As the' intriguer, Lord Gerald Chomley, Mr. Wafter Walton Wynne, plays a nasty part with: considerable skill. Mr. Her- bert Maule as the Duke. o Vernay, Miss Rose Hersee as Victorine, Miss Winifred• Lawrence as. Mary, and Miss Tsuber Olaneatf as Lady Heathcote each do remarkablY-well. "Her Side of the House' is preceded by "The Lone Valley Had irt which Miss Rose Hersee takes the part of* Connie. and treats the audience to such dramatic touches as are scarcely reached ,during. tbe presentation of the more ambitioui. pray of the. evening. The scenery" and the. dresses of the company are on the magnificent Scale and make quite a feature of the ,productiOn. Special . "mUsic, AO°, lightens the rather long intervals andadds con- siderably to the enjoyment of the fare prov;.-led.

COMPANY.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

The Geni Family, who head a capital bill at the hippodrome ,this week, are ten of the smartest acrobats we have seen for some time. With marvellous rapidity they perform the most daring feats in acrobatics; including tumbling, somersaulting and hand-to-hand balancing. The Throe Arleys present their wonderfully trained canines, whose acrobatic untiCs call for loud .applauSe and .much laugh- ter. Unattended, save for the referee, the .dogs the part in a:very ceiting ." football 7 match, Knocking the ball ,with their noges, the dogs strive hard to penetrate each other's goal, and the fun is fast: and furious. Bart and Bart appear with success in their acrobatic absurdity, "The Baggage Wreckers." Whilst being smart acrobats, they are also comedians with many Smart gags. TakaSe ib a Japanese magician, whose astonishing sleight of hand tricks mystify and amuse all. The Barnard Family present fa. novel turn in "Dolls Alive." On the marionette's' principal the dolls give a capital entertainment and cause roars of laugh- ter. Elsie and Gaye Robertson are character duettists and dancers, who entertain in a pleasing manner. •

THE GREEN TICKET.

Warrington Doctors to Give Treatment Visitors a Fair Trial.

INSURANCE COMMITTEE MEETING.

The green ticket under the Insurance Apt for persons in receipt of medical benefit who may be on holiday is now to be accepted by the War- rington doctors, and thus another controversy under the Act has vanished.

Ala meeting of the Warrington Insurance Committee, the Mayor presiding, Mr. Tilling, the clerk, said the Conunittee had had some correspondence with the doctors on the panel about the question of temporary residence. The Secretary. of the Medical Association, Dr. T. M. Murray, now wrote stating that they had decided to give the system a trial. The minutes of the Finance Committee showed that the Committee had received an account from the Warrington Corporation of £152 ss. 4d. fOr the treatment of insured persons in Sankey Sanatorium for two months ending 31st August. The CLERK reported that the checking of the first quarter's prescriptions had been Com- pleted. The claims by the chemists amounted to £590 2s. 90., and the corrected amount was £582 us. nd. The Clerk said the chemists could examine the corrected accounts if they desired.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
A DOCTOR'S ACCOUNT.

The minutes of the Medical Benefit Sub-com- mittre stated that a retter was read from the Commissioners, enclosing a copy of a letter and account Of £5 13s. 4d. for dressings, &c.,' from Dr. Ligon. The Clerk, said the minutes, re- ported that Dr. Liston submitted the account to him some weeks ago, but inasmuch as it did not give any particulars as to the names of the persons to whom the dressings had been sup- plied, he had returned the account to Dr. Liston, with a request for the necessary information to be filled in. The Doctor had now sent the account to the Commissioners, asking them to autlwrise payment. It wits resolved that the account be re-submitted to Dr. Liston, asking for further details. _ _

Mr. DELANEY : What has happened in this case? _ .

The CLERK reported that he hod an interview with Dr. Liston, who stated that. it would- now be impossible to give the information asked for, seeing that the work was done so. long ago. Dr. Liston had now withdrawn the account.

ALLEGED EXCESSIVE SICKNESS.

The CLERK reported the receipt of a circular from the Insurance Commissioners, in which it was stated that a Commission had been ap- pointed to make inquiries with regard to the alleged excessive sickness claims, and added the suggestion that there would be no objection to Approved Societies appointing their own medical referees on a temporary basis, or the Insurance Committee could act for the Approved Societies, and thus reduce the fee of the medical referee.

The Insurance Committee, added the Clerk; was thus at the disposal of the societies, but the cost would have to be borne by the societies. On the proposition .of Mr. MASSEY, seconded by Councillor flotEnTs,, Mrs. Simpson was =elected a member of the Einance'SUb7committee.

Warrington Tradesmen Complain to Runcorn

Rural District Council.

A WATER CONTROVERSY.

Much inconvenience is caused to the trades- men of Warrington who have to deliver goods in Grappenhall arid Latchford Without through the streets not being named and the houses not numbered. The matter was brought to the attention of the Runcorn Rural District Council at the meeting held on Tuesday. A letter was .read from the Warrington Trtidernen7s 'Association pointing out.. that a number of _streets and roads in arappenhall and Latchford Without- were unnanied," and many houses were without numbers.- As this was a source of great inconvenience' to trades- !inerN- Messengers and others, the aSsOciation would be glad if the Council could see its way to remedy the matter.

Mr. • Lister-Kaye said it ..was -true ,4 great amount ,of inconvenience was caused by the absence of street name-plates and "'numbers On the houses. The letter was sent to the Parish Council, for them' to tifidertake the task of\ptitting the mat- ter right

Counsel's Opinion Wanted. A disputed question in the Warrington Water Act of 1899 was mentioned. It was stated that at a meeting Wilke Whitley Water Committee the Clei.k had produced the Act and read the clause for the protection of the Distriqt Council. He also explained that a difference had arisen between the Town Clerk of Warrington and himself in the reading of the clause, and asked that he be authorised to obtain counsel's opinion Upon the point. It was resolved that the opinion of counsel.be obtained. The Council decided to confirm the proposal of the Water Committee.

"HULLO, RAGTIME."

LATEST REVUE DIRECT FROM THE

LONDON HIPPODROME.

"Hullo, Ragtime !" the latest revue ,that conies from the London Hippodrome to the New Theatre, Manchester, shortly, should set the whole town dancing, humming and laughing. There is, indeed, a galvanic quality about the revue, which would make a saint in stone tingle withAa sembla.nce of life. The thing is ' all warmth, colour and beauty, relieved with' certain humour, occasionally lit up by,. the sacred lamp of burlesque, a sort of kaleidoscope stream flowing onwards in tides of bewitching and fantastic melody. In the mad whirl of topsy-turvydome we 'are introduced to a romantic actor, al an hotel hall porter, to a prominent music-hall actor, a beauty specialist, .and a number of politician and eminent personages are doing most 'out- rageous things, in the most outrageous 'fashion. Also some 'quaint American Comedy. of quite a novel character to The English stage, and to a beautiful Princess who has killed her husbands off like .so many flies and now settles down to write her memories. Such is the invention of the joint authors—Mr. Max Pemberton'and Mr. Albert de Courville. There are ragtime melo- dies galore by Mr. Louis Hirsch, who intro- duced ragtime into England.,

Ts,,A-RY PARTY DRAGGED BY TUMBREL.

The Junior Liberal' Were Itl4 infitne of pro, viding Warrington last evening, with an inspir- ing meeting, which ought to do good in arousing further interest amongst the public in the cause of progression. The meeting of last nigh 6 was intended to be an open-air demonstration' at Bridge Foot, but on account of the threat of rain the scene was changed to the Co-operative Hall. _there was a good attendance ,when Mr. C. E. Parker took the chair, and a rousing reception was given to the two chief speakers, Mr. Fran- cis Neilson, M.P. for Hyde, and Mr. J. B. Martindale, prospective Liberal candidate for Widnes.

The CHAIRMAN stated that they had two distin- guished visitors, and he would not stand be- tween the audience and the speakers. What he intended to say would be found in the "Examiner."

AT GRIPS WITH THE HOUSE OF LORDS. Mr. NEILSON referred to the." extraordinary" meeting he had addressed at Bridge Foot the last time he was in Warrington. They had waited for an opportunity to come to grips with the louse of Lords, and that opportunity came when they rejected the Budget. The rejection of that Budget at last enabled the democracy to come into conflict with the Lords and the question of their veto. Talk about killing two birds with one stone The election of December, 1910, was more won- derful than the one eleven months before, for the Parliamentary Act that brought it about was an Act that not only laid down the powers of the Lords in- regard to legislation, but it put it that the Lords had no longer the absolute power to reject Liberal legislation. (Applause.) A man not so strong as Asquith might have frittered time away and let the opportunity slip of dealing with the• Lords. Perhaps if he waited another year there would have passed out of the minds of democracy the old soreness, and' they would not have risen to the occasion as they did. It means more than that. It means that we who support Mr. Asquith can see that we can clear from the decks legislative lumber which has impeded progress for 25 years. (Hear, hear.) _ _

I hope to see the Bill for the better govern- ment of Ireland on the Statute Book, and I hope to see the Bill for the Disestablishment of the Church in Wales on the Statute Book. It is a great thing for the democracy to have these matters settled and out of the way.

payers,. who have been so heavily burdened by this .f:-.9%,757 per annum. But this taxation of the land is no new idea. I find that a Commission in 1885 reported that the present system enabled the landlord to hold up his land and keep up the speculative price of land. monopolies, and that people who wanted 'land could. not get it. That was said in 1885, 'atrid, it is still' true to-day, but that system will have. to got. To ensure that, it lies with you, the electors. It fs you,. the mass of the people outside, who are the makers of the Government, and the Government,. however bad, reflects the minds of the people: It; a Government makes progress it goes to show that there is an enlightened, intel- ligent people. This now time to make up your minds, in view of the election two years hence. You have time toi think, and think as you did in 1906. Thew it was a majority of 1,500 in Warrington, and; what is it now? On this ques- tion think, and when. you think you will turn back to the resuW in Warrington that you had in 1906. LLOYD GEORGE FINANCE.

Great Schemes That Have Provided the Money to Abolish Aged Paupers.

£4,000,000 FOR INSURANCE; RECORD DEBT REDUCTION.

The Chairman's speech was as follows : Before introducing our distinguished speakers I want for a few minutes to speak to you about housekeeping. Ido not mean iridividual house- keeping, but national. I want us to amuse our- selves, not by thinking in shillings, but in millions of pounds. This subject- is -suggested by the fact that we are speaking on ground that is almost becoming historic, it was here at Bridge Foot that we fought (as far as we could fight, locally) the great battle of the People's Budget. I have pleaiant recollection's of taking the chair here at several of those meetings. It was in 1909, that is, four years ago, that a gentleman, who is often called by, his opponents "A little lawyer from Wales," became our national purse bearer. He recognised that the great social reforms which were then urgent could not be carried through without a large. increase of national income, and so brought for- ward his proposals and embodied them in the great Budget. This Budget was stoutly opposed by the Tories; Tariff Reform was their alterna- tive. It passed the Commons and was throwzi out by the Lords. I will not follow the details of the conflict with the hereditary house; suffice to say that the Country compelled the Lords to accept it. Now, Lloyd •George required for his social legislation, more particularly old-age pensions, 'something like £14,000,000. He re- jected with scorn the Tory proposal to tax the things people buy, thus putting the bi,gge4_part of the burden on those least able to bear it: ,rn- stead he went for money where money was. The Income Tax was raised by 2d. in the X on Incomes arising from interest, dividends, Sic., an 4 threepence 'wits taken off incomes earned by•Nyor:111- This was the first. tithe that a differentiation of this kind was made, and you will admit it to be wise and just. Then we had the super-tax, by which those earning over £5,000 had to pay an additional sixpence. Were I to ask all those present who had to pay super-tax to hold up their hands, I do not think anyone would say that it was unfair that those earning such incomes should have to pay this extra.

Then estate and legacy duties were increased. Mr. Lloyd George reasoned that accumulated wealth, gathered not alone by energy and indus- try, but largely, because of favourable circum- stances, should pay a substantial portion back to the State, under whose protection the money had been accumulated. The Budget was ' founded upon the principle that all should bear their share in proportion to their ability, and so the Excise duties en spirits-and tobacco were raised .so that all class(mshot.ild be, cootribotory. It will long go to .the.credit of the poorer classes that they cheerfully paid the extra for their baCcy and beer, when they . realised for what purpose the money was required. This was in striking contrast to many rich people, who thought that they could frighten the nation into opposition of the Budget by talks about dis- missing servants, stopping subscriptions to churches, cricket clubs, and the like.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
EXTRA NATIONAL INCOME.

Now the merits of the two big taxes—lncome Tax and Estate Duties—as Mr. Lloyd George explained at the time, were that they would be progressive in their yield. The Income Tax which. Mr. George budgetted to yield £3,500,000, now yields an increase of over £13,000,000. This, of course, is not all due to increased rates, but partly to general national prosperity. Mr. George anticipated the death -duties to yield an increase on the first clear year of £1,850,09; it now yields over £8,000,000. However anxious and ready wealthy people may be to avoid other taxes, and imperil the success of Liberal finance; however • anxious they were to discredit Mr. Lloyd George, they could not cheat death. They' might threaten to send, their capital to 'foreign countries, but they could find no method of conveyance that would transfer it either to heaven or the .other place, and so it has to be left -behind, and through the People's Budget the nation claims its part. Through the opera- tion of this Budget .R 25,000,000 additional now goes to the National Revenue. There is an in- crease of £6,000,000 from sources which were not altered by the Budget, such as the Post Office, telegraphs, &c. This makes an increased annual revenue at date of £31,000,000.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW IT IS SPENT-AGED PAUPERS

ALMOST OBLITERATED.

Let us now see how this £31,000,000 has been spent. The expenses on the Army and Navy, which may be looked upon as unproductive, but as a form of national insurance, have increased £11,000,000. Practically the whole of the rest of the increase has been spent on social ancipro- ductive expenditure. The largest amount of this is the cost of old-age pensions, which absorbs £12,000,000. When ,Ive used to talk about . this Act from this platform in former years we could only look forward and anticipate its effect upon the people, and upon the finances of the country. ,Sufficient time has elapsed to put us in a position to estimate the actual effect of. this boon to those of our people the sun cf whose life is setting in the west. First there are the many it -raised out of the odious position of being in receipt of pauper relief, and made , them self-respecting citizens.. We find that in

England and Wales the number of aged paupers has been reduced by 171,704. I -am giving the figures as at the beginning of this year. Most of this decrease has been in outdoor paupers: Proportionately for every four persons receiving relief in 1906, only one is receiving such relief in 1913. In other words, we have cut down the, number of old-age paupers by 75 percent. Now this does not mean that this 171,704 persons were at one time receiving relief. Over seventy the death-rate is high, and the great bulk of this 171,704 have never been paupers, hut would have had to become such but for this timely aid. In other words, it has largely stopped the supply of aged persons who would have to apply for poor relief. But the number of those receiving pensions are not anything like represented by the number who would have had to depend on' the poor rates. There have always been in our country those whose pride has induced them to live in penury and comparativelnisery that they might retain the self-respect which poor relief undermines. These are the great majority of those who, when their eyes become dim and their natural force abates, have received the help. There are about one million aged persons in this kingdom at present in the receipt of pensions, fully four-fifths of these would never have come on the Poor Rates at all, but who, without these pensions, would have had to pinch and starve. In the receipt of these pensions there is no foss of self-respect; they feel they have as much right to it, and perhaps more, than Lord Cross,, or the descendants of Welling- ton and Marlborough. But coming back to the 171,704 reduction in aged persons receiving Door relief, we might naturally expect that the ratepayers' burden would be substantially reduced, and this is fact. In agricultural districts, where proportionately the poor rate was heavy, it has reduced this as much as ten per cent., and ail local authorities have been benefited more or Tess. So much for the Old Age Pensions Act. £4,000,000 FOR INSURANCE. The Great Budget has lso: provided the fund for the working of the Insurance Act, and. this absorbs from .0,500,000 to £44903,000 yearly. The Act has been in operation too short a time to form any accurate estimate of its effect. But are we not justified in believing that it will be the most remunerative national investment that ever the nation has made? We may take for granted that it will lessen immensely the amount of human suffering and privation; it will be the means of saving many valuable lives; it will help us to stamp out the most fatal of all diseases—that of consumption. But the effect on the general health of the people will be very great; it will make them mere efficient in pro- duction, more virile, and altogether give them a fuller and a better life.

A RECORD IN PAYMENT OF DEBT. Amongst the other items which absorb the remainder of the £20,000,000 avairable for pro- ductive expenditure, and in which increasing national funds are being spent, I may name national education, the development grant for improving our roads, &c. Whatever balance is left after these national needs have been satis- fied goes to the liquidation of the National Debt, and the Liberal Government has made a record in the last seven years by paying off the average of about £12,000,000 a year, £82,000,000 alto- gether. With such a record, ladies and gentlemen, I have no fear but that you will agree with me in saying that the Great Budget, the People's Budget, was well worth fighting for, that the Liberal party deserves well of this audience and the country generally, and there is very little chance of the electors of this country placing their confidence in a party whose only definite 'proposal is the introduction into the body Politic of the rotten, worm-eaten plank—Tariff Reform. Ladies and gentlemen, with that I ; leave it. I have no doubt that our visitors will ; refer to the future policy of the Liberal party. I recognise that no party can live upon its past, however good and great. But at the same time the confidence we may have in the future 'doings of a party depends largely on what they have done in the past.

HAS LORD LOREBURN BEEN ASLEEP?

Lord Loreburn comes forward of all people of I the world. Laughter.) He , says: "Now let us have a round table conference:" What is the matter with him? Has he just wakened up? Has he been asleep for two years and more? We had a round table opnference, ang what was the result?. It broke up, it .could not come to an agreement. .Let us face, the thiig like men. I read in this morning's papers that a certain Captain Craig over in Ulster—(laughter)—has heard of Lord_Loreburn's suggestion, and he. pours contempt and ridicule upon it. Of course, before there can be another round table .conference, at which Lord_ Lansdowne or Lord Londonderry and other Tory leaders can attend, they have .got to .ask permission.frOm the Cap- tamn Craigs. (Laughter.)' Lord LorebUria . has riot, re'Okoneit with Captain Craig. He his for- gottOn about that. What is the Tory party to-day? How do we see it to-day--dragged contemptuously at the back of the last tumbrel? (Laughter.) It is the appendage of the Unionist spirits from Ulster. Who are the leaders of the Tory party? Mr. Bonar.Law, Sir Robert Finlay, and those other men, who have been familiar Unionist figures for years? Not a bit. They are the office boys of Sir Edward Carson and the Captain Craigs. (Loud' laughter and applause.) It is one of the funniest things that have happened in politics for years is this letter from Lord Lore- burn.

DEMOCRACY HAS MADE UP ITS MIND. I know what is in the minds of the democracy. I have had the opportunity of seeing the atti- tude of the people in by-election after by-elec- tion, and I saT that the British democracy has rnadeqirrits mind that Ireland must haye Howe. Rule. It is not affected by any bogies or talk of civil war, or the taking in of wives and orphans. (Laughter.) Ireland is to have Home Rule, and that settles it. Mr. 13,alfourlls us ,ths,t a General Election is not far off, and that we must have a General ' Election before the Bill reaches the Statute Book. I have an idea why so much is made about this question of Ulster. What is behind it? What is the subtle Motive? The motive is the fear of an attack on the monopoly of the land. The landlords are afraid. Those people came behind the tears of the widows and orphans, and say the same tears will .do for us. (Laughter.) I wonder how much is real and how much is false, and how much is really to take the attention of the masses of this country away from the economic condition of the people.. (Applause.) I have come to think that even the Belfast ' question itself is a matter of losing the plums which have dropped so easily and so lightly into the laps: of the dominating party -in Ire. land. We all -squeal when . someone else -gets the plums we have been watching ripening for i3o long, • . . , Dealing with the -Inestion .of "land cam- paign, land programmes, and land reform," he said we had heard a great deal about them lately. He did not think Lord Lansdowne's idea was 'going to create a revolution in the country.
