MR. F. E. SMITH.

Mr. F. E. Smith is a hot gospeller on some subjects, and a very attractive speaker. We must all confess, no matter what our political opinion is, that Mr. F. E. Smith says a thing in clear, lucid English, and there is no misunder- standing him. A little while ago he said, "The impertinence of these Radicals allocating land teform to theniselves." (Laughtei.) , Mr. Smith thought the Tory party were the only land reformers. (Laughter.) Mr. F. R. Smith suggested something about a round table land conference.

I would like, said Mr. Neilson, to go to a round table conference, and get Mr. Lloyd George, Mr. Asquith, Mr. Outhwaite, and per- haps myself on one side, and Lord Lansdowne, the Duke of Norfolk, Mr. F. E. Smith and Mr. Harold Smith on the other hand. (Loud laughter.) It would not last long. Mr. F. E. Smith knows the need of social reform. He has been one of the men in the Tory Party who told them that, they must have a social reform pro- gramme. They formed themselves into a com- mittee to map out a social programme, and they must know that they must not leave out the land question, because they cannot have social reform until the land question is first reformed.

WARRINGTON AND THE LAND QUESTION.

Dealing with the land, he said: I have a docu- ment here which throws some interesting light ulkin the question, and that from the Warring- ton point of view. In Warrington there are 3;116 acres, and of that there are 750 rated as agricultural land. The 2,600 odd contribute an-. nually £99,757, and the 750 acres contribute £230. Your town does not, correspond with some I could mention, like Manchester, Liver- pool, or Newcastle-on-Tyne, where there are astounding inequalities. The land in Warring- ton rated as agricultural is fairly small. Now; under the land values proposal, that 750 acres will be taxed upon its selling value, not upon the annual value that goes down in the rate book, so that the owners may get the 50 per cent. benefit under the Agricultural Rates Act. If his land is for budding and the owner wants one, two, four, or five hundred pounds for it, we say he should be taxed .upon that. Thus you will immediately increaso enormously the ratable basis .of yot7 bough, and you /will he able to give an enormous relief to the ,rate-

GUARDIAN DEPUTATIONS. 'nteresting Discussion at the Meeting of

the Runcorn Board. ACTING AS A GODFATHER.'

The evergreen question of deputations, which periodically seems to arise in . every Board of Guardians, made its appearance at the meet- ing of the Runcorn Guardians on Tuesday. Mr. .Hallows moved that a committee, con- sisting of the Chairman and Vice-chairman, Messrs. Woodward and T; Wright, propose the appointment of members of the Board to the different deputations in future. He suggested that a Standing Committee would be more sat- isfactory and remove the responsibility of mak- ing the appointments from one man's shoulders. Mr. Linaker, sen., asked if that meant instead of a certain gentleman making the appointment it should be made by a small committee. • Mr. Hallows : Mr. Linaker: It is perfectly open for anyone to object. Mr. Hallows': They don't like to do so. .Mr. Morris seconded. He had, he said, a fair share of deputations, but he thought the time had come when the question of deputations should be gone into with the object of seeing Who had been and who had not. Mr. Linaker, .sen., said it was. always Mr. Woodward's effort to get new members on 'deputations:

A Reflection. Mr. Woodward said he-- tOok•the resolution as a reflection upon what he had done during the last ten years. He had a list of the attend- ances before him, and he found that, with one or two exceptions, no member had been to a particular conference more than once at the expense of the Board. of Guardians in the last ten years.- Several members had been once to each. of .these.:ednferoces. Several members who had been elected to the Board three years ago had been on deputations. He personally had, been •on 'each. 'deputation once in the f•ast ten yours. It had always been his desire to give consideration to those members who were constant in attendance at committees and who could intelligently .enter into the Work of the conference, and Come back and give proper reports. In his appointments he had feared no man, nor favoured anyone. Mr. Hallows: Members grumble outside, but they won't speak in the Board-room'. The -Chairman: So you are acting as god-

'at-h.e—r for them. (Laughter.) The motion was lost.

Old-age Pension Effects. Mr. Wood referred to a question raised by Mr. Linaker, sen., at the last meeting as to the effect of the Old-Age Pensions Act in the Union. Since then he had made it his business to find out something like the correct effect of the Act. In 1906 they ,had in the Union 209 persons over 70 years of age in receipt of outdoor relief. At the present time they had only 26, and many of those cases were persons receiving more than ss. a week and:they had not for this and. other reasons applied for it. In 1906 they were paying -X3;130 .10s., and in 1912 they were pay- ing £1.,817 7s. 10d., a saving of £1,313 2s. 4d. to the rates. In the whole. county the number receiving old-age .pensions was 3,158, and last year that was 230, a decrease of 92.7 per cent. Mr. Linaker said that Mr. , Wood seemed to think it was hi S intention to 'throw a dispar- agement upon the Old-Age Pensions Act. What he did say was that he had read an extract from a White Paper,' which stated that the amount paid in old-age pensions was £2,000,000, and that it had relieved the rates in some places from sevenpence to a shilling in the X. He won- dered to what extent the Act had relieved the rates in their Union. He was not opposed to the Act. He belieyed that old-age pensions were a fine thing for many deserving poor, but ,he did not entirely agree with the administra- tion of them Mr. Wood thought that Mr. Linaker had mis- construed the White Paper report. It did not state that the rates had been reduced So much in the X. It meant that if the £8,000,000 being paid in old-age pensions was being paid in out- relief it would raise the rates 7d. to is. in sonic places. Mr. Linaker: I know what it meant, but it doesn't say that.

CASE OF GREAT GRAVITY.

at the Police Court.

NATURE S ENIGMAS.

Sir,—As was suggested to the writer by recent remarks of your Nature contributor, the face of Nature abounds with enigmas. The great soli.: tary boulders which lie on the grass of the pasture—Who set them 'there? The winding linear ,tiollows, curiously like channels of ancient rivers, which extend access the country- side—who.cleaved them.? The strange chased- work of scratches, engraved on the shoulders of scattered hammocks or nude rock—who wrought it? Tho rolled pebbles. Which are spread over the bed of the feeblest brook or rivulet—who strewed them? The pits and other depressions which constantly occur amid the tilled fields— who dug them? The tracts of incredibly level peat-moss—who spread them? The sea-shell, which may be disengaged from almost any ex- posed bed of limestone or calcareous sandstone —who sowed them? Truly, this ancient earth of ours has many mysteries—cyclic secrets. 3-c.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
RAILWAYS AND' THE PUBLIC.

Sir,—A contributor in one of our morning Papers suggested, the other day, in view of the terrible railway accident which occurred last week, the formation of an association, having for its object the protection of railway passen- gers. Such an association would make repre- sentations to the Board of Trade and the various Railway Boards, from time to time, for the carrying into effect of any improvements or alterations in our railway system which may be considered necessary for the greater comfort and safety of passengers. I would ask your readers to remember that it IS not incumbent upon a railway company to consider or carry into effect any recommenda- tions prescribed by a jury, and expense is very often a large factor in deciding whether or no such recommendations be acted upon. It seems to me that at the present time the public has too little say in the control of our railways, and it is only by organisation that passengers can obtain recognition of their rights. I shall be pleased to hear from anyone inter- ested in this subject with a view to the imme- diate formation of this society. R. M. MONK.

Lonsdale Villa, Kniltsford-road, Grappenhall, Warrington, September 11th, 1913.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0202.txt | included=true | included =====
HOME RULE.

Sir,—! am sure that a great many, of your friends -who are not biased by political pre- judice Will regret the revival of Horne Rule by the present Government. It has stirred up the latent animosity of the two ,great sections Of the Irish people, and has set back the march ot progress some score of years.. Your corre- spondent, "F.W.R.," may sneer at passing events, but I doubt if he would have entered on his self-imposed task so lightheartedly had he waited a day or two longer. We need not go to Ulster. We have sufficient taking place in Dublin at the present time to make all sen- sible people pause-and ask in whose hands are we placing the government of Ireland. , A poor country, in alliance or amalgamation vlith a rich one, always benefits by the associa- tion of interests. A small firm is stronger in every way when united to or in association with a large one. The larger and richer country is certain by its interests and influence to greatly benefit the smaller; this has long since been realised in Scotland, where you find no agita- tion for a separate or divided authority. Scotch- men and Englishmen work together for the benefit of one common country, and why not Irishmen, too? Why should not the inhabi- tants of these little islands unite in forining one homogeneous nationality? In every part of our world-wide Empire the so-called three or four nationalities work together in one harmonious whole; it is only reserved for the limits of the British Isles for these petty national squabbles. The race that makes the most bother about it is the most scattered and the least progressive. We want to cease this political bandy and finite in advancing the best interests of the Empire at large, and each individual section in particular.

PHIL SQUOD.

SHALL WARRINGTON HAVE A DEBATING SOCIETY?

Sir,---

He loved to play at soldiers, . And go marcning with the.boys. • Such, I take it, is an exact description of the rnemOerS of our local Pebating Society---playing at politics, as children play at •Shdp. •Young fellows, with a smattering of political knoWleuge gleaned -fi-oni a biassed parents' casual •allusions to "his party," join the ranks of the society, and sit on 'their side of' the House," with a mental attitude which shuts out once and for all the possibility of their being wrong and their opponents right. Time and again Borne Buie Bills, Tariff Re- form Budgets, &c., Scc. are passed with tre- mendous enthusiasm, [tiler speeches which are merely repetitions of the horrible, hackneyed, cat-call phrases of a party hero. And "to what purpose is this waste"? Only a small percentage of members ever rises to speak; and those members who do, are just the men who might, with advantage, give politics a long rest. Party hates and intrigues abound in greater force, and in the end the actual state of poli- tical chaos is not affected in the slightest.' „ The very system of party politics is not worthy of imitation, in the first place. Both sides of THE House are a living example of the Knights and the Shield. And if,

Jest should turn to earnest When he throws. away his toys, what profit? _

If it is rt- 'care for things "which make an appeal to intellect," take something intellectual, not a sham.

Study our Poor Law, our systems of dealing with social amenities. Find out what measures have been taken to grapple with these problems, and then COMBINE (not divide) to deal with these evils.

Make yourselves a source of advice, and an example to our City Fathers, and our party- ridden legislators.

Then, playing at soldiers will eventually re- sult in aetual warfare against the rottenness of present-day politics.

ONLOOKER.

WARNING FROEI THE BENCH.

At the Warrington Police Court on Thursday,' before Mr. A. J. Jolley (in the. chair) and 'other .magistrates, Joseph Thomas Dolan, 141, Lovely- lane, pleaded guilty to embezzling the sum of £4 135.,. the monies of his employer, ,Ben"edict Tyrer, a well-known local pawnbroker. -• ' Mr. A. Browne prosecuted and Mr: Jenkins represented the .defendant. Mr. Browne said that defendant was formerly the manager of Mr. Tyrer's Orford-lane shop. Some time ago it was found that there was a deficiency in the stock, and he was dismissed. As the manager, he was in a very responsible position, having to be trusted a great deal. In the first case of embezzling £4 Gs. id. it would appear that Mr. Pemberton, cycle • dealer, Orford-lane, had an account with Mr. Tyrer, in the ordinary way, of £4 14s. Defendant had had a lot of repairs and had purchased different articles for machines, which now turned out to be Mr. Tyrer's property, to the value of ,E 4 12s. 4d. Mr. Pemberton paid defendant £4 14s. Id., and defendant in turn paid Mr. Pemberton the full amount he owed. Defend- ant only credited Bs. in Mr., Tyrer's books, but ruled out the whole of the account, thereby showing at once that the account was settled. In the second case a Mr. William Ellison, florist, Orford-lane, owed Mr. Tyrer 7s. 6d., and the defendant , had an accotint owing to Mr. Ellison for flowers, &c. Mr. Ellison gaye defendant a receipt for Is. 6d. and Mr. Ellison paid the defendant the 7s. 6d. owing to Mr. Tyrer. This receipt was never entered in the books at all. The principal object in the pro- secution was that there had been a number of defalcations in the past by managers of pawn- shops in the town, and the association, of which Mr. Tyrer was a member, had decided that in future no cases would be overlooked. There were, be understood,,flve cases which had been overlooked since October •last, and the Pawn- brokers' Association thought it time to bring such matters to an end.-

Very Awkward.

Stock was taken in the- present case, and it was found to be correct, but subsequently, -by accident, it was 'discovered that defendant had. kept back invoices;which made the stock short. These were, for -goOds sold before he was, dis- missed, and before the stock-takinq one of the invoices was found amongst the receipts. 'iliat state of affairs might make it very awkward for a mew manager, 'who might be suspected. Defendant had offered to return what he had in kind which belonged to Mr. Tyrer and make the defalcations good, but the prosecution had refused that offer. They had, however, no desire to send the man to prison. Mr. Jenkins was. obliged for the kindly. way. In which the'prosecution had acted in the mat- ter. Since the adjournment he had inspected the books and he had endeavoured to test the accuracy of defendant's statements. .1-1 e had suggested that perhaps through pressure. of business and carelessness prisoner had forgot- ten to make the entries. Of course, to come to business men and say that the alleged offences were the innocent acts of a; careless man was folly: Having explained Prisoner's position to • him he had advised him (Mr. Jenkins) to plead guilty to the two items. He submitted that there was not that degree of guiltiness which a trained thief would exercise; still there was wrongdoing. In the case of the 7s. 6d. Mr. Ellison had said: "Let us set one account off against the other." He gave him (1, receipt and he forgot to enter it when he,got back to the shop, but forgetfulnesq was not a fustification in the eyes of the law, and he pleaded guilty. The matter of £4 6s. Id; was precisely of the same character, Pemberton and defendant giving mutual receipts.

"Oh, I forgot!"

He went back to the shop, where he was kept blisy, and in his haste he forgot to enter the amount. But it was no use the man saying "Oh, I forgOt 1" He suggested that the magis- trates might temper justice with mercy in dealing with The defendant. - He was satisfied that there,was no desire to crush the man, who was just starting in business On his Own. He •tifid a ',Wife and four children. - He trusted the "Bench -would speak to the defendant in such a :way that the ease would be ever remembered by him.. The Bench retired, and on their return the Chairman said the case was one of great gravity. If a case of that kind came before the magistrates again it wore'd be very- severely dealt with. They Would bind defendant over for 12 months in the sum of £lO, and order himn to pay £5 ss. towards the costs.

Scottish Bowlers' Day Out.

For Early Risers

The 'members of the Warrington Caledonian Association brought their bowling season to a close on Saturday by a most enjoyable outing to the Hermit, Newton*Willows. The weather was splendid and the green was in fine condition. In the bowling drive some very keen endi were played, and the result was: Ladies- 1, Mrs. W. Stirling; 2, Mrs. J. McDermid. Goats-1, Mr. G. Marshall; 2, Mr. W. Mitchell. In the ladies' main doubles. Mrs. W. Mitchell and Mrs. F. Love were first, and Mr. J. Ten- nant and Mrs. Lockerbie second. Tea was pro- vided by Mrs. Copple, and the catering was again of the best. An impromptu concert was arranged, the artists being: Mrs. T. Donnelly, Mrs. W. Hutcheson and Miss Lyall ;.Messrs. J. Burns, W. Aitken, T. Robertson, T. Donnelly, Y. Clegg, W. Murray, W. Stirling, F. Love, G. Marshall and J. 3.lcDermid. The singing of the children in the " Caledonian anthem, "It's jist like bein' at lame,'.' was much enjoyed, and great praise is due to Mr. Robertson for. the trouble he has taken to get them to such perfec- tion. In the singing contest for the children, the girls were easily the prize-winners. Mr. Murray presided. A vote of thanks was accorded. the secretary for the carrying out of the ar- rangements, and a vote of thanks was given to Mr. and Mrs. Copple. Mr. Copple responding. A. very enjoyable evening was brought to a close with tl;_e singing of " Auld lang syne."

It would not do to go home with an empty basket.

A fish shop was handy, so he bought a large fish.

"Did you actually catch this in the canal?" asked the wife.

LI Yesh, me dearsh ; had a terrible struggle withsh it."

THE LATE MR. CHARLES RICHARDS.

A Veteran of the Old Coaching Days.

The funeral took place on Tuesday af Mr. Charles Richards, of Lymna, who died suddenly on Saturday morning from heart failure, at the age of .84. The doctor had been attending him for some time, :owing to an attack of pneumonia, but he had somewhat recovered from this, and was beginning to get about again, when on Friday night, without the slightest warning, he became suddenly ill again, and died early the following morning. The funeral was a very impressive one, and took place at the parish churchyard. Little is known of his early days, except that he commenced work as a pageboy to the late Sir Watkin Wynn, but many are the stories he used to tell of the old coaching days, and his varied experiences while in the employ of Lady .Mannering, of Otley Park, as coachman and postillion.

He was a native of Shropshire, and came to reside in Lymm some 40 years ago, and since then has taken prominent part in the different organisations in the district. He carried on, for some years, a stationery business, • together with a daily newspaper round, and he also did the billposting for the district. Eventually, he retired from the stationery business, but kept on with the billposting up to the time of his death. He assisted Mr. Mullard (the clerk to the Council) by taking the rate bills round. Mr. Richards was a prominent worker in con- nection With the May Queen festival, and did much to make it a success. He was also a trustee for the Lymm Tontine Dividing Club, and an active member of the Tradesmen's Association. ,

MARRIAGE OF MISS FLORENCE H. ROGERSON.

Pretty Ceremony at the Wesleyan Church.

The Wesleyan Church, Lynam, -I,i4s the scene on Wednesday afternoon of another pretty wed- ding, and as both the contracting parties are well known, the occasion attracted a large num- ber of friends. The bride was Miss Florence Hannah Rogerson, youngest daughtec of Mr. and Mrs. John Rogerson, of Lymm. Tne bride- groom was Mr. John Margate, of Grappenhall. The bride.was given away by her father, and the ceremony .was performed by the Rev. J. H. Pawlyn, of Lymm. The organist was Mr. Harry Brown, who played appropriate music; also the choir, of. which the bride was a member, was in attendance. Mr. Arthur Allman, friend of the bridegroom. efficiently performed the duties of best man. The groomsman was :tr. James 'Drink-water.

The bride was charmingly dressed in white mouselline silk, trimmed with lace, while she wore a bridal veil of embroidered tulle and orange.- blossom. She carried a bouquet of carnations and white heather.

She was attended by two bridesmaids, Miss Lillian Rogerson (sister) and Miss Margaret Rogerson (niece). Miss L. Rogerson was taste- fully dressed in white lace, trimmed with amber silk, with black hat trimmed with flowers to match, and carried a bouquet of bronze chry- thinthemums. Miss Margaret Rogerson was daintily dressed in white silk, with bat to match. , - -

The chapel was nicely decorated with flowers and ferns by the members of the choir. The children of the iprimary class, of which 'the bride was a teacher, presented her with a floral horseshoe, and were taken by motor car to the reception where they were entertained. The happy couple were in receipt of a number of valuable presents, including a silver hot- water jug from the members of the choir, and a beautiful clock from the Sunday school; also a silver cake knife from the primary children.. Later they started for the honeymoon, which is being spent at the Isle of Arran, in Scotland.

Alfred Broome. father of defendant, said complainant's car came straight at his son's, and they swerved to the wrong side in order to save life.—Another witness named Kelly stated that both motors seemed to get "mixed up."—The Bench dis- missed the case.

A BAD CASE OF NEGLECT.

Preferred a Lymm Girl to Ills Wife and

Family.

At the Warrington Police Court on Saturday, before Mr. R. T. Fairclough (in the chair), Councillor Locker, Messrs. A. J. Jolley and 'l'. Pemberton,

George William Hollis, formerly of 4, Morris- court, Warrington, was charged with having neglected his three children.

Sir. A. Browne, for the N.S.P.C.C., said the case was out of the ordinary run, and there were aggrzivated circumstances. The defendant was 27 years of age and was engaged by a window cleaning company and made 245. to 265. per week. He had, given his wife LI. On the previous Saturday morning he went off without giving his wife any money. He pawned a suit of clothes and kept that money. Inspector Wain- wright went down and found that,there was no food in the house. It was ascertained that de- fendant had lived in lodgings in Manchester with a single young woman, and he was only brought back the previous night. The girl, who was 19 years of age, belonged to Lymm. A letter had been found from the girl to the defendant, in which she stated that she thought it best they should part. If the talk that was going about .concerning defendant were not true she would not leave him. If defendant, she said, had been a straightforward young fellow lie would have spoken the truth at first. Con- tinuing, 'Mr. Browne said the paxents of the girl were communicated with, and the girl returned. Prisoner had sent 10s. to his wife, but she had gone to live at Little Lever, near Bolton. where she had got employment as a weaver. Defend- ant had not only pawned his suit and boots, but had sold his bicycle and had got a situation with a window cleaning firm in Aignchester. l'risoner said that his wife pawned every- thing she could get. The girl wanted to come with him.

Inspector Wainwright said the defendant wss dressed in The latest fashion, whilst his wife had to make anything do. Defendant was bound over for 12 months.

Roberts (the chairman) said that as so Much time had elapsed they would give defendant the benefit of the doubt, and dis- raiss the cases against him.

NEARLY FELL OVER WHEN TOUCHED.

Alleged Cruelty to a Pony. A strange story about a Stockton Heath pony was narrated at the local Petty Sessions on Tuesday, Councillor 11. Roberts presiding. Harry Webster, 20, Mitchell-street, Stockton Heath, was charged with ill-treating a pony•by working it while it was in an unfit condition on August 15th. John Taylor, Walton-road, Stockton Heath, the employer of Webster, was summoned for causing it to be worked. Both men tendered a plea of not guilty. Inspector Dee, the local representative of the R.S.P,C.A., said he was in Walton-road. Stockton Heath, when he saw defendant Webster in charge of a roan pony, drawing a greengrocer's cart. He examined the animal and told defendant the pony was in a very weak and poor condition, and Webster admitted that it was in a poor state and said: "I have been expect- ing this for some considerable time now."

Witness added that he told Taylor Of the horse's condition, and he promised it should not work again. When witness put his hand on the animal it nearly fell over. "The horse was only skin and bone," added the Inspector. P.C. Crampton said he visited the stable in company with Inspector Dee. The pony was in a very poor, weak and emaciated condition. It almost dropped over when he *put his hand on it.

Webster now said the animal worked all right. Taylor added that it bore no sores. It Was not lame, nor was it in. pain.

"Are you going to work it any more?" Taylor was asked. "No," he answered, "I am going to sell it."

The Chairman: -For somebody else to work? (Laughter.) __ _

• , Defendant No; I shall sell it to a dealer, who buys such horses. As Taylor gave en undertaking. tn have the animal destroyed, the Bench fined him 10s., in eluding costs, and dismissed the case against Webster.

HATTON.

INVADING THE TORY PRESERVES.—A Liberal meeting was held at Hatton on Monday evening. The speakers who dared to invade this Tory preserve were Mr. Topilinson and Mr. Dockett, connected with the Liberal van. Mr. Tomlin- son spoke first and met with a mixed reception. He said that an open-air Liberal meeting in Hatton must be regarded. as a novelty. Whilst the gentleman was speaking he met with many interruptions.—Mr. Dockett, in a splendid speech, lasting about an hour, satisfactorily answered many queries put by the villagers. Mr. Lockett dealt chiefly with the land question and told the farmers how much they were in- debted to this much-reviled Government, what liberties and rights they had given them, and were still striving to give them. He told the labourers how Mr. Lloyd George and his col- leagues had fought for them in insuring their health and granting theta the pension when they came to the ago Of seventy, and how the Liberal Government was still fighting to give them decent houses to live in, and a living wage to exist upon.—Mr. Tomlinson closed the meet- ing with a few words on Tariff Reform. He pointed out ,how Free Trade benefited this island how we could have the best of everything brought to our doors from far dominions at a reasonable rate, and although food prices had risen the world over, in no country was food as cheap as in England. The Liberal motto was " Fight for the right," and, in the words of a great Liberal statesman, England should be the treasure-henso of the poor, and not the play- ground of the rich.

PENKETH.

SAILING CLUB RACE.—The Fiddlers Ferry Sail- ing Club had a race on Saturday for. Mr. Proc- tor's prize and. club prizes. Five entries were secured, and the yachts were: 'one,. Messrs. Thomson. and Knowles; Comrade,. Mr. • H. Bar- them; Thelma, .Mr.. H.. Warburton; Pastime, Mr. Meacock, and Nina, Mr. Whittaker. • The lone, however, scratched. The boats 'were started by. Mr. F. Bennett, commodore. All went well until Runcorn was reached, where the Comrade was in difficulties, her mast being broken. This left three boats to finish what proved the best race of the season. The feature of the race was the remarkable way the Thelma held on after having the worst of the start. She overtook the Comrade before that boat broke her mast, then closed up with the other two. and eventually won by six seconds from the Nina, the Pastime being third. A protest was lodged after the race against the Thelma, but was re- jected at the meeting of the club held on Thurs- day, the Thelma being given the race. .This puts her level with the Comrade on points for the Kelsall Cup, so a keen contest is assured for this cup. Mr. H. Bennett acted as time-taker at the Liverpool end.

TIIE HISTORY AND ROMANCE OF THE HOUSE OF LORDS.

For more than six hundred years, the Renee of Lords has shared in the governance of England and her widespread Empire. Once it has been abolished, and more than <num it has been curbed. Its home has been burnt down over its head; popular agitations have repeatedly threatened it, and yet it sur- vives, the proudest citadel of soqial and poli- tical privilege remaining in Europe. We cannot think of the great past of Westmin- ster without it; and Westminster, whatever jibes may occasionally escape us, is our Val- halla, our Acropolis, our Forum. The robustness of the old-age of the House of Lords is due, 714 doubt, largely to the fact that it was, in tlke beginning, the stronger part of the legislature, being based upon a well-establishe4 feudal nobility, whereas the rights of the Commons have only slowly grown through the centuries. Before the Compost, the principal part of the adminis- tration had been in the hands of a few great Earls, descendants of royal or quasi-royal houses which had at last been forced to sub- mit to the single King of England. After- wards, the power of the King was greatly strengthened at the cost of the nobles. The balance of power, however, constantly shift- ed: and it was to this balance of power be- tween King, Barons, Church, and Towns, added to the effect of the ancient custom of holding Councils of the great Chiefs, and to the growing need of money for the central Government, that the institution of Parlia- ment is due.

What is regarded as the first complete Par- liament, that established by Simon de Mont- fort, consisting of knights representing the counties, and citizens and burgesses repre- senting the towns, met in 1264-5. It was summoned in the name of Henry 111. under coercion of the nobles, who d'emanded the carrying out of the Great Charter. This first Parliament was revived twenty years later by Edward 1., and our fundamental institution has lived continuously since, though with in. terruptions, and has been gradually imitated by .all the civilised nations of the world. At the outset, be it remembered, the Peers in Council were not, as such, -an hereditary class. It was only slowly that the custom of summoning descendants grew into a right.

THE FIRST PICTURE OF PARLIAMENT. At first, -what afterwards became the two branches sat together. There is an ancient drawing which depicts Edward I. sitting on the throne of the joint house, with the King of Scotland and the Prince of Wales a step lower to his right and left; the Archbishop of York a little lower still; and, on the 'Wool- sack in front of the throne, the Lord Chan- cellor, the two Chief Justices and the Baron of the Exchequer. Behind these, facing the throne, are two clerks, and then seven per- sons suppmed to be the 'Faithful Commons,' with another on a higher, seat supposed to be the Speaker, that is to say, the person whe was appointed to communicate with the King on the Commons' behalf. Up the sides of the Chamber are ranged four beaches of 'Peers and Bishops. At the end of the 14th century, the two Es- tates separated, the Commons sitting in the ancient Chapter House of the Abbey, and the Lords, with the Bing, in what was called the Painted Chamber of the Palace at Westmin- ster. The separation is another illustration of the gravity of the question of finance. The Lords were the King's Council, his Peers, that is to say his social equals; and he sat with them, as he is still supposed to preside, in person or by his deputy, the Lord Chancel- lor, over the sittings of the Upper Howe. Rut it was invidious and inconvenient to discuss the votes :which were to be made to the Bing in his presence. The Lords, at least under the Norman scheme, undertook in return for their vast grants of land, the main burden of organising the national defence. Upon the Commons fell the burden of raising money both for civil and military necessities., The custom, therefore, grew- of referring the whole money question to the Commons, who passed their grants on, simply for oonfirma. tion, to the other Chamber. SUFFRAGETTES OF THE OLDEN TIME.

Our suffragettes are erroneously supposed to be a modern phenomenon. The fact is that women have very frequently played an active part in Parliamentary proceedings. Certain abbesses and peeresses were sum- moned to the early Parliaments, though it is doubtful whether they actually sat; and as late as the reign ot Henry VIII., .peeresses were represented by proxy. Women have al- ways made an important part of the picture at Westminster on great public occasions; and they have more than once in 'person brought their grievances vigorously to Par- liamentary notice. Thus, in 1428, the House of Lords was invaded by a number of "stout women of London" to defend the sacred rites of matrimony in the case of the Duchess of Gloucester.

The relations of the two Houses, other than those relating to finance, are too large a sub- ject to be entered upon here; but two events demand notice. The first carries us back to the period of the great Civil War when, al- though the Lords had at first supported the Commons against Charles 1., they afterwards provided many of his stoutest Cavalier sup- porters. The power of the more representa- tive branch of the legisLature was not strong- ly established; and, when it summoned the Peers to give up Strafford, they tamely obeyed, and the tyrannous nobleman -was exe- cuted in 1641. The execution of King Charles ,followed, on February 6th, 1649; and less than a week afterwards the C,onunons agreed to a resolution declaring that "the Houss of peers in Parliament is useless, dangerous, and ought to be abolished," a phrase which las become a commonplace of subsequent con- troversies. Abolished it was, but only for the moment. Indeed, it fell to Cromivell himself to recreate the House of Peers—"the least happy of his 'contrivances," said Lord Macaulay, for it "displeased all parties. The Levellers were angry -with him for creating a privileged class. The multitude, .who felt respect and fondness for the great Ifistorical names of the land, laughed without restraint at a House of Lords in which luck- draymen and shoemakers were seated, to which few of the old nobles were invited, and from which almost all of those old nobles who were invit- ed turned disdainfully away."

AN OLIGARCHY OF GREAT FAMILIES The peerage had undergone' a considerable transformation, and it was to undergo still greater changes. The feudal aristocracy, al- most destroyed during the Wars of the Roses, has scarcely a score of representatives in the present House of Lords. Under the Tudors, the Stuarts, and the first three Georges, a heritage of military force gave plaoa to a heritage of great states. Henry VIII. de- spoiled the Church of its land for the benefit of his nobles; and the Stuarts sold peerages almost in the open market. "I never heard of a peer with an ancient lincarre." sax Lard

FLODDEN FIELD.

The Fourth Centenary of Scotland's

===== 0002601_19130913_art0226.txt | included=true | included =====
Day of Gloom.

TtiE STORY OF A FAMOUS DAY RETOLD.

Just south of the Tweed, in a remote portion of the Borderland, and famous because of the events Of a single memorable day, are the daisy covered slopes. of Flodden Hill. on September 9th, 1513, perished the flower Scotland's nobility, crushed utterly re , f by the English army. 1t was, perhaps, the sad- dest day .that. ever, came to Scotland, and an interest, deep and pathetic, lingers over it still.

shall have all the enemy in the plain before me," he said, "and assay them what they can do." Others pressed the king to•descend the bill at once and destroy them in detail. But he had decided that until Surrey had formed his men and was ready not a blow should. be strucL

This was James' idea of chivalry; modern fighting ,men, we imagine, would call it by a very different name. THE BATTLE. It was the afternoon of September 9th. Earl Surrey had formed his lines and begun to move when it was discovered that Flodden Hill was hidden in a haze of smoke. The Scots had burned their tent, and under the ,cciVer of the smoke were moving down the hill and towards the .neighbouring hill of Branston. The English had been moving in the same direction, with .the intention of occupying it, but as a light autumn breeze carried the smoke away they found, themselves forestalled. The Scotch army was . advancing silently and steadily towards them, and the moment of the shock of war had. come.

The Scotch army was in four divisions. The king himself led in the centre, and the Earls Home, Huntley, Crawford and Montrose c6m- manded elsewhere. _

_ The English force was .in three parts, led by Lord Thomas Howard, Sir Edmund Howard and Sir Marmaduke Constable, the Earl of Surrey being in the centre. Spread wide across the field, it was as though three battles raged at the same, time, and it does not appear that any of the combatants knew how matters progressed elsewhere. The division under Earl Howe opened by a furious attack upon Sir Edmund Howard, and com- pelled it to retreat, but a body of English came to the rescue, and the Scots were driven back. By some means the impression was created that the English were defeated, and .Earl Howe's men quitted their ranks in search of plunder. After pillaging what baggage lay in sight, they moved away towards the Border. Earl Huntley gave chase, but was soon upon the spears of the Scottish left wing, led by Crawford and Montrose. Fierce contlitt began which closed in the defeat of the Scots and the death of both the Scottish earls. At the other extreme the Scots were equally unsuccessful. Bowmen of Lancashire and Che- Shire were tere opposed to the Campbells, the McLeans and McKenzie's, and did terrible execution with their arrows. In great confu- sion and amid 'much slaughter, the Scots gave way, leaving the Earls of Argyle and Lennox I dead tip6ll The ,119_14, - •

_ In the centre it was different: Here the Eng. lish were not able to meet the charges of the , Scots, who rushed down With. fine Courage.end, broke their ranks: James seemed certain ,of great victory, and swould prebtibly have secured it had 'not the tide of battle been unexpectedly turned against him. ' The division under Howard and .Dacre, hav- ing defeated Montrose and Crawford, flung themselves upon the Scottish centre which had A • •- , now to bear the attack of %tit nurnip.. .Tha slanghteii 01 ihelgw., hours that brought night- fall is terrible to enntranplate: In the midst of the welter King James and his then fought like heroes, but courage was unavailing. They were outnumbered and outmatched. As night ap- proached Earl Surrey attacked the King from the rear and managed almost to surround him, But James did not live to know of his complete and terrible defeat. Before nightfall he had perished in the fight. Forming-. a- ring - round his • body, - his men' fought with the courage of desperation, and only the darkness of night brought an end to the bloodshed and strae. The hours of darkness were anxious ones for Earl Surrey., The main body of the Scots was still *unquelled, and 'Earl Home had an unde- feated division around him. They might make a bold fight on the morrow and snatch a vic- tory. But the dawn of day removed his fears. The Scots had gone, leaving behind them Borth- wick's famous guns upon the field.

THE COST OF IT ALL Flodden was won and lost at terrible cost. From eight to ten thousand Scots were killed, and the English lost about half as many. But Flodden op a bitter memory to Scotland, not because of the quantity, but because of the quality of the slain. Not only the king, but thirteen Lords of Parliament, twelve Earls, five elder sOns Of peers, the Archbishop of St: Andrews; and fifty :gentlemen ,of 'high rank lay dead at Sunset upon the -slopes of Flodden, The ScotS,..under .cdver of darkness, stole across the Tweed and over ;the dry marshes td their own country. These fugitives carried the desolating news through Scotland, raising What' Sir Walter Scott describes as "the universal wail."

To quote Sir Walter's passage in full: Their kings, their lords, their mightiest low, They melted from the fields as snow, When streams are swollen and south winds blow, Dissolves in silent dew. Tweed's echoes heard the ceaseless plash, While many a broken band, Disordered, through her currents dash, To gain the Scottish land: To town and tower, to down and dale, To tell red Flodden's dismal tale, And raise the universal wail. Tradition, legend tune and song, Shall many an age that wail prolong; Still from the sire the son shall hear Of the stern strife and carnage drear Of Flodden's fatal field; Where shivered was fair Scotia's spear And broken was her shield.

• The scene- in Edinburgh after the news of Flodden lies beyond all description, and the wail echoes yet in the dirge still used in Scot- land as the. "Dead March,"

The flowers of the forest were a' wede away. James IV., who fell at "red Flodden," was much beloved of his people. He blended strength of body with sweetness of expression, and a high sense of honour and a chivalry that almost reached the level of romance, endeared him to all who knew him.

Strange stories, which might or might not be interesting to our readers, were told concern- ing the fate of his body. It was said to have been carried to Earl Surrey, who conveyed it to 'London, and curiOus happenings are re- corded by the historian Stowe. A stone pillar, called "the King's stone," marks the place where he fell. but whether his body lies beneath it, under the daisies of Flodden Field, we shall probably: never know. AM.

TIIE UNWISDOM OF A KING.

Seldom has a king shown less' of kingly wis- dom than .did James the Fourth of Scotland in the autumn of 1513. Things were strained be- tween him and bluff Henry ,of 'England, and James decided to invade England LO take. ven- geance for the murder- of Sir Hobert Kerr, the warden of the Marches. So say the histOry books. But modern research has shown that there was another reason; the chief one, per-

haps the only one. James was Under the spell of Anne, Queen of France, and had received from her an amorous letter, in which she urged him to make a great stroke towards the furthering of her interests. This stroke was .to be Me conquest of England. The letter appealed to all that was noble and chivalrous in him, and along- with it came a ring from the Queen's finger, and 14,000 FrenCh crowns to meet the cost. The king at once decided to make the great venture. His Lords and Commons besought hirn in vain not to make war upon England. Margaret, his wife, pleaded passionately on behalf of herself and children, but James was under an infatuation, and entreaties fell upon deaf ears.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
SIGNS AND POTENTS.

If tradition speaks truly, other voices joined with theirs in warning and fear. • It is told that as the King knelt at evening prayer in the Chapel Iloyal of Linlithgow, a strange and weird -figure stood before him in the gloom. The man wtts clad as a pilgrim, and bore the pilgrim staff of St. Andrew. Long yellow 'locks flowed over his shoulders,. and he spoke most: solemnly: "Sir' King, my mother :hath sent me to thee desiring thee not to pass at this time where thou art purposed, for if thou dost thou wilt not fare well in thy journey nor any who pass with thee. Further she bade thee meddle with no, woman, nor use their counsel, ,for .if thou' dost thou -shalt be confounded and put to shame." There were those present Who tried to sieze the mysterious visitant, but he vanished, says the chrpiliAcri "as If he. hog Deen.2 'nh of .flie sun or a whiff of the'w'hiAwind." Yet again came a 'potent. When BorThwick was getting ant a -gun a curious .cry, "The Summons of filatcock " was heard at the City Cross, and the, nobility of Scotland wire sum- moned., each by name, to appear in the next dread world. It is said that one of them on hearing his name appealed to God, and broke , the spell, but that all the rest perished on Fled- den Field.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
DIE; CAMPAIGN BRUN.

There must have been in the Scottish mind a strong feeling. against England, for notwith- standing the . deep opposition shown to James' plan, 100,000 fighting men rallied to his banner, and marched from Linlithgow, whence the Queen watched them with tearful eyes. The 22nd of August saw them across the Border, and the campaign liegan. Siege was laid to Norham, Wark, Etal and Ford, in each case successfully, and with the capture of Ford, James.. found .that among his prisoners was the wife of Sir James Heron. .Delighted with the, wit and brilliance of Lady. :HerOn, the king showed no disposition to press his campaign, but remained- there-for some days. of pleasant relaxation. lie paid dearly for his philander- ing,...for 'the -astute..lady. mkanaged,-to communi- cate with the English leaders, and the days of deftly gave them time to prepare and concen- trate. It has been doubted whether time was lost solely upon this account, but that valuable opportunities were lost is quite Certain. The soldiers lost zeal, and of the 100,000 that marched from Linlithgow, only 30,000 were left when the English army came into view.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0230.txt | included=true | included =====
ON TIIE ENGLISH SIDE.

The news of the invasion spread fast. Henry VIII. had left England the previous June, but certain arrangements had been made in view of a possible invasion, and Earl Surrey was ready to make a rapid match to the North. His lighting, force was 25,000 men, but on reaching .A.lnwick many others 'joined him, and in .point •numbers his army 7gnite matched that of King James.,

. . From AlnwiCk the Earl Sept to the King an offer to fight him upon a fair field on September 9th, if he, would remain in England so king. James replied that he was eager for a. battle, and -would be ready upon that day. Choosing a •strong position upon Flodden Hill, the King placed his army there and waited .the Earl's ap- proadi. Surrey realizedithe advantage his enemy had gained' and sent to ask the king to meet him upon the level ground of the plain. The reply was a curt refusal.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0231.txt | included=true | included =====
A BOLD MOVE.

The Ehglish general was upon the horns of a dilemma. He waS short of food and must fight at once or retreat. To retire would be as bad as a defeat, yet to attack the Scotcli was to risk the destruction of his army. With true courage he decided to risk it. More than that, by a bold stroke he de- sided to cross the river 'Till and take up a posi- tion between the king's army and Scotland. It was a desperate 'step and involved_ Me risk of his army being cut to pieces as ;they '-crbSsed the river.

Great excitement prevailed :among the 'Scots as they witnessed the Earl's movement. The captains ran to the King to show how easily they could fall upon the rear and cut the Eng- lish to pieces before they could gather them- selves for a fight. The river had to be crossed slowly by fords (there was .but one small bridge), and in crossing, the army was divided into many many sections. As Bortliwick, the Master gunner, saw the bridge ,crowded with soldiers he fell upon his knees in an entreaty to the king to allow him to destroy it with his long- range guns.

But the'king waS obdurate and answered that he. would hang any man who fired-a, shot. A few cannon balls upon the slender bridge would have inade the English cause hopeless, but fight was determined to ght after the manner of the day. It was to be a hand-to-hand and man-to-man 'encounter. Possibly he , felt that his strong position made deTeat impossible., "J

QUAINT AND AMUSING.

A little Pennsylvania', town, Paxinos.by';name and of 200 population, owned by a brick manu- facturer, is to be sold under the hammer. The town is in a bankrupt condition, and only two properties in it are not owned by the manufac- turer.

USE OF CAMELS IN SOUTH AFRICA.

The Cape Mounted Police have now in use up- wards of 30 _camels. These animals having proved most deful for police work in Gordonia, it is intended to try them in the desert portions of Kiirumam and Wyburg. The additional camels are being ordered from Egypt.

SUGGESTION TO OUR CONDUCTORS

In Stockholm the street car conductors sell hatpin protectors for a cent. apiece to all women who Wish to buy. The implements do not pro- tect the hatpin. They are fastened to the point and prevent a hatpin from doing damage to the person or property of men and women sitting or standing by.

.The telescopei-so far from being, as is gener- ally averred, the outcome of_ the' famous -eperi- ment of 'Galileo, was known at least three hun-. dred.years before his time ; while the microscope certainly dates from the early part of the ninth rentUry. although greatly improved in the six- teenth by Jansen and others.

AN UNSUCCESSFUL INNOVATION.

The introduction of the electric lht into the ballroom at Buckingham Palace has not been altogether a success, as there is a dreadful glare, which is made more tryina by the white walls. The room was formerly lighted by wax candles. the effect of which was very agreeable. except on a summer nieht. when the temperature be- came unpleasantly high.

Motion picture theatres now play a part in thercatching of criminals in Bohemia. When a criminal of importance is "wanted" by the Police authorities of Pracue, and difficulty is ex- perienced in findina him, a PhotoaraPh is sent to every motion picture theatre in the country. and this is shown, with a short a-xplanatory note. until the criminal is found>or the order for the display of the photogrkir is withdrawn.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0243.txt | included=true | included =====
21GZAG LIGHTNING.

-Photography long ago proved that what was once taken -for zigzag -lightning is really sinuous, i.e., without sharp angles. Otto Meiss- nor,-In " Das Wetter,' _ offers an explanation of the angular appearance of lightning. lie be- lieves that the sudden glare of the flash causes an involuntary movement of the head or eye; the original image of the :flash persists for a moment on the retina, along with the image pro- duced on the eye in its new position. Thus we get the impression of a broken line.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0244.txt | included=true | included =====
WHEN THE SILK HAT WAS BANNED.

The New. York 'ladies-driven, to-seek- protec- tion from a crowd who objected to their split skirts might find consolation in the fact that the pioneer Of another unsightly fashion met with a still more unpleasant experience, recalls the "Daily Chronicle.' On Jappary4 15, 1793, when John Hetherington emerged from his haberdashery shop in the Strand wearifig a silk hat he was surrounded by a mob of such pro- portions that he was arrested and charged before the Lord Mayor with inciting to riot.

WHISKY IN BEEF.

A Canadian paper says the introduction of whisky into the forbidden district of Northern Ontario in pieces of beef has been discovered by Chief Rogers, of the Provincial Police, and several prosecutions have been undertaken as the result. It was known that, a woman in the Porcupine district was getting whisky, and after, the authorities had been puzzled for some, time, an examination :of beef going in. was Made, and deep cuts were noticed: • .E.‘"plorkition developed the fact that. THE POPE'S SWISS GUARD.

The Pope's Swiss Guard, which has revolted and been dismissed, dates from the Middle Ages, when no ruler thought, himself ' seCure.'Writhout` a body guard of alien and republican Switzers. To this day it is essential that every member of the Papal Guard should be a native .of Swit- zerland, states the "Daily .Chronicle." Dressed in their wonderful scarlet,; black and yellow uniform (designed' by Michael 'Angelo), aAned with halberds on high occasions and with mus- kets on ordinary days, they are memorable to every visitor to the Vatican. They number about 100, and with the Noble Guard; the Guard of the Palace), the Papal Gendarmerie, and the Vatican Firemen, they make up the little tem- poral artily of which the Pope is Commander- In-Chief.

A DANCING AILMENT.

Doctors in Philadelphia have had to treat so" many women and men for an inflammation of the muscles of the thigh, caused by excessive "turkey trotting," that the disease of "sartori- tis " is being specially recognised. It gets its nene, says the "New York Tribune," from the sartorius, the longest muscle in the body. In society, however, it is better known as the tt turkey leg." The sartorius muscle has invari- ably been found to be so badly strained that the patients have been advised to keep off their feet until the injury had healed. The women were all affected on the right limb,,lire men on the left, because the "turkey trot" is a one-step affair. One doctor says that if turkey trot- ting" does not stop the succeeding generation will have a permanent shortening of the leg.

It is probable that a large proportion of the deaths from typhoid, both in the Sout, African War and .in the_ American War with Spain, were due to the instrumentality of the flies that swarmed about the camps ;• and it, is equally likely that they bear a share in numer- ous lesser outbreaks of typhoid of which the cause is never known. In addition to typhoid it is also believed that flies are active carriers of tuberculosis, ophthalmia, anthrax, cholera, and other terrible maladies, besides the infan- tile diarrhoea, or enteritis which causes so heavy a mortality among the children in our cities every slimmer. •

Literary Gleanings.

MR. HALL CAINE'S NEW NOVEL.

A critic describes Mr. Hall , Caine's new novel, "The Woman Thou Gayest Me," as an extra- ordinary achievement in its way. The story runs to nearly 600 closely-printed -pages, and yet the plot could be told in a few lines: woman married by force to a brutal profligate loves an Arctic explorer, gives birth to a child, and dies of consumption. That is all: yet the story holds one. Again, there is not a single full-length drawing of an actual vivid indivi- duality, yet one accepts the chief people as (a) the brave man, (b) the profligate, (c) the self- sacrificing woman, just as one accepts the types to be found in. "Every Man in His Humour." We have bravery, profligacy, sac- rificial adoration raised to the nth degree, where n stands for the infinitude of bravery, profligacy and adoration. And, of course, bravery is the virtue, par excellence, of man, self-s6crifice of woman, and a sneering profligate the man of Belial personified, no less.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0250.txt | included=true | included =====
THE VALUE OF ONIONS.

Many people imagine that to express a' liking for onions denotes a vulgar taste; but this much-despised vegetable has -many excellent qualities. It contains a large quantity of nitro- genous matter and.uncrystallisahle sugar with a: pungent sulphuric oil.- - If _children were encouraged.-. to ..eat onions many an illness might be prevented, and many a doctor's bill saved. If baby has a cold, or seems croupy, frequent doses of onion syrup will give a wonderful relief. The Syrup is ob- tained by cutting the onion into slices and cov- ering each with brown sugar and putting one on the top of another in a basin. Roasted onions with sweet oil make a good poultice for a cold in the chest.

In a sick-room you cannot have a better dis- infectant than the onion. It has a wonderful capacity for absorbing germs; a dish of sliced onions placed in the sick-room will draw away the disease; they must be removed as soon as they loose their odour and become discoloured, and be:replaCed by fresh ones. For those who can take them, a raw onion eaten just before retiring is very beneficial—it acts as 'a tonic to the nervous system, purifies the blood, helps digestion, and very often prevents insomnia— " Family Doctor."

Some years ago when the capital of the French colony-ofrSenegal'was. a dull unprogresf sive,town where officialism and red tape pre- vailed, a French traveller, with a friend, had a most amusing experience when he• wished to obtain a bath. There was no bathing estab- lishment,.in.the: capital of Senegal at the tirile ; tut rumour had it that it was possible to pur- chase baths at the hospital.

Accordingly the travellers repaired to the hos- pital, where they stated the purpose of their visit.

Yes, here is your case. LUTHER'S HATRED OF FLIES.

It is recorded that Martin Luthee hated flies "because they are the images of the devil and of heretics." The devil in, the doctor's eyes is the spirit of uncleanliness, .and the heretics are they who violate the laws of hygiene and spread dis- ease throughout the world. Houseflies are the most active missionaries of evil.

Two books .on flies have just been published in England. In one Dr. Edward Halford Boss remarks: "Everything seems to have been dis- posed by a provident nature for the germination of disease germs in and on houseflies." In the other C. G. Hewitt writes that all flies normally carry a varied collection of bacterial organisms attached to them, and practically nothing can prevent them from infecting with these micro- organisms fresh matter, which, in one case, is usually food.

Individual, flies have lived in captivity for six- teen weeks. Flies which have been caught and marked and then liberated, have been found, within forty-eight hours a mile from the point of liberation. The distance which flies may travel, therefore, carrying with them whatever foulness they may be possessed of, is almost unlimited. A number of investigators have shown that the germs, with which, as has been said, every fly is probably loaded, remain active for a material length of time; for a longer period when taken into the insect with its food and carried within it until re-ejected than merely attached to it externally. A fly which had fed on the bacillus of tuber- culosis was found still to contain the bacilli after fourteen days. Also the bacilli, having been taken into the fly and Subsequently excreted; remained virulent for fifteen days after ejection.

• That flies are every day similarly collecting germs and subsequently walking not on well- guarded cultures, but on children's bread and

===== 0002601_19130913_art0255.txt | included=true | included =====
SUNDAY IN GERMANY.

Some curious particulars as to Sunday obser- vance in Germany are supplied by the Berlin correspondent of the "Christian World ": When the recently-elected Prussian Diet meets the Government intend to lay on the table of the House a Bill providing for the. better. obser- vance of Sunday. As matters are at _present in Germany Sunday is not a day for religious observance so much asa day for recreation, amusement, sport, and .theatre-going- Take, for instance, the opening of shops on Sunday. At the present time shops may be opened from 8 till 10 on, Sunday mornings, and from 12 till two. They. must be shut from 10 till 12,. as these are the hours of Divine service., They must be shut after two, so that employes may have one afternoon a week for rest. %"It is now proposed to permit Sunday opening only from 8 till.lo a.m., thus lengthening the free time of shop assistants. But there is one class of establishment with which it is not proposed to interfere. All cafes, restaurAnts, beerhouses, &c., may be open all day and all night. You may not buy a pair of boots save -between 8 and 10, but you may buy as much beer as you like, and enjoy your- self within the walls of a public-house even when the church bells are calling you to ser- vice. The new law proposes no interference with the rights of the citizens in this respect. But in one particular there is a change. Hither- to you have been permitted to read a newspaper in the window of a restaurant; in future this is forbidden, provided the window looks out on a street from which the passers-by can see you. Under the coming law, if you want to read your newspaper, you must do so in some part of the establishment invisible from the street!

Another curious change. At the present time you may buy a Sunday paper from 12 till two, not from 8 till 10. But at the railway book- stalls you may buy your paper at any hour on Sunday: To get at the railway bookstall, however, you have usually to pay a penny for a platform ticket. The new law will make it penal to sell newspapers on Sunday by the ordinary .newsagents, but will not restrict the holders of newspaper stalls. With regard to theatres and other places or entertainment, which are all open on Sunday, the new legislation makes no important changes, A TerntArliOlc zcipiation will be.thaf; I concerning the- Veiling-of shO-p-wliThw. LC/

Berlin and other large German cities shops are no longer supplied with shutters, but as many windows are exceedingly attractive it has been determined to ask the owners of shops whose windows are apt to draw a crowd to cover the window with a Cloth, so that its contents can- not be seen from the outside.

TONSORIAL ARTISTS.

Accustomed to the deft dexterity of the up-to- date barber, the Briton who in the cop-- -•

. . n ut tne tverid pLetiti liifh.li tIrAMMTedly In the hands of the tOtiSdrial artiStS iti the variou§ countrleg visited would have Softie Mir- prisind 65456i:faces to relate on his return home. The methods in vogue in many parts of the world are described in the "New York Herald." Nearly all the barbers in Tripoli are Italians, and the shops are as well equipped as those of the Italian cities. The native Arab barbers require only a pair of scissors, a pair of clippers, and razor for their equipment. They erect their temporary shops in the market-places by spreading some matting over a few poles. Arabs have their heads shaved, keeping the hair, short, so that the white skull cap over which the fez is worn will fit closely. There are many barbers in Muscat, Oman, but no barber shops. Wealthy persons are ,at- tended in their own homes, while the poorer classes are shaved in the bazaars. Both cus- tomer and barber sit on a mat facing each other. It is customary to shave the entire head.

In Aden the barbers are Hindoos, who gener- ally call at the homes of thein customers. The European residents have these barbers call every morning at about eight o'clock, and pay about ss. a month for the service, which in- cludes one hair cut.

. In Bombay, India, the workmen, coolies and lower class of servants have their barbering done on the street. Native barbers call at the houses to attend the wealthier classes. In Karachi all the barber work of. the city is done by peripatetic w4:rlimen; .• • . . . -The Harput barber places around his cus- tomer's neck* a. peculiar pan .with ,a . semi= circular piece. cut out of,,oße side, so that it fits 'Partially around the necli under the chin. Water is put into the pan and the barber makes a lather with his hands and rubs it on the cus- tomer's face, usually using his hands for this purpose also.• In Bagdad the well-to-do members of the community have the barbers come to their homes., In Syria. there are barber shops ,of the, European type which cater to the better class' trade, while the cheaper barbers ply their voca- tion in the streets.

In the cheaper shops of Jerusa)em the peas- ants can have their heads shaved and their beards trimmed for from 3d. to 7d. The .shops have only a crude wooden bench and • chair, with a hand glass for the customer‘to hold. In the better shops of Jerusalem armchairs are used. In Zanzibar, a city of about thirty thousand population, there are only about two hundred white men and women, and most of the white men shave themselves, depending Oil the 'arri7 val of a steanier In the harbour to get a. hair cut. There are aifew barber shops roughly fitted Up, which are generally conducted by Indians and patronised by Hindoos and natives. In the native quarters and the bazaar itinerant barbers serve the natives. Such a barber sits in the,shade 61 the overhanging roofs, seizes the head of the customer, who is curled up before him, and gives him a dry shave, soap and water not being used. , Ali the natives keep their heads closely shaved. Chinese barbers formerly travelled about serving their customers at their homes or places of business. Each barber carried his equipment, which consisted of a stool on which his customer was seated, a small wooden tub used as a basin, the old-fashioned triangular, wedge-shaped Chinese razor, and possibly a jar or other vessel for carrying water.

In the interior this equipment is still, used by the barbers who serve • most of China's popula- tion. The chief duty of a barber before the cutting of queues became general was to comb and‘ brush the long hair, plait it again into a queue, and shave the forward parts of the head. Few Chinese have beards, and so shaVing is not generally necessary.

STILL THEY COME.

My Dear ,children,-- Ithas'been my pleasing duty to enrol two new members this week, whose postcards have been received up to Wednesday morning. You see, any who send in after then have to wait till the next week. Why? perhaps you will ask. Well, on Wednesday morning the pt'inter man begins to get his pages ready, and he comes along and says, "Now, then, Uncle Jack,' get your column ready." If you do not have it ready he gets cross and threatens to leave it out altogether, and that would never do, would it? He has to be Obeyed, and So if you do not send in by Wednesday, and do not, therefore, see your matter, just blame the printer. Now about our competition. I want you all to write and tell me how you spent your holi- days, tell me what you saw or did: You can either write it on a postcard or on a piece of piper and put it in an envelope. If you send an envelope seal .it up and put a penny stamp on, for: the. won't take it for a half-penny. I think we should have a good number of com- petitors, and I shall give two prizes. You can post it any time on that day, as long as the postmark shows that, you sent it away on 20th SePtem.ber.i Below I am giving the names of your two new cousins, whom we welcome most heartily. A Bit of Coal.

This is. my life history I am 'writing. I am known as "coal," but thousands of years ago I was a fern. But not a fern like one sees nowadays. Dear me, no! Ferns were ferns then. NVe grew as tall as small trees, our roots stretched down into swampy soil, and Our green fronds waved in the sunshine. really don't know what England is coming to. In my young days it was nearly all cov- ered with forests, in which grew ferns, such as myself, and great trees; and as for the sunshine it was real sunshinebrilliant, hot, glaring. You may be sure we drank in as much as we could of it, smothered as we were, and we throve and flourished. Then, bit by bit, we began to wither. Other trees were pressing in on us, intercepting the light that should have come to us. Strange trees they were, quite different from my fern companions. Gradually we were pushed down into the soft, swampy soil, and other treeS failing on us, pressed us lower, down, down down. Thousands of 'years passed, and it must have been during these thousands of years that we were changed, by the pressure and chemical action of the earth arouncl US, into coal. Then suddenly there was a loud blast, and I awoke and found myself lying with my companions on the floor of a strange place. It was a long, low, narrow passage, dark except where lighted by sunshine in small cages, carried by creatures I have since learnt are men. Then we were shovelled into a truck, which a thing that walked on four feet. called a horse, pulled. We ran slowly along through many other dark pas- sages, and in all we heard " clack ! clack !" and once a loud explosion, like the one that had scattered us out of bed.

At last the truck carrying us stopped, and we Were lifted upwards. As soon as we reached the open air we were pulled along again, and ran up a hill to .what I heard called a' tipper." Here I was separated from my smn.der com- panions, and shot Over into a large truck, to- gether with other big lumps of Then a snorting monster, breathing great white clouds, dragged us, with other trucks, to another strange place—l think I 119.1 r l it called a depot. After lying there for ibme time, we wcre shovelled into greqt sacks, and jolted along, more slowly than bef6re, in a cart. Then we .were Carried on a man's shoulderslinto -little dark room. Coal cellar, did you say.? Here I lie, -awaiting my turn (which is coming very soon) to go and simply men with the warm and light of that brilliant sunshine I absorbed so many thousands of years ago.—Yoursi\BumED SUNSHINE.

The Abstainers' League.

The following inte4esting statement is made in the " Wesleyan Albthodist Church Record." under the heading of Latchford : "The new temperance movement which was endorsed by the conference of 1912, at Liver- pool, has almost completed one year's working and the result is that nearly 100,000 pledges have been enrolled, more than one-half of which are in the Adult League, while the League is at work all-over the country. It has been said at times of political election, what Lancashire does, all the rest of England will do,' and in the canvassing of the Abstainers' League, Lan- cashire has led the way with nearly 20,000 names.

the methods of the League are that all young Methodists between the ages of thirteen and twenty-one are asked to enrol in the Young Abstainer? League and adults in the Adult League, but the special feature of the League is that there will be an annual reaffirmation. In the case of the abstainers who have no real temptation to break the pledge it will simply mean that the Church will come annually and ask for an expression of temperance principles, but in the case of young people and others who, through heredity and other causes fall, to them will come the invitation to re-affirm with no special singling out. This may prove the sal- vation of many. "It, is proposed to hold an annual rally of all members of the League. and hero at Latchford we are to have a special delegate from London to preach the temperance sermons on November 16th ad 17th, Mr. Robert Denholm, who has recentlY been appointed temperance advocate.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 13, 1913.

[ALL RIGHTS RICSIRVEDI FREEDOM AND ITS COST.

Generosity and progress are the unfailing characteristics of free peoples. Despotism, ren when it is good, tends to make its sub- rots suspicious and its way of advance is t 0 wards bigness and big bodies do -not neces- sarily bear the best truits. Despotisms have had for the most part a short life and have left few legacies for mankind. What legacy of Assyria is comparable to that of the Jews? It must be so. Despotisms must rest on force and that force is in the control of one or of a few rulers. Free peoples are generous. Free Britain has given asylum to exiles like Mazaini and Kossuth. It has, greatly daring, been the friend of nations struggling to be free; it has spent vast treasures in emancipating African slaves, and it has given eelf-govern- ment to its colonies. Britain has been gener- ous and it has also 152 en progressive. Through the ages it has grown more careful to do jus- tice for the weak and oppressed; more consid- erate for the health and the happiness of all its members; more tender to the children, more humane with criminals.. Every Briton has his rights, his person is inviolate, his house is his castle, he is free to speak his mind and to join in any association. And as years go on rights more and more are seen to imply duties and the people who govern themselves become more and more concerned to use their rights so that their neighbours may be neither ignorant, nor poor nor un- happy. Fredom has done much for Britain and Wordsworth could' well boast "that vir- tuous liberty bath been the scope of his pure song."

e FREEDOM AND PROGRESS, Generosity and progress are ti character- istics of a free people. It must be so. Free peoples have by effort and by struggle won their position, the strength by which they stand is in their own heads and bodies, they are not dependent on any great man's proteo. tion, they are what they are because they themselves have striven, and endured. Their 'Sallies are exultations, agonies and love, and man's unconquerable mind. They have a consciousness of strength which is their own and yet is also part of the eternal force of the universe. They feel strong, tnerefore they laugh at suspicions and they can. do noble and generous things. It . is the timid—the self-distrustful—the servile who see dangez in every stranger. William Penn because he felt in himself the strength of Al- mightiness met the savages with an open hand and achieved a victory unknown to those who fearing treachery met them with 'weapons. Freedom whicii breeds strength makes a people generous. Free peoples are also the progressive peoples of the world. Every fresh thought of every mind ()ones to drive them onward. Despotism and °lige arches have only the service of one or of a few minds, they don't like, as the Governor of Genoa said when he ' imprisoned Mazzini "Young people thinking without knowing the subject of their thoughts." Despotisms deprive themselves of new forces which are always pressing to drive mankind to more hu- mane ways; free peoples have open access to such forces. The spiritual, source of progress which like an encompassing sea surrounds mankind finds throuph every man and wo- man an inlet by which to enter and refresh society with higher thoughts and higher ideas. If men and women are not free tiose inlets are blocked and the society of which they are members loses the greatest driving force. New thoughts and new ideas may he condemned as heresies—as follies—as tre ons; many of them may often be mixed with vanity, but the nations which admit their free play are the progressive nations and it is the nations who repress them who decline and fall. Nations must be free if they are to progress to. greatness—not ju3t the greatness which consist,-,1 in just laws and upright poi..ey, but also in a noble literature and lofty pleasures, in the. acknowledged supremacy. of peace and zoodwill.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0285.txt | included=true | included =====
A. PASSION FOR LIBERTY.

Freedom which is thus valuable is only PG/. sible at great cost. Freedom for nations is practically identical with self-government. ft is the condition in which every citizen has some share in making the laws and is able to look forward to the highest position among his fellows, the condition' in which every citi- zen has the power of doing and enjoying what is worth doing and enjoying in common with others. This self-gOvernment is always won by great cost. English history indeed is just the tale of how the people have thrown off the restraint of foreigners, of kings and of nobles. They would not have laws imposed by Rome and the renaissance spirit took form in England in a very passion for liberty. The marks of that passion are seen alike in the great Charter and in the great buildings which survive to this day. The Norman churches with their constraining-imprisoning arches were hateful and as at Westminster splendid structures were destroyed to make way for others whose aspiring arches and far- reaching vaults better expressed the prevail- ing feeling of hope and freedom. The people were set on being free and the struggle has gone on through the ages underlying the wars, the conspiracies and the policies or disfigure history. There have been, .which -action, times like those of Charles lltgilmo.,reisfwyolifenrethe people seemed content to sit down and play while others governed, but always deep down there has been an on. ward movement, and it is the delight of his- torians to trace its evidence. At times it has broken out in revolutions, in riots and in re- form bills, but for the most part it has gone silently forward and Britain is now probabry the nation which is most advanced in self_ government. The fact to be remembered is the cost at which this freedom has been won, and women have suffered and struggled, they bore reproach and shame, they were con- demned as traitors and they still persisted, Freedom is hard to win and it is equally hard to hold. The condition on which it can be kept is one of eternal vigilance, and the effort by which it has been gained must be persistent. The people of to-day have inher- ited a freedom won at great cost, they can re- tain it only at the same cost. They have self-goyern.ment, they -have the power of mak- ing the nation great, they have within their reach all the privileges which belong to such a possession, but how easily may the govern- ment fall into other hands if they cease to watch, to strive and to work. Temptations are many. The world is so full of things, why should everyone not be as happy as kings, why should there be this trouble of continual thought about public affairs ? why need a man leave his home and take the trouble of voting? why should he mix up in politics and soil his hands by taking part in elections? why trouble to serve on boards and councils when efficient aciministratare are so capable? SIGNS OF THE TIMES.

•It is in many ways as hard in th3se modern days to retain the power of self-government as it was in old days to win the power. We are not indeed required to risk our lives or endure fines in order to pro- tect our persons from a strong man's will, but sacrifices which involve denial of pleas- inone mwoatK a more ure—which interfere with business and irri- t than tate or vex our pride require sometimes, even greater effort. aneedgainst watchfult°thieves, there is more danger to free- domagainst in offers of relief than ,in threats of 1,5. be: T straint. People of to-day unless they 'are on the watch so easily accept relief from the ef- fort of self-government and so easily' fall Into habits which make, it irksome to consider the duties of self-government. The old forms re- linain, laws are made and executed in the peo, pie's name, but they have no thought about them and do not think of other laws. They a/ re losing thei- freedom and the results of the ioss are to be traced in signs of the times. People have not the old consciousness of strength, they are suspicious, anxious about spies and unable to sleep in their beds for fear of foreigners. There is not the old energy to increase the reign of justice. There are shouts indeed for reform, but love of pleasure blocks the way of the spirit and shouts end in inaction. The loss of freedom means loss to the commonwealth, but' the greater loss is to the individuals who miss in their ,own lives the joy and strength which Continued in next column
