Thurrli Ptitts.

St. Paul's Vicar and the Schools.

The Rev. W. Bracecamp, vicar of St. Paul's, writing in his magazine, makes the following reference to the Church Schools: "We must make a desperate effort to save our day schools from being handed over to the Local Authority. In order to do this, we ought to raise £l,OOO, to be invested, in the names of trustees. The interest, as long as the schools remain the pro- perty of the Church, must be used for struc- tural repairs, &c., and if ever the schools are taken from us, to be used for Church purposes in the parish. I hope all our readers, who have been educated at Heathside, will take the matter up keenly, and help in every possible way to raise the amount required. I will call a meet- ing shortly, which I hope will be well attended, and the work of raising the money enthusias- tically entered upon."

===== 0002601_19130913_art0288.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe P.S.A

The Rev. T. T. James addressed Wycliffe P.S.A. on Sunday on "The spirit of gratitude." By counting their many blessings, he said, and naming them one by one, they called forth grati- tude from their thankful spirits. Prayer ought not to be bad or useless. By praying according to the sincere needs of the soul, they did got answers to prayer. God delighted in listening to prayers. They often prayed when deprived of friends or had suffered a great loss. They then felt their needs and prayed. Praise ought to be uppermost in their thoughts. Mr. W. Bes- wick presided. Miss Carrie Hastings sang "How lovely are Thy dwellings" (Liddle), and this was greatly appreciated. Mr. Yearsley and Mr. Healey also took part in the meeting. The brethren sent their congratulations to Mr. E. Wynne Jones on his acceptance of the call to Earlestown Baptist Church as lay pastor. They also passed a vote of sympathy with Mr. A. Hays in the prolonged illness of his wife. Mr. Payton presided at the organ, and two new members Were heartily welcomed.

Brick Street Independent Methodist

Church.

----- -- The 20th anniversary- sermons of the above church were held on Sunday, when Mr. C. Thornton, Ashton-under-Lyne, was the preac)ier morning and evening. At the morning service he. gave an address to the scholars and teachers. Miss Bradbury also rendered a solo very sweetly. At the evening service Mr. Thornton preached an excellent sermon on lessons to be derived from the Transfiguration. Mrs. Thorn- ton sang the solo. The choir rendered the two anthems, "o how amiable" and "The radiant morn" very finely, and were heard to very great advantage. In the afternoon the cantata "God's Garden City" (A. Pearson) was given by the choir and scholars. This proved• to be a very melodious and attractive work. The choir rendered their parts splendidly. the solo parts being very well interpreted by Miss Eva Shaw, Miss Ethel Lightfoot and Miss Florrie Barton, sopranos; Mrs. William Barton, jun., and Miss Elsie Bar- ton, contraltos; Messrs. Henry Lightfoot and A. Morris, tenors; and Mr. W. Barton, jun., bari- tone. Mr. A. Trow accompanied on the piano with skill and discretion, and Mr. W Tomkin- son presided at the organ. Mrs. James Dodds, of Penketh, was the chairman, and gave an interesting address to the scholars and friends. The collections realised about 1:8.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0290.txt | included=true | included =====
WYCLIFFE 40th ANNIVERSARY. Interesting Sermon by the Rev. T. T. James.

"If God be for us, who can be against us?" This was the text from which the _Rev. T. T. James, the pastor of Wycliffe Congregational Claire'', preached an eloquent and striking ser- mon at the celebration of the 40th anniversary of the dedication of this church, which has done so much for the spiritual welfare of Warring- ton.

Anniversaries, he said, are milestones on the wayside, causing us to east our minds back over the road we have come. To many, he added, that place of prayer was indeed a treasure-house of sacred memories.

It is surely, lie added, with a sense of pro- found gratitude we must realise how this Church has stood here through all these years, in the midst of a community growing in numbers, in- creasing in its commercial and industrial acti- vities, developing ever more widely in its municipal and civic life; how the members of this Church have been privileged to witness, by the grace of God, to the permanent and un- changing power of the Gospel of Jesus Christ, and to the fact that through all the changes of .social life and oiv,anisation that. Gospel is still competent to meet the needs of humanity; how, amidst the stress and toil of life, in face of the overpowering sense of material interests, and. the incessant pressure of the visible and pre- sent, this Church has stood as the perpetual sign that man cannot live by bread alone, and that beyond the seen and the temporal lie the invisible and the eternal. During this time religions have come and gone; mushroom growths, lasting for the moment Organisa- Aions have been born, have had their day of life, and passed into oblivion. Movements which were to make a new world in twelve months have flitted across the stage and are already in the museum of history. And not by any merit that we can claim, but by the grace of God, the Church of Christ remains with her task as clear as ever, and we in our time, her sons and daughters, are called to serve her well.

Not Abject Apology. In this hour of thankful celebration we turn to that motto we have made our Own, the great word of Paul in this Bth chapter of the Epistle to the Romans: "If God be for us, who can be against us?" I need hardly say to anyone who has read this chapter carefully—and who has not?—that it contains a wonderful philosophy. But before I go into the whole Meaning that is involved in these words I would persuade you first into the spirit of them. There is an atmos- phere about this ringing cry of Paul which is itself bracing and invigorating. There is the note, not of abject apology for the Christian faith, but of defiant confidence in the face of danger. You do not want me to spend time in painting over again for you the conditions of the early Church, and yet it may not be com- pletely unnecessary. • There is a kind of feeling abroad that in our own day the Church of Christ is faced with un- exampled difficulties, that she is called to main- tain her place under circumstances of a more adverse nature than have ever existed before in history. Without minimising the difficulties presented by the age in which we live, it may safely be asserted that our burden is not heavier than that which belonged to the members of the primitive Church. If to-day we feel sometimes that the Church stands in a world largely alien to the Christian spirit, remember that nothing could have been more diametrically opposed than early Christianity and the paganism in the midst of which it sought a footing. No men ever had a harder problem than those who first sought to live out the Christian faith in a paga7, world.

lam more than a little tired for Our apologies for Christianity. Ido not stand in this pulpit to apologise for my Lord: I stand here to de- elare Him. I do not stand here primarily to defend the, Gospel: I stand here to announce it. I confess to being weary of the people in debat- ing societies who amiably wonder and argue whether any longer they can hold the Christian faith. You cannot. You cannot hold it. No man ever could. If once you discover its secret IT WILL HOLD YOU, it will grip your soul, it will earry you out of yourself into a new world, and von will breathe the atmosphere of that early Church which viewed its enterprise as a desperate adventure with terrific and sleepless ions on one side and a changplecs and undefeat- able God on the other. "If God he for us, who Pan be against us?"

FREEDOM AND AND ITS COST.—Cont.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0291.txt | included=true | included =====
come from civic courage and political In- tegrity. • Freedom it must be remembered is not just emancipation from restraint. It is not even the possession of rights except in so far as the rights imply the performance of duties. The rights of man lie not in his ancestry which is from the brutes but in hit possibilities which are divine. The free man is the fully devel- oped man and citizen able. himself to contri- bute the best to the commonwealth and set on making every other citizen fit to make a like contribution.

Freedom in this sense can only be retained at great cost. Each citizen must for himself, as we have seen, make sacrifices to take his own part in government--irksome, distaste- ful, distracting as it may be, but he must also exert himself both to induce his neigh- bour to do the same and also help to make his neighbour fit for such a duty. The burdnn of a. citizen in a free people is very heavy, the cost of freedom is great but in its cost may be said to be its value. Because it brings out energy and courage, because it demands Fe!f- denial for the public good, because it requires constant effort, ,because it keeps a man's eye on his neighbour's goad, therefore freedom is worth its cost. It has in itself the promise of a nation which being strong and • generous will advance to a greatness beyond that dreamt of in any empire of the past.

THE CYCLIST'S NOTE BOOK

BY "SILVER-STREET."

Knutsford and Wilderspool Roads.

A CONTRAST.

The thanks of all motorists and cyclists who use our south-eastern road are due to the Road Surveyor for the admirable way in which he achieves the very difficult task of keeping the constantly sinking surface in order. The proximity of the river is probably the cause of the frequent displacement of the road material, but the way it has been attended to of late years is all we could ask. Why Wilderspool- road should be comparatively neglected one cannot think, but it certainly is, especially on the western side, where for hundreds of yards the setts are hopelessly out of repair. But one thing that strikes every road-user is the desperate condition of the wood pavement in front of St. James' Church. It must be seven years at least since the last time those wooden setts were properly laid, and now the time has surely come when "every valley shall be ex- alted, the mountains and hills laid low, and the rough places plain." Is not the serene quietude of the interior dearly bought, when it is at the expense of so much railing accusation outside? For the ser- vice only lasts an hour or two, whilst the other goes on all the 24 hours.

CHESHIRE REST HOUSES.-IX.

At Castle Mill, Ashley. I know niree good houses for refreshment in the neighbourhood of Castle Mill, and will deal .with Mrs. Overend's first. It is the last, farm on the right-hand side before commencing the plunge down to the River 13011 in: . When Ciie present Earl of Tatton changed liouses at Westminster and Sticceeded_ hiS brother at KnUtSford„lie took the Strange course of forbidding all the tenants on ,the Tatton estate to cater for visitors.' -It had long been prohibited at Rostherne by the 'late Earl, but now the ban was extended far and wide over the whole Egerton domain. For years only one person defied the order; Mrs. Overend was obliged to obey; no one 'shall:drag that other name or place from me, for does not the " Examiner " circulate all over Cheshire? At present, however, the heel seems to have been raised somewhat, and, many farm ladies have

resumed their kindnesSes to us roadsters; but. gentle reader, can you imagine that you are living in free England, and is it the 20th cen- tury, when the caprice, to use a polite term, of one man, can deprive thousands of us 'of our country haunts and woodland pleasures? All is well that ends well, and those who stay tea at Overend's farm, Castle Mill, have a rare evening's treat.. Walk down over the Wooden Bridge, and back along the river valley to the old Mill, or if you have a longer time, climb the other bank, and walk past Yew Tree House and Broad Oak Pam'', returning through Castle Wood and over the old bridge. .If it is Sep- tember or October you will find hundreds of "red coats," the scarlet berries from the "lords and ladies," the English arum lily, which abounds in that wood. Castle Mill is a beauty spot that should be known to every Warring- ton cyclist.

RIDES IN THE LAKE COUNTRY.

Our third ride closed at Coniston. The fourth; of 38 mites, will be a surprise, even for seagoned tourist. Passing the Waterhead Hotel and rounding the north of the Lake, the six-mile ride down the east side of Coniston Water is a revelation of Lakeland beauty. The road passes the back of Brantwood, where John Ruskin lived, and then descends to the very edge of the Lake. It runs through a pine wood *right to Nibthwaite, and is Al in surface, gradient and .beauty. Down the Crake Valley to .Greenodd, Ulverston and Conishead Priory. Bardsea, Baycliff and Aldingham are all on the coast, and'then'the .course is inland to Gleaston. Leece, loose and Furness Abbey; a few ti.n- teresting miles to reach a gem of architecture. The road then lies through Dalton and Ireleth, to Broughton-in-Furness, ,a pretty town, N.vhdre the stay maybe made for the evening. _ - _ _

The fifth and last ride for the week Is to Con- iston, nine miles, and over to Colwith, Bridge. The road up Langdale must be traversed twice, for there is no outlet beyond Dungeon Gill. At Elterwater the hilt must be faced To cross Loughrigg Fell, and I wish every one may have such a vie?,v of Langdale, from the top as I had the first time I went up there.- Coming down to Grasmere is the famous lied Bank, and then, from the town, the nigh road to Windermere Station completes 32 miles. To reach our starting-point, Grange, is another 15. Lamp time to-night 7.51. Next Saturday 7.33.

RIFLE SHOOTING.

APPLgTON v. GAIISWOOD HALL.

COLLIER'S LUCKY ESCAPE.

WITH THE MUM

Nearing the Season's End. WORKS BOWLING.

The bowling season is fast drawing to a close, and a sort of half and half interest now exists amongst local trundlers. On Saturday a number of games were decided in the annual handicap in connection with the Warrington Works Association, which promises to be a greater success than ever. In the Individual Merit Competition, pro- moted by the Warrington and District Associa- tion,.A Scott and J. Ashton met to fight their battle over again. History repeated itself, for Scott won in grand style by 21-12. A number of games were played in the first round and the second and third rounds in the Preliminary competition were decided. Everyone was surprised to see J. Jolley go down to W. Bridge (Cemetery) by 18-21. Jolley is a past champion, and is a member of the Bull's Head team. The bowling main in connection with St. Austin's Ward (Conservatives) was won by H. Forster, who beat H. Bingham in the final by 13-12. Details from the fourth round were i Fourth Round.

Hayes, F. W. Hitch- mough. Hughes and Deakin, W. Derbyshire, J. Llewellyn, S. Griffithi, W. UnsWorth,, Edwin Bradshaw, J. Heywood, I. Greenwood, Eustance and Co., _Harry Rogers, and J. Murray; Mrs. Turner, Mrs. Walker and Mrs. Campbell.

On Saturday Crosfield's team met• Gossage's in the return friendly match at the Mersey Hotel, Widnes. After an interesting match Crosfield's were declared winners by 44 points, or a win on the home and home matches by 167. After the match Gossage's entertained the vic- tors to tea.

By "Scrum."

A DTKENSON DAY,

ponderous chap since fie has gone to Leigh, idund his old clubmate too much for him. Fearnley and Cox perspired and worked like Trojans. Green played well in the loose. It was largely a half-back's day, and on this occasion the Warrington naives covered them- selves with honour. Ruddick worked the scrums admirably, and Mere was ,not a better tackler on the field. - Nicholas combined speed with judgment.

If any man shone -More particularly;- frdm. amidst a clevex lot, it was George Dickenson. It was his day out, and his play amply justified the remarks our special contributor ancrmy- self made in Saturday's "Examiner." Every- body must be glad that George is showing such form, and when his benefit comes there ought to be a packed ground. Bradshaw was respon- sible for a lot of good work,: and Davis and Brookes were very capable in all departments. There was one thing Which struck me, and it was, not only is there a perfect understanding between the Warrington threequarters, but there is not a man who displays the least desire to .indulge in .selfishness. This absence of a desire to do things on their own, is a splendid qualification, and I sincerely trust this spirit will be long maintained. If so, I have no fear but that the Warrington quartette will come to rank as amongst the best in the N.U. League. "Jolley- I hope to see doing big things at full-back," I wrote in our last issue. Jolley did them, and I have no fear that he will do some\greater things still during the season. Not only is he artful and resourceful, but he is calm, and that is a valuable asset for the last defender to possess.

THE HUDDERSFIELD HOem Why Was Davis Sent Of f

We had a few mysteries to unravel after the Huddersfield match on Monday, mysteries of the uncertain type which make the glorious pastime of N.U. football all the more interest- ing. One was why did Warrington meet with such a heavy defeat, and were Huddersfield. 40 points to 3 superior? Another was why did the referee send Davis off the field in .the second half? A third was why was the wing three- quarters of . the Whrrington team s 6 persist- ently starved? There were other questions which the Warrington spectators who .attendeil at Fhrtown can, ask for themselves, but-I think the tame above are of the most importance. . In Seeking a scihition of the first, Why did Warrington meet with such a heavy defeat, I must candidly admit that our lads were in all respects. 'eXcept 'One, -inferiof to theft °lino- nents. Those who travelled to Yorkshire in the hope of seeing a repetition of the Cup final were badly disappointed, and scarcely anything else was to be expected. Huddersfield, without doubt, are a stronger. side than any we .have heard of yet. They are trained to the pink, but whether they can keen up at that Pitch for the next month or two is -a moot question. ' I have seen many a team in the same finished condition at the opening of the season, and six, or eight weeks later have noted them wrecks. Huddersfield will lie mar- vels if they escape the hand of fate In that respect. Now Warrington have not been trained to the same pitch, and that was mainly the " innard secret" of their defeat-.

Warrington Shall Have 'Revenge. To revert to a boxing simile, it was the case, of the highly-trained Jack Johnson and the less trained Jefferies over again, and training alone won. If Warrington was defeated so decisively we need not don sackcloth and dust ourselves with ashes over it. Every match Warrington ntav will imnrove them, whereas there is more than a snorting chance that Huddersfield will deteriorate; highly trained players cannot keen un to ton pitch. and when the teams meet at Warrington early in January we may expect a different result. The chance will then come for sweet revenge. As to the. point. Were linddersfield 40 points to 3 sunerior to Warrington? this raises a flues- tirm about whinh there can he two or more oninions. according to the nnsition of view. A linddersfield man might. say they ,were decid- odiv while a Warrington croaker might he inclinedlo say ditto, hut I nersonallv bold the cilinion that. Warrington Were not inferior to . the extent the score indicates. Wa'crington had ouite their shar f t.hr rTTIP. In ffiet. they Vvere more freauently in home territory than Huddersfield were in the War- rinvfon half of -the nitch. It was only through snasmhdic. though hrilliant, dashes that Hud- dersfield did manage to score. There was no doubt that the plums did not fall folhe hardest working side, which was Warrington.

Jump Not To Conclusions. It would be a mistake to read from the score that Warrington all through were hopelessly hemmed in, for they were not. Out of the 70 minutes, Huddersfield were not more than 15 in the visitors' half of the ground, but when they came it was death. Their tries were clever without doubt. In the forward line .they did not do anything which the Warrington for- wards didn't do. At half-back the homesters were quicker, but they had a fault which should have been penalised a score of times,, had the referee been more observant. Time after time the home halves never put the ball into the scrum at all. It was an unfair trick, but it paid, for before the Warrington men could get out, the ball was speeding on to the goal. At threequarters there was no Comparison. Warrington were simply out of the running. Davis and Dickenson were too slow in centre, when up against a quartette like that of Hud- dersfield, and no matter how successful they were against Leigh, they were not in the class of the liUdderstleld cracks. The consequence, and this brings me to the third question, was that Bradshaw and •nrookes were starved. Barely did they get the 15all,and On one of these rare occasions Bradshaw scored Warrington's try.

Jolley In Crack Form.

- _ It was a mistaken policy, when up against such •sprinters as the Huddersfield threequar- ters, for the Warrington centre men, when they got the ball, to run with it into the ruck, instead of parting with it to the wing men with the utmost speed. No one could bore his way through the Huddersfield defence, and the only way to get down was as near the line as pos- sible. ..This fact Warrington realised when it was too late. - _

Lurk was against our lads as well. Two penalty kirks were given, one struck the up- right and the other the cross-bars and rebounded

Jolley several times was cheered for his bril- liant work, and when a Huddersfield crowd, one of the most biased in the country, cheers an opponent, he must ,be showing exceptional merit. he pivot men amongst the threequarters given im the assistance he deserved—well, I do not .hink Huddersfield would have made 40 points.

The Mystery of Davis.

Why Davis was ordered off in the second half is a mystery, and the secret appears to be known only to the referee, but I hope the matter will be thrashed out at the next League meeting. To all interests it would appear as if Davis is the victim of a mistake. The inci- dent arose thus. Gleeson ran for a fry, with Davis at his heels.

At the, line Gleeson touched down and Davis rolled Over hini,and fell a distance of two yards away. When Davis got up he was ordered off, and Gleeson afterwards stated that he was sur- prised, as Db.vis had done nothing to him. I-lad the referee confused, quite accidentally, of course, Davis with Chesters, whom be cau-

tioned earlier in the game? Otherwise the whole thing is inexplicable. The -incident happened iit the popular end of the -ground and had Davis-done anything. the least, hit shady against, the idol of the enclosure —Gleeson-L-there would- have been a huge out- cry by the Huddersfield partisans. Not -a voice was. raised; in fact, the spectators did not seem to realise Davis had been ordered from the game. It will be very interesting to see the referee's report upon the matter.

THE DAVIS MYSTERY. Warrington to Fight the Matter.

The sending off by the referee of Davis at Huddersfield is not to be allowed to go un- challenged,

At a meeting of the Warrington Committee on Wednesday the Secretary was instructed to call as witnesses the linesman who was at the side of the field where Davis was alleged to have committed the infringement. Gleeson, the Huddersfield threequarter, is also to be called on behalf of Davis, to whom the committee feel a great injustice has been done.

THE "A" TEAM WIN AT LEIGH.

Baker Makes a 'Favourable Impression.

It is satisfactory to be able tct record the fact hat the " A " team also were able to record an They

riitial victory at the expense of Leigh.

had a stiff order on hand in this Lancashire Combination match, but won by two goals two tries (10 points) to one try (three points). Warrington scarcely got into proper stride until nearly half-time, and by then Leigh had put on a goalless try. A bad infringement by the home side gave Warrington a penalty, which led to a goal; and epds were changed with Leigh leading by one Point. There was some brilliant work by Warring- ton in the second period. The forwards were .active, the backs keen to utilise chances, and the threequarters put in many dashing runs. From two of these Baker and Daintith scored, but Critchley only succeeded in converting one of the' tries.

Baker, :the new local lad, played a most promising game. He has football in him, 4nd, with a little cultivation, will yet do great things, while he was one of the speediest men on the field. Daintita seems also in fine form.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0315.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MATCH AT BATLEY.

Quite IL different tale should be told after the match at Batley to-day, than. that I had to record about the visit to Huddersfield. Batley are not of the same calibre as the Huddersfield team, and if Warrington play like they did against Leigh, there should .be no, doubt about the result,

The committee at the ,meeting on Wednesday detided to make no change in the side, and I think they have actecl wisely. The more the Men play together the better will the side- be- come, and I think the team which is going to Batley is quite able to hold its own, and a little bit more, at Mount Pleasant. The teams will line out in the following order

WARRINGTON. Jolley. Bradshaw, Dickenson, Davis, Brookes. Nicholas, Ruddick. Thomas, Cheaters, Fearnley, Cox, Clare, Green

Kitson, Judge, Hodgson, Hilt, Fenton, Debney. Brooksby, Smith. Ward, Parker, Laughlin, Tindall. Lyons. BATLEY.

The team travel by the 10.40 a.m. train from Bank Quay, and are due in Batley soon after one o'clock. The •home committee report that they expect a large "gate," as Warrington is a popular side in that part of Yorkshire.

THE "A" TEAM AT HOME.

The "A" team play al Wilderspool, and have as their opponents Leigh Shamrocks. A good game should ensue, with a victory for the home side. The Warrington team, which will be similar to that which defeated Leigh away, will be:—

J. Brown, back; Waywell, Davies, Jordan and Baker, threequarter backs; Dainteth and Brown, half-backs; Naylor, Harmer, Bradshaw, Brown, Boardman and Critchley, forwards. The referee will be Mr- Cross.

RYLASIDS' RECS. :WIN.

The match between Rylands' Recs. and Ditton was played on the Gorsey-lane enclosure, before a fair number of spectators, and Rylands' Won handsomely. Ditton had their-full League team out and a good following. Rylands, who were short of Bramhalf and Horrocks, winning the toss, elected to play with the wind in their favour. Ditton :kicked off. After give-and-take play in the centre of the field, Rylands' tacked through Carter, Griffiths -.being held a yard inside the line. Erratic passing on the part of Bylands' right centre lost many opportunities: From a pass by Warren to Gallop, then to Carter, on to Griffiths, who passefi back to Carter, Rylands' scored.

Carter, who played a splendid game, com- pletely bluffed the opposition and scored, for Potter to kick the goal. Griffiths scored a fine try, after a nice passing bout. Ditton next took a hand, as from a pass from Warren to Griffiths, O'Gara intercepted, and rac-

ingk along the touch scored under the posts. From bad play on the " Wiremen's " part, Dit- ton scored again, half-time arriving with the score 11 points to 8 in favour of Bylands'. flylands' kicked off in the second half, and quickly took measure of their opponents, and commanded the game, tries being scored by Griffiths,Cartoi. and Ifouocksi

After' this the game becaine‘a bit rough. but one of the Ditton players having been given niarching Orders, the game ended in favour of the " Wiremen" by 1 goal 6 tries (20 points), to Ditton; 1 goal 2 tries (8 points). Carter-was in fine form, along with Griffiths, Wari.e'n and Stretford.

RYLANDS' RESERVE N. NVARRINGTON WHITE ROSE.

The second string of the Ijylancis' Recreation Club have an interesting match to-day. They meet he Warrington White :Rose in the St, Helens and District Junior League at Bewsey- lane. The Rylands' team will be:— F. Potter, back; V. Bleasedale, J. F. Matson, P. Morris and T. Farrell, threequarter backs; T. Riley and T. Waiting, half-backs ; C. guck- ler, G. Ogden, W. I,Velsby; S. Cooper, R. Bibby and Jackson, forwards.,

DASHES FOR. THE GOAL-LINE. Jolley was in a jolly humour. The others felt quite as happy. In fact, they were all "Sunny Jims.". Dickenson, the veteran, 'did the trick. There is plenty of life yet in the old dog. ilis try was the best of the match. Gallop didn't gallop as lie thought. Davis was a sore thorn in the way. Gallop, however, did not like it at all. He had to put up with it and say .nowt. The Leigh captain is now a weighty chap. What was it that happened to Chesters? Was he really kicked Or did he sham? The trainer was soon off the patch. " Everybody was happy rata ChesterS escaped. Clare crawled in a lovely try, didn't he. It was the only way for him to get in. To score with two men on ones' back is Not quite so easy as one might think. So Clare crept cautiously over the line. Why was there a fight at half-time? What was there to light about, at all? The players took the game most sportsman-

like:. Why should two spectators, then, indulge in

• a fight? Cain was the most able man on Leigh's side. Still Ruddick was ready for him every time. George delighted everybody by his fine try. A few more Similar displays will mean That the ground will not hold all those \\ill° will want to attend his benefit match. Where was Warrington's much-promised

combination? Only, once was a,tiny wee bit displayed. The .committee felt very happy after the

match. Most of them lit up big fat cigars In order to celebrate the splendid victory.

Phew! but it was terribly hot. Truth, and it was. Huddersfield 40, Warrington 3. We could hardly believe our eyes. Still it is no good crying over lost points or spilt milk. Let us preach revenge, and get it when Hud- dersfield come to Wilderspool. ' It was not a repetition of the Cup final was Monday's Warrington never did themselves justice until the last few minutes of the game.

• Jolley was, however. too -" rosy " for Rosen- feld, that he was. The ex-Australian never got such an upset- ting before in his career. What did Howard Davis do when the referee sent him off? . .

It was one of those little' mysteries which no one could understand. Gleeson, the man alleged to have been struck, said Davis didn't touch him.

Nobody .was more surprised than Gleeson when Davis was ordered to depart. The general impression amongst the crowd was that Davis had got hurt and retired. Perhaps the. referee thought Davis was the Warrington -man he had previously cautioned. Altogether It was an unfortunate and Un- happy incident in the game. Where were the centre threequarters—where, oh, tell us where? - Why didn't .they feed Bradshaw and Brookes a " leetle " bit more? Bradshaw only got one proper Chance and he scored a try. The Huddersfield forwards .are so heavy that the Warrington men bounced off them. The Huddersfrld forwards are reported to weigh together over a ton. What could our slim built chaps do against such a deadweight?

Jolley was the-smartest back on the field; Hol- land came second.

'Ruddick, on Monday, was very fitful; bril- liant at times, indifferent at others.

Nicholas was baffled by the way Huddersfield put the ball into the scrums. Bather, we should say, by the way the ball was not put into, the scrums. . They infringed the rules continuously. Why were they not penalised? Half the Hudilersfleld spectators are members of the "gentle sex." -- How they screamed when their own " blioys" scored a try.

It. was na very good bit of work, was not it? Warrington `A..' Leigh "A"• are a stiff 'lot, at home, and the Warrington. Win was. creditable. - The reserve men proved themselves a sturdy lot, with plenty of spice and dash. They scrummaged well, they passed all right, and there was some good running. Baker justified all the earlier expectations formed of his football abilities.

The young local lad showed that he can run and that he knows the art of swerving. It was a happy beginning that he should be one-of two to score a try; it was a good 'un.. Dainteth justified the forecast about him. He is in good form.

!Viands' N.U. team have got to work in splendid style. They defeated Ditton on Saturday by 20 points to eight. . "Not'. a bad beginning, is it?" quoth the de- lighted captain of the team. Gallop was the means of starting quite a "galloping " run, and from it Rylands' scored. Hurrah, Horrocks, that was in truth a really splendid try.

'Carter catered to the crowd with one almost as good, don't you think? The " Wiremen" seem out to do big things this season.

Warren and Srretforcl appear to be coming men. They played .well. Carter wns In the .best- of form, and the rest sensibly fed him.. Why-did Ditton begin to play rough when the score was Mounting up? , Was it because' they were "cut up" in more ways than one? , . ,

One of their men got the order to march, and the lesson was effective.

The WARRINGTON & DISTRICT LEAGUE MATCHES.

By " Linesman.”

Amongst our local matches on Saturday the most important was at Folly-lane, where Gros- field's entertained Lostock Gralam, in the North Cheshire League, the Grakunites being last season's champions. The game was not so one-sided as the score (4-0 against Crosfield's) may indicate. The visitors roved themselves the better marksmen, and made the mosti of their chances.

In the Warrington and District League there was .only one match down for decision in the First Division, most of the clubs preferring trial matches before entering on the League tourney. The match between. The Divex and Weston Point was only a moderate affair. The lads from Runcorn won: the match by two clear goals, and this should encourage them on for their engagement against Great Sankey, whilst the Divex were handicapped by playing a man short in the 'first "45," and they ivere, also minus several of their best players, who are away on their holidays.

In the Second Division Orford St. John's had a comfortable • match in hand with Fairfield United, winning by seven goals to nil. St. Barnabas' and Emmanuel, at Lovely-lane, was a very even game, and the Barnabas' lads

is( managed to win by two gonTs to one

Warrington Excelsior met Bank Quay Bees., and a good match 'resulted in a win for the Excelsior by four goals to one.

his charge from further downfall, and half-time arrived with the visitors leading by one goal to nil.

At the interval the visiting captain allowed _ _

the-Divex to make their team Tup, and Archibald went in goal and Heys centre-forward. This re- arrangement proved a good thing for the home team, and play was more even and keener than in the first .half. Good .efforts .to get thrOugh were made. by Lightfoot. and Heys, while at the Other end the visitors ought' to have added to their score, but lost their Chances.. Halse once had an open goal, when he pushed the goal- keeper instead .of shooting. S. Leach was the scorer for the visitors again, after some good defensive play; by Pearson and,. Flute, while Nanc,ollis .was very lucky to:save a splendid attempt by Heys just: before the finish. Result: Weston Point, two goals; Divex, none.

NORTH CHESHIRE LEAGUE (Ist Division).

Crostield's v. Lostock Gralam.

Crostield's opened their season's campaign in the North Cheshire League by meeting last seasom's champions—Lostock Gralarn—at Folly-

lane. The home team won the toss, and Coun- cillor Peacock kicked off. The Lostock forwards came away smartly, only to be pulled up for offside when near the goal. This relieved the home defence, and the forwards carried the ball into the Lostock quarters, only to be

leaten by their backs. Morris had a busy time,

Dunne placing wide when in .a good position. The home team, led by. Hawthorn and Foster, made some good attempts to get through, but failed before the visiting backs. Tindall fouled in the penalty area, from which Dunne scored, and at half-time the visitors were leading by one goal to nil. On ,resuming, the home team played With grim determination, and Hawthorn, Foster and Whitfield showed good combination. Weedall scored a second point for the visitors, Morris ,being at fault, ,t • this period 'Dunne was playing a fine game .for the visitors, and Walsh, at back, was kicking well 'under pressure. ,Weedali got. thrOugh again for Lostock, and Costello added a fourth just before call of time. The .Visitors won by four goals to nil..

LATCHFORD F.C.

In the world of Association football in ant. around Warrington the..tratchford Chib can claim to be..the senior -organisation in the dis- trict, having now been in existence for upwards of 20 years. Like all other clubs for sport, it has had its good times and its bad ones, but the committees of different periods of the e!ub.s life have always been successful in keeping their flag flying, in spite of the adversities they have had to contend with. The present genera- tion are only able to trace its doings back a few years, and discuss the events of more recent. times. ' Since the breaking up of, the Warrington Football Association, a few years ago the club has allied itself to the Altrutcham and District Association, now better Known as the North Cheshire League, and durieg their membership :there has , be,en ,some exciting struggles witnessed on the Thetwall-lane ground for both cup and League nours. The club's enclosure has also been honour,Nl ty the Cheshire County Association, when, some two seasons ago, they allotted a semi-final tie for the Cheshire Amateur Cup between clubs repre- senting Sandbach and Lostock Graham.

The last two or three seasons have been very trying periods for the committee of the Latchford Club, when they struck out in an enterprising venture by inaugurating a Works Competition, which proved to be a success, both from a players! point of view and .financially as •well, but through some unforeseen. difficul- ties at the commencement of last season it was very doubtful as to whether they would con- tinue or not.. When it came to the last hurdle a few of the loyalist members of the club had .'consultation ..together, and the necessary, funds were forthcoming, and the club got on its way again. Although they did not. win ' either of , the first two places in the League, they finished up the season in a very satisfactory position, and they also got into. the Semi-final for the Cup, but were defeated by Barnt9n, who ultimately wan the final tie. . - For the. season just commenced the coin- rnittee have b.een working hard, and have been put to sonic heavy eXpenSes in the way of re- placing the canvas they had alongside the top end ,of the field by erecting a corrugated fence in its, place. The club managers are. expect- ing the public of the district to rally round the club and give them better support than .for- merly. The committee have got together a very useful lot of promising players for the season's campaign, and the players signed on include: Robinson, Grace, Downs, Webb, Carter, Smith and Taylor, of last year's team; along with Lees and Fogg, late of , Barnton ; Challinor, of Naylor's Works, team; Stubbs, T. Illidge of Latchford Recs. ; Siddall, Tweedle, and Rite, of Oughtrington, as•goalkeepers. The members cif the, committee are:. F. Walden, R. Tinder, R. Fairhurst, T. Green, J. Gleave, Maskell and R. Taylor; honorary secretary, Mr. T. Powell, Latchford-street, Latchford.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT LEAGUE. (2nd Division.) Orford St. John's v. Fairfield United.

Orford St. John's opened their season's League fixtures at Long-lane with a match against- their near neighbours, Fairfield United, the following being the teams as lined out by Mr. A. Holt (referee):—Orford St. John's— Forbes.; L. Atherton and J. Smith; T. Caldwell; Tickle, Smith, Meachin, Slidell and Hankinson. Fairfield United—Bibby; Rowlands and Dooley; Dolphin, HaMblett and Burns ; Gleave, Chal- •linor, Bebbington, Short and Appleton. The United won the toss and Meachin started the ball rolling. The " John's "at once began to' attack the visitors' goal, and, finding their backs weak, Meachin opened the score, while the same player added another goal from a penalty kick for foul charging. The Fairfield lads now took up the running. Atherton handled in the penalty area and Bebbington missed the net. Meachin scored again, and half-time arrived with Orford St. John's lead- ing by three goals to nil. On resuming, the United lads made some good attempts to break down the Orford defence, but Atherton, Forbes and Smith were equal to the demand and they, kept their lines intact, and, owing to the pace being kept up and the summer-like conditions under which the game was being played, the Fairfield lads fell away in their attack, and the home team brought pres- sure to bear on their goal. During the last 15 minutes Meachin.sc,ored once and Sudell twice, and when time was called Orford St. John's were- easy winners by seyen goals .to nil.

Warrington Excelsior v. Bank Quay Recs.

This mach was. played on the ground of the Excelsior and was the opening fixture for both club §in the League. The _following players took part -,—Excelsior-J-J. Rigby; E. Crozier and D. Waltham ;T. O'Neil, R. .Spencer and A. Hunt; H. Walker, W. Leach, A. Williams, H. Leach and G. H. Cox. Bank Quay Recs.—J. Warburton; E. Berry and J. Eccles; B. Butler, E. Thomas and A. Booth; H. EccleSton, J: Davies, D. _Bradshal,v, J. Smith and 3. Banner. Referee—J. T. Hadkiss.

This game was exciting and keenly con- tested, both teams showing good combination. The. Excelsior proved themselves to be the best marksmen and goals were scored by A. Hunt, T. O'Neil, J. Rigby and W. Leach, while A. Bradshaw scored for the Recs. Rigby's goal was the result of a Penalty, the Recs. missing one. R. Spencer played a very good game for the Excelsior at centre-half. Result: Excelsior, four goals; Bank, Quay Bees., one goal.

St. Barnabas' v. Emmanuel.

The St. Barnabas' team entertained the Emmanuel team in the opening match of their League fixtures at Lovely-lane, before a fair number of supporters. Play ruled very even, and the game was keenly fought. Both teams gave a good exposition of the code, and victory rested with the Barnabas' team by two goals to one goal. Shuttleworth scored both goals'for St. Barnabas', and Hughes for pnmanuel. Great Sankey v. Padgate United.

Great Sankey opened their season's fixtures with a friendly match against Padgate United, on the Penketh Becreation Ground, before a good number of their supporters. From the kick-off 'Sankey, with the wind at their backs, made an attack on the visitors' goal, and good shots by Bennett and , Darlington gave the visitors' custodian some trouble. The Padgate players. did not show up very well against the home defence, and Sankey again took the ball to the other 'end, and the visitors' backs con- ceded a corner, from which Bennett scored. At half-time Sankey were leading by two goals to nil.. .*

In the second portion the home team had matters more their own way and added three more goals to. their score, ,while S. Hardman scared with 4..g00d 'shot for the visitors, the game ending ::Sankey, five,goals ; Padgate, one.

ORFORD ST. JOHN'S.

In the Orford St. John's' Football Club, who have this season joined the. Warrington and District League (2nd division), weshave a club who for .the four years of their existence have always occupied a prominent position in War- rington junior football, having been successful in gaining honours in the competitions they have taken part in. Last season they were Members of the Warrington and District Alliance ,(an unaffiliated competition), of which they gained the championship, playing 32 matches,, of which they won 31 and drew one, and headed the League table with 63 points. They were also semi-finalists in a medal competition, and were defeated by Earlestown Athletic on the ground adjoining the Kink and Queen, Padgate-lane. Like other junior clubs who have a very suc- cessful run, their players are Sought alter by other clubs of higher rank, and their playing , strength has been weakened by the migration of Horrooks, caldwell, Isherwood and A.

"THE EXAMINER" OFFICE,

BEWSEY STREET.

A capital and inspiring sign of Congregational progress in the town is to be seen in Elmwood- avenue, where, at a cost of X 2,520, a new Con- gregational school-chapel has been erected to replace the altogether inadequate and unsuitable Cockhedge Mission. On February 13th Mrs. Ziba Armitage laid the foundation-stone of the new building, which was formally opened by .Mr. Ziba Armitage, in the presence of a large assembly on Thursday afternoon. - The building, which will accommodate 350 People, comprises a church hall 56ft. by 361 t., a guild room 26ft. by 16ft., six large class-rooms, minister's vestry, kitchen, and ample sanitary accommodation. It has been planned 'so that the hall and each of the class-rooms can be used separately, being approached by large, wide and well-lighted 'corridors, thus obviating using the large hall as a thoroughfare, a condition which was almost unavoidable in the old type of plan when the hall was surrounded by class-robras. The site is a corner one, and sufficient land has been acquired to provide for the building of a church at some future date. The hall is lofty, and the ceiling broken with large gablets. The Renaissance type of architecture has been used for the building, and the boldly projective blocked cornices and gables add to the beauty of the building. The inside joiners' work has been carried -out in pitch and stained varnished wood. l'he roof principals are exposed and ample provision has been; made for ..ventilating the hail and various class-rooms and cirtidors by Means of 'inlet and -extract ventihrors. fi he building is heated 'by :hot water the low pressure principle 'by ,means ; ines from .a. furnace boiler • placed in' the basement. "The whole 'has been carried, out from the designs and under 'the supervision Of 'Mr. S. P. Silcock, F.R.1.8.A., and Mr. Joseph Dolan has had the building work in hand.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0347.txt | included=true | included =====
THE 'OPENINt tEREMONY.

The Rev. T. T. James presided at the -opening ceremony, and there were present Mr. •Ziba Arniitage, the Revs. John Yonge, William Reid and James Cregan (chairman of Lancashire Congregational Union), Messrs. E. S. Garland, W. Beswick, J. Ball, C. Eccles and B. Farrar (deacons), Mr. and Mrs. William Bennett, Alderman Bennett, Messrs. W. H. Payton, A.

Perris, T. J. Clayton, T. London, W. Knowles, J. Darby-shire, W. H. Wallington, J. W. Slack (treasurer of the building fund) and .3. Atkin- son. _ _

6.74.0 •..• • The Rev. John Ycrnge offered prayer, and Mr. Silcock I:oresented the gold key. to Mr. Armitage, and asked him to open the door. Mr. ARMITAGE, in opening the door, said: "I have great pleasure in using the key. Words of mine are not n9cessary to give reality to this ceremonial act of 'unlockingN the door of this new. sanctuary; and very few will serve to record the gratitude and thankfulness with which our hearts are charged, that at length, after long years of desire and expectation, and many fluctuations of hope and despondency, this House has been raised to the glory of God. From this day forward the Gospel of Jesus Christ our Saviour will be here proclaimed, and prayer and praise will be offered to our gracious Lord. As I open this door, let us all pray that the Divine favour may rest upon the work of His servants, and peace and prosperity attend the ministry of the Word. In the name of Jesus Christ I declare Elthwood-avenue Chapel to be dedicated to the worship and service of God." THE FIRST SERVICE.

Afterwards a service was conducted in the new building, the Rev. James Cregan being the preacher. The organist was 'Mr. R. Wallington. At the outset, Mr. CREGAN said he esteemed it a great privilege to preach the first sermon in that church. As chairman of the Congrega- tional Union he thought he was justified- in bringing the hearty congratulations and warm- est Christian regards of all the leaders of Lan- cashire Congregationalism.

Preaching from the text, "This is my com- mandment, that ye love one another, even as I have loved you," he said that the true mark of a man's divinity was his power to love. The power to think lifted him above the animals, but the power to love raised him in the sight of God. It was the love in Jesus Christ that: made Him the subject of adoration of all the ages. They had often heard great arguments 1n favour of Christ's divinity in His incarnation and miracles, but too often had people forgotten to look for the great argument for His divinity in His love. •

The greatest gift God could bestow on the world was that of a man who loved the world in a redeeming, saving feeling, and helpful love. A great thinker might be a great blessing to the world, but a great thought without love could neither redeem nor pave. A strong power lift- controlled by love might blight, blast and damn, and the possessor of that power might find him- self on a St. Helena of the ocean. Love was the great pre-eminent thing in a man's life; it was a friend of the friendless, and comforter of the comfortless. Love went everywhere; into the hovels and into palaces. Love was the birthright of every man on earth, and the hum- blest who possessed it was greater than the loveless king, sage, or philosopher. Love made Martin Luther a great man, and it also made Florence Nightingale greater than Napoleon. It made the happy possessor of it in the hovel greater in the sight of God than a loveless queen or princess. . WHAT IS LOVE?

No one could tell what was love. We could tell what it was like, of the burflens it bore, of the tears it shed, and what it brought' with it: We -knew it was like God, Jesus, and the Holy Spirit. - No floods could drown it, no flames. consume it, and no weapons destroy it. Love was Heaven, and Heaven was love. Jesus knew how much we needed love when He came to save the land. He was anxious that men should follow the powers of His love, and that they should go forward with it It had been spread all through the Christian centuries, from village to village, town to town, city to city, country to country, and kingdom to kingdom. He hoped that in that new church they woqld go on ' spreading this great love. It had been dedicated to the/service of God that the love of Christ might go on and on. They were begin- tang a new church, and they should think about the way in which Christ revealed His love, and

For the holidays, DRESS BASKETS, TRUNKS, SIT Cases, &c.—J. S. GARLAND, 51, 53 and 55, Bridge- street, Warrington.—Advt.

•_-,_,.._

try to follow that way. Christ's love had three or four characteristics. His was the love for everyone, an appreciative love. We nowadays were particular what we loved. The people we loved must be respectably intelligent, well dressed, well off, people who could love in re- turn. No; we did not mind paying a contri- bution when it was Missionary Sunday, but , don't ask us to love those thick-lipped negroes. We say the Salvation Army is all right; they go down in the slums, but don't ask us to go i down there. Jesus loved all people. Those who were social outcasts, and alters who re- sponded to the appeal. Some people did not mind building a new church, paying for preach- ers, and join in singing to people; in fact, any- thing at all. They would send religious hooks to people, along with well-marked Bibles, but they could not love properly without going down to it. They must love the people with an appreciative love. They looked at people and misunderstood them, because they were pre- judiced. After referring to St. Peter, the speaker said if he had been in our day he would have been a " star " preacher, and probably have received a call to New York. Imagine Peter going to the door of a modern theological college and meeting a modern Principal. Love that did not awaken and inspire was not true love. There were congregations- in the North of England who did not awaken and inspire what was best in the minister. There was not a decent Sun- day morning congregation in that part of the country, and yet they expected the: minister to preach his best, and that everything should be of the highest class. The people were to blame, -because a minister could . not preach to empty seats—to dead wood. God loved the people with an enduring and sacrificial love, for He gave His only Son, and love always wanted to give. It was not satisfied with writing affectionate epistles with crosses at the end. True love was always wanted to give, and they should consider what they were giving to God, to Jesus, and the IChurch. They wanted to, concentrate it by having hearts renewed, refreshed and warmed in that church.

===== 0002601_19130913_art0348.txt | included=true | included =====
A "JOY" MEETING.

Tea was provided in the school, and after- wards a large congregation assembled at a

Photograph by Burrell.

public meeting, over which the Rev. T. T. James presided. The speakers were the Revs. J. Cregan, A. J. Vizier (secretary •of the Lanca- shire Congregational Union), John Yonge and W. Reid. Mr. J. W. Slack also gave a financial statement.

The CHAIRMAN said that day was one of rejoic- ing and congratulation. He felt certain that all their friends, especially those who had for a long time attended the Cockhedge Mission, were to-day feeling what a happy thing it was to have a new home. They were not going to call the old building any names, as it had many happy and sacred memories for many of them. He prayed that in time to come that building would be a great centre of work, and that it would more than justify the willing efforts that were put forward to attain it. They were not a new Church in any sense, for they had a long and glorious history, and many noble traditions and honoured names connected with that his- tory.

They were pleased to have the Rev. John Yonge with them, and he wished to express, through Mr. Yonge's representative capacity, their thanks to the members of Wycliffe Church for all they had done for.the Cockhedge Mission. (Hear, hear, and applause.) The-Rev. JOHN YONGE said he came- with un- mixed feelings of delight. They were looking that evening at the fruits of the Cockhedge people's hopes, desires and labours for many long years. He hoped they would enjoy that building, which was a comely and comfortable one. He. hoped that 4siy would be the beginning not only of a new, but of a very successful movement. (Hear, hear.) He wondered • what Samuel Whitley would think if he mild look in upon them. He would be highly pleased and gratified to see the realisation of their hopes. He (Mr. Yonge) trusted that they would be able to gather in a number of people who 'would- be regular attenders at worship. He hoped they would get a mita fitted for the pulpit, but they must not be in .a hurry; they must wait for the real and intended Man to come to them, (Ap- plause.) • - • • • The Rev: JAMES CREGAN gave an eloquent and inspiring address on "The essentials for a suc- cessful Church." What kind of a Church was a successful one? Some people said a success- ful Church was one that was always crowded. It might be crowded every Sunday, and in the sight of God be far from successful. A Church might have plenty of money, and in the sight of Heaven not be successful. There might be an eloquent preacher, and a magnificent choir, and yet it would not be a successful Church. MEANING OF A SUCCESSFUL CHURCH A successful Church was that in which men and women were blessed and saved; where they were trained for the service of the Kingdom of Jesus Christ. He hoped that Church would , succeed along the' best lines. "(Hear, hear.) They wanted a living relationship with the liv- ing, Heavenly Church of Christ. The second essential was unity. • Destitute of unity, a church could not be successful. "Behold 'how blessed a thing it is for brethren to dwell to- gether in unity." If they were to make any impression on the district and communities they must have unity. The members of the Church must be united in doing something.. In a great many places there were far too many unemployed people in the churches. The 'work was left to -so many, and the other's did- the . . grumbling and doing everything in their pawer to hinder the earnest few who were-bearing the burden' of the church in the heat of the day. Even the little child could help in the church and endeavour to make it successful. Then they must have real brotherhood amongst the, members. Our churches were spoken against from time to time by certain people, because of the absence of brotherliness amongst them. He was not going to praise the churches too ranch, but he would say that -with all their faults they were the most brotherly institutions in the world. He urged the mem- bers of that church to create the feeling and spirit of brotherhood more and more. Nothing was so beautiful as a band of people united together in a true brotherhood. (Hear, hear.) They should also evangelise their own district. They should get all classes in and teach them in the right-way. The blackguard-looking men were despised by some, but the church was lost

, . OBSERVATIONS MADE AND RESULTS. - The inspector visited' Warrington on August 12th, 14th and 15th, and September Ist, and made half-hour observations ot the chimneys of the following firms, whose names, together with dr 'lowing table :
